< 0.01

~0.99

<
0.01

Under the standard pull convention,
no nuisance parameter
exceeds
2\sigma
. The largest pull is norm_wjets_vbf at

-1.89\sigma
, followed by shape_jes at
-1.41\sigma
and
norm_qcd_vbf at
-1.27\sigma
. Under the constraint convention
(dividing by the postfit uncertainty), norm_wjets_vbf (
-2.15
) and
shape_jes (
-2.02
) would nominally exceed
2\sigma
, but this
inflation is expected for well-constrained parameters and is not the
standard presentation.

•

norm_wjets_vbf (
-1.89\sigma
):
The
W
+jets
normalization in the VBF category is pulled down to compensate for the
observed 31% data deficit. This is expected given the data/MC
mismatch in the VBF category and the role of
W
+jets as a
significant background there (approximately 29% of the VBF MC
prediction).

•

shape_jes (
-1.41\sigma
):
The JES shape systematic is
absorbed by the fit to adjust the predicted distribution shape. The
JES shift redistributes events between bins and categories, and a
negative pull shifts jet energies downward, reducing the predicted VBF
yield to better match the observed deficit.

•

norm_qcd_vbf (
-1.27\sigma
):
The QCD multijet
normalization in the VBF category is pulled down, consistent with the
overall VBF deficit requiring reduction of all background predictions.

•

norm_wjets_baseline (
+1.24\sigma
, zero impact on

\mu
):
This NP is pulled by
+1.24\sigma
yet has exactly zero
symmetric impact on
\mu
. The zero impact arises because the

W
+jets normalization in the Baseline category is nearly fully
degenerate with the overall background normalization: changing the

W
+jets yield can be perfectly compensated by adjustments to other
normalization NPs (particularly
Z\to\tau\tau
and QCD Baseline),
leaving
\mu
unchanged. The non-zero pull reflects the fit
absorbing the data-MC deficit through this parameter, but the
parameter’s effect on
\mu
is nullified by the correlation
structure of the simultaneous fit.

These pulls are physically motivated and not alarming for an open data
analysis with known MC limitations. The theory NPs are essentially
unconstrained (post-fit uncertainty approximately 0.99), as expected for
normalization-type systematics that are poorly constrained by this
dataset.

The TES nuisance parameter is constrained to a post-fit uncertainty of
0.18 (18% of its prior width), which is unusually tight for a
single-channel measurement. On Asimov data, the TES constraint was
already 0.26, and the full data further tightens it. This strong
constraint arises because the TES directly shifts the tau

p_{\mathrm{T}}
, which propagates to all three observables
(
m_{\mathrm{vis}}
,
m_{\mathrm{col}}
, NN score) through their
dependence on the reconstructed tau energy. The 40-bin combined fit (20
bins per category) provides substantial statistical power to constrain
this shape parameter. Published CMS analyses report similar TES
constraints in the
\mu\tau_{\mathrm{h}}
channel, though typically less
tight due to the use of per-decay-mode TES variations rather than the
inclusive 3% used here. The MES (post-fit uncertainty 0.32) and MET
unclustered (0.30) parameters also show significant constraint,
reflecting the sensitivity of the NN score distribution to energy scale
variations.

Nuisance parameter best-fit values and post-fit uncertainties from the NN score full data fit. The x-axis shows (\textbackslashhat\lx@text@lbrace\textbackslashtheta\lx@text@rbrace - \textbackslashtheta\_0) / \textbackslashsigma\_\textbackslashmathrm\lx@text@lbraceprefit\lx@text@rbrace (standard convention). The black points show the best-fit values with error bars indicating the post-fit uncertainties. No parameter exceeds 2\textbackslashsigma under the standard convention (the largest is norm\_wjets\_vbf at -1.89\textbackslashsigma). The shape systematics (TES, MES, JES, MET unclustered) show significant constraint from the data (post-fit uncertainties well below 1.0).

Figure 46:
Nuisance parameter best-fit values and post-fit uncertainties
from the NN score full data fit. The x-axis shows

(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})/\sigma_{\mathrm{prefit}}
(standard
convention). The black points show the best-fit values with error bars
indicating the post-fit uncertainties. No parameter exceeds
2\sigma

under the standard convention (the largest is norm_wjets_vbf at

-1.89\sigma
). The shape systematics (TES, MES, JES, MET unclustered)
show significant constraint from the data (post-fit uncertainties well
below 1.0).

The three shape systematics (MET unclustered, JES, MES) dominate the
uncertainty budget, contributing a combined impact of approximately

\sqrt{0.285^{2}+0.214^{2}+0.148^{2}}\approx 0.39
to
\Delta\mu
.
This is consistent with the expected sensitivity being limited by energy
scale calibration uncertainties in this tau final state. No single
systematic exceeds 80% of the total uncertainty: the MET unclustered
impact of 0.285 is approximately 26% of the total

\sigma(\mu)=1.08
, well below the 80% regression trigger
threshold.

Comparing the Asimov (Table
24
) and full-data
(Table
45
) impact rankings reveals dramatic
reshuffling. The most notable change is TES, which drops from rank 4
(impact 0.214) on Asimov data to rank 15 (impact 0.025) on full data —
a factor 8.5 reduction. This is driven by the strong data constraint on
TES (post-fit uncertainty 0.18). MES similarly drops from rank 1 (0.404)
to rank 3 (0.148). In contrast, MET unclustered rises from rank 2 to
rank 1, and JES from rank 3 to rank 2, because these parameters are less
constrained by the data (post-fit uncertainties of 0.30 and 0.70,
respectively). The reshuffling demonstrates that the Asimov impact
ranking, while useful for planning, is not a reliable predictor of the
observed systematic hierarchy — the data constraints reshape the
uncertainty budget significantly.

The total systematic uncertainty, computed as the quadrature sum of all
impacts, is approximately 0.39. The statistical uncertainty dominates:
the total
\sigma(\mu)=1.08
yields a statistical-only uncertainty
of approximately
\sqrt{1.08^{2}-0.39^{2}}\approx 1.01
, confirming
that this measurement remains statistically limited.

.12.8
Goodness of fit (full data)

A dedicated GoF investigation was performed using both Pearson

\chi^{2}
and the Poisson log-likelihood ratio (LLR, the proper
saturated-model GoF test statistic:

T=2\sum_{i}[n_{i}\ln(n_{i}/\hat{\nu}_{i})-n_{i}+\hat{\nu}_{i}]
) with
200 toy pseudo-experiments per approach. The NN score toy failure rate
was reduced from 14.8% (original run) to 1.0% using retry logic with
perturbed starting values.

The GoF
p
-value of 0.000 is confirmed genuine and not an artifact of
toy failure bias (the original NN score run had 14.8% toy failures;
reducing this to 1.0% does not change the
p
-value). The
per-category
\chi^{2}
/bin values are individually acceptable
(0.49-1.19), but the combined test statistic exceeds all 198 converged
toys (NN score: observed 33.1 vs toy range [10.2, 28.6], mean 17.8).
This indicates that the probability of both categories fluctuating high
simultaneously is small, and the model does not perfectly capture the
inter-category correlation structure.

The GoF failure is attributed to the known 5% overall data/MC
normalization deficit and 31% VBF deficit, which cause residual shape
mismodeling that the nuisance parameters cannot fully absorb. The GoF

p=0.000
means the profile likelihood uncertainty may not have
nominal frequentist coverage; the quoted
\sigma(\mu)=1.08
should
be interpreted with this caveat. The per-category results (Baseline:

0.61\pm 1.60
, VBF:
-0.04\pm 1.34
) provide a robustness check,
as the Baseline-only result is consistent with the combined result and
the Baseline category has acceptable per-category GoF (
\chi^{2}
/bin =
0.86). Despite the GoF failure, the signal strength measurement is
robust: the per-category fits yield consistent results (Section
.12.9
), the NP pulls are physically motivated, and
the result is consistent with the SM.

48
, Figure
49
, and
Figure

.

.12.10
Post-fit data/MC comparisons

The post-fit data/MC comparisons show the quality of the model
description after the profile likelihood fit has adjusted the nuisance
parameters. These figures demonstrate that the fit successfully absorbs
the pre-fit normalization discrepancies.

\subsubsubsection

NN score post-fit
distributions

Figure 51:
(a) Post-fit data/MC comparison for the NN score distribution in
the Baseline category. After the profile likelihood fit, the predicted
yields are adjusted through the nuisance parameter pulls, improving the
data/MC agreement. The ratio panel shows the post-fit data/prediction
ratio, which is closer to unity than the pre-fit comparison in Figure
41
. (b) Post-fit data/MC comparison for the NN score distribution in
the VBF category. The fit absorbs the 31% pre-fit deficit through the
normalization nuisance parameters (
W
+jets VBF pulled to

-1.89\sigma
, QCD VBF pulled to
-1.27\sigma
in the standard
convention), producing improved but not perfect data/MC agreement. The
residual shape differences reflect the limitations of using
normalization-only adjustments for a potentially shape-dependent
effect.

\subsubsubsection

Visible mass post-fit
distributions

Figure 52:
(a) Post-fit data/MC comparison for the visible mass distribution
in the Baseline category. The large negative signal strength
(
\hat{\mu}=-6.70
) pulls the signal contribution negative,
effectively adding events to improve the data/MC agreement. This
demonstrates why the
m_{\mathrm{vis}}
approach is vulnerable to
normalization mismodeling. (b) Post-fit data/MC comparison for the visible mass distribution
in the VBF category. The fit adjustment is substantial, reflecting the
combination of the 31% data deficit and the limited discriminating
power of the visible mass in the VBF
category.

\subsubsubsection

Collinear mass post-fit
distributions

Figure 53:
(a) Post-fit data/MC comparison for the collinear mass distribution
in the Baseline category. Similar to the
m_{\mathrm{vis}}
approach,
the large negative
\hat{\mu}
compensates for the normalization
deficit. The broader collinear mass distribution smears any localized
features. (b) Post-fit data/MC comparison for the collinear mass distribution
in the VBF category. The fit adjustment is the most extreme among the
three approaches, consistent with the largest negative signal strength
(
\hat{\mu}=-10.74
). The high unphysical solution fraction in this
category further dilutes the discriminating
power.

.12.11
Comparison to published CMS
result

The published CMS result (
[
CMS:2014nkk
]
) measured
\mu=0.78\pm 0.27
combining all tau-pair final
states (
e\mu
,
e\tau_{\mathrm{h}}
,
\mu\tau_{\mathrm{h}}
,

\tau_{\mathrm{h}}\tau_{\mathrm{h}}
), both center-of-mass energies (7 and
8 TeV), and the full luminosity of 24.6 fb
-1
. Our result of

\mu=0.63\pm 1.08
is consistent with this published value: the
difference of
0.63-0.78=-0.15
is well within the uncertainties
of both measurements. The pull between the two measurements is

(\mu_{\mathrm{this}}-\mu_{\mathrm{CMS}})/\sqrt{\sigma_{\mathrm{this}}^{2}+\sigma_{\mathrm{CMS}}^{2}}=-0.15/\sqrt{1.08^{2}+0.27^{2}}=-0.13\sigma
,
confirming excellent consistency.

The ratio of uncertainties is
1.08/0.27=4.0
, which is consistent
with the reduced scope of this single-channel analysis:

•

Single channel:
The
\mu\tau_{\mathrm{h}}
channel provides
approximately 23% of the total
H\to\tau\tau
sensitivity.

•

Single run period:
11.5 fb
-1
vs the combined 24.6
fb
-1
.

•

No SVfit mass:
The published analysis uses the SVfit
algorithm, which provides 15-20% better mass resolution.

•

Looser tau ID:
The Loose working point retains more
background than the Medium/Tight used in the published analysis.

•

Simplified systematics:
No pileup reweighting, no generator
comparison systematics.

Figure
54
presents the flagship
comparison of the primary NN score result with the published CMS
measurement, while Figure
55
shows all
three approaches alongside the published result. The NN score approach
is the only one that yields a positive signal strength, consistent with
the published value and the SM prediction. The
m_{\mathrm{vis}}
and

m_{\mathrm{col}}
approaches are visually incompatible with the SM and
published values, further underscoring the importance of multivariate
discrimination for this measurement.

Comparison of the primary NN score signal strength measurement (\textbackslashmu = 0.63 \textbackslashpm 1.08) with the published CMS combined result (\textbackslashmu = 0.78 \textbackslashpm 0.27, JHEP 05 (2014) 104) and the Standard Model prediction (\textbackslashmu = 1). The pull between the two measurements is -0.13\textbackslashsigma, demonstrating excellent consistency despite the 4-fold larger uncertainty of this single-channel, single-run-period analysis. This figure constitutes the core physics deliverable of the measurement, quantifying what this Open Data analysis adds to the existing body of H\textbackslashto\textbackslashtau\textbackslashtau knowledge.

and
m_{\mathrm{col}}

signal from this dataset.

.13
Conclusions

This analysis presents a measurement of the Higgs boson signal strength
modifier
\mu
in the
H\to\tau\tau
decay channel, performed in the

\mu\tau_{\mathrm{h}}
final state using 11.5 fb
-1
of CMS Open
Data at
\sqrt{s}=8
TeV. Three fitting approaches are compared
using a simultaneous binned profile likelihood fit across Baseline and
VBF event categories.

The primary result, using the neural network discriminant on the full
dataset, is:

\mu(H\to\tau\tau)=0.63\pm 1.08

corresponding to an observed significance of 0.61
\sigma
and an
observed 95% CL upper limit of
\mu<2.85
. The result is consistent
with the Standard Model prediction (
\mu=1
) at the
0.34\sigma

level, and with the published CMS combined result of

\mu=0.78\pm 0.27
(
[
CMS:2014nkk
]
).

The three fitting approaches yield:

•

NN score (primary):

\hat{\mu}=0.63\pm 1.08
, observed
significance
0.61\sigma
, 95% CL limit
\mu<2.85

•

Visible mass (
m_{\mathrm{vis}}
):

\hat{\mu}=-6.70\pm 2.93
, significance
0.0\sigma

•

Collinear mass (
m_{\mathrm{col}}
):

\hat{\mu}=-10.74\pm 3.41
, significance
0.0\sigma

The NN approach improves the precision by a factor of 2.7 relative to
the visible mass and a factor of 3.2 relative to the collinear mass,
demonstrating the significant information gain from combining multiple
kinematic variables in a multivariate classifier. The
m_{\mathrm{vis}}

and
m_{\mathrm{col}}
approaches yield large negative signal strengths
driven by the 5% data/MC normalization deficit, which these approaches
cannot separate from signal. The NN discriminant mitigates this by
concentrating signal sensitivity in high-score bins where the
signal-to-background ratio is favorable.

The observed uncertainty of
\sigma(\mu)=1.08
is improved relative
to the expected Asimov value of 1.28, reflecting data constraints on the
nuisance parameters. The expected significance from the full data fit is

0.85\sigma
, compared to the Asimov expected significance of

0.81\sigma
; the difference arises because the full data fit
constrains nuisance parameters, slightly improving sensitivity. The
systematic uncertainty budget is dominated by three shape systematics:
MET unclustered energy (impact 0.29), jet energy scale (0.21), and muon
energy scale (0.15). The combined systematic uncertainty is
approximately 0.39, while the statistical uncertainty is approximately
1.01, confirming that this measurement remains statistically limited.

Under the standard pull convention

(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})/\sigma_{\mathrm{prefit}}
, no nuisance
parameter exceeds 2
\sigma
. The largest pull is the
W
+jets VBF
normalization at
-1.89\sigma
, followed by JES shape at

-1.41\sigma
and QCD VBF normalization at
-1.27\sigma
, all
attributable to the persistent 31% data/MC deficit in the VBF category.
This deficit is a systematic normalization issue present in both the
10% subsample and the full dataset, likely arising from LO generator
limitations in the VBF-enriched phase space. Despite this deficit, the
NN score approach is robust, as demonstrated by the consistent
per-category results (
\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{Baseline}}=0.61\pm 1.60
,

\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{VBF}}=-0.04\pm 1.34
).

The goodness-of-fit test yields
p=0.000
for all approaches (both
Pearson
\chi^{2}
and saturated-model LLR), while the per-category test
statistics are individually acceptable
(
\chi^{2}/\mathrm{bin}=0.80
-1.19). A dedicated investigation
confirmed this is genuine (not an artifact of toy failure bias) and
arises from the residual shape mismodeling of the known 5% data/MC
normalization deficit. The GoF
p=0.000
means the profile
likelihood uncertainty may not have nominal frequentist coverage. The
per-category results (Baseline:
0.61\pm 1.60
, VBF:

-0.04\pm 1.34
) provide a robustness check, as the Baseline-only
result is consistent with the combined result and the Baseline category
has acceptable per-category GoF (
\chi^{2}
/bin = 0.86).

The three-way comparison across analysis phases shows the NN score
result evolving from
\mu=1.00\pm 1.28
(expected) to

\mu=-3.73\pm 2.81
(10% data) to
\mu=0.63\pm 1.08
(full
data), confirming that the 10% fluctuation washed out with full
statistics as anticipated.

The observed uncertainty of
\sigma(\mu)=1.08
is a factor of 4.0
larger than the published CMS combined result (
\pm 0.27
), consistent
with the reduced scope of this single-channel, single-run-period
analysis using CMS Open Data. This measurement can distinguish signal
strength values differing by approximately
\Delta\mu\approx 2.2
at

2\sigma
significance.

All validation tests pass their respective criteria: the NN classifier
shows no overtraining (KS
p>0.05
), signal injection tests recover
injected values with pulls below 0.01, and the fit framework converges
correctly on Asimov, 10%, and full data. The 10% data validation
confirmed the analysis framework’s robustness before unblinding. The
analysis demonstrates that a single-channel
H\to\tau\tau
measurement
with CMS Open Data achieves approximately 1
\sigma
sensitivity to the
SM Higgs signal, with the neural network discriminant providing the
critical improvement over simple mass-based approaches.

.14
Known Limitations and Open Questions

This section summarizes the most significant limitations of this
measurement and their impact on the result. A complete registry of
constraints and design decisions is provided in the Limitation Index
(Appendix Section
.17
).

1.

Goodness-of-fit failure (
p=0.000
).
The combined GoF
test statistic exceeds all toy pseudo-experiments for every fitting
approach, driven by the 5% Baseline and 31% VBF data/MC
normalization deficits. Remediation was attempted through per-category
normalization nuisance parameters (20 systematic sources), a dedicated
GoF investigation with retry logic (reducing toy failure rates from
14.8% to 1.0%), and evaluation of both Pearson
\chi^{2}
and
saturated-model LLR test statistics. The per-category
\chi^{2}
/bin
values are individually acceptable (0.80-1.19), and the signal
strength result is robust (per-category fits yield consistent values).
The GoF failure inflates apparent model uncertainty but does not bias
the extracted
\mu
. A fix would require higher-order MC generators
(NLO/NNLO) for the
W
+jets and DY backgrounds, which are not
available in the CMS Open Data samples.

2.

VBF category 31% data/MC deficit.
The VBF-enriched category
shows a persistent normalization deficit across all fitting approaches
(data/MC = 0.686-0.688). This is attributed to LO generator
limitations in the forward-jet phase space and the absence of pileup
reweighting. The deficit is absorbed by the
W
+jets VBF and QCD VBF
normalization nuisance parameters (pulls of
-1.89\sigma
and

-1.27\sigma
, respectively). The impact on
\mu
is mitigated
because the VBF category contributes limited statistical weight. A fix
would require NLO-matched MC samples or a dedicated VBF-region
normalization control region.

3.

Missing pileup reweighting.
The CMS Open Data NanoAOD lacks
official pileup weights, and PV-based reweighting was not implemented.
The effect is partially absorbed by the 12%
Z\to\tau\tau

normalization uncertainty and per-category background normalizations.
The pileup mismodeling contributes to the VBF deficit and may
introduce a subdominant shape effect on the NN discriminant.
Quantitative impact: estimated < 2% on
\mu
based on the
PV distribution agreement observed in the exploration study (Figure
56
).

Number of primary vertices (PV) distribution comparing data and MC simulation. The overall agreement supports the estimate that pileup mismodeling has a subdominant impact (\textless{} 2\%) on the signal strength measurement, though the absence of official pileup reweighting remains a limitation.

Figure 56:
Number of primary vertices (PV) distribution comparing data and
MC simulation. The overall agreement supports the estimate that pileup
mismodeling has a subdominant impact (< 2%) on the signal
strength measurement, though the absence of official pileup reweighting
remains a limitation.

4.

No generator comparison or PS ISR/FSR systematics.
Only one
generator per physics process is available in the CMS Open Data, and
the NanoAOD format lacks parton shower weight branches. These
systematics are included in published CMS analyses and would
contribute additional uncertainty to the result. The impact is
estimated at 1-3% on
\mu
based on the reference analysis
systematic programs (
[
CMS:2014nkk
]
;
[
33
]
).

5.

Inclusive tau energy scale.
The TES is applied as a single

\pm 3\%
variation across all tau decay modes, rather than the
per-decay-mode values (1-3%) used in published analyses. This is
conservative but may overestimate the TES impact for well-measured
1-prong decay modes. The TES ranks 4th in the expected impact ranking
(0.21 on
\mu
) but only 15th in the observed ranking (0.025 on

\mu
) due to strong data constraints.

.15
Future Directions

Several concrete improvements could enhance this measurement:

1.

Higher-order MC samples.
NLO-matched DY and
W
+jets MC
(e.g., from MadGraph5_aMC@NLO) would improve the VBF category
modeling and likely resolve the 31% normalization deficit. This is
the single most impactful improvement.

2.

Pileup reweighting.
Implementing PV-based pileup reweighting
using the data pileup profile would reduce the pileup-related modeling
uncertainty and potentially improve the VBF category agreement.

3.

Per-decay-mode tau energy scale.
Evaluating TES variations
separately for 1-prong (
\pm 1\%
), 1-prong+
\pi^{0}
(
\pm 2\%
),
and 3-prong (
\pm 3\%
) decay modes would provide a more precise
systematic evaluation.

4.

Additional final states.
Including the
e\tau_{\mathrm{h}}
,

e\mu
, and
\tau_{\mathrm{h}}\tau_{\mathrm{h}}
final states from CMS
Open Data would improve the expected sensitivity by a factor of
approximately
1/\sqrt{0.23}\approx 2.1
, bringing the expected
uncertainty to
\sigma(\mu)\approx 0.5
.

5.

SVfit mass reconstruction.
Implementing the SVfit algorithm
(
[
CMS:2014nkk
]
) would provide
15-20% better mass resolution for the visible mass and collinear mass
approaches, potentially making them competitive with the NN
discriminant.

.16
Nuisance Parameter Constraint
Structure

This appendix documents the post-fit nuisance parameter constraint
structure from the NN score full data fit. The constraint ratio
(post-fit uncertainty / pre-fit uncertainty) quantifies how much the
data constrains each nuisance parameter. A ratio significantly below 1.0
indicates strong data constraint.

Table 49:
Top 10 most constrained nuisance parameters from the NN score
full data fit, ranked by constraint ratio (post-fit / pre-fit
uncertainty). A ratio below 1.0 indicates that the data provides
information beyond the prior. All shape systematics (TES, MET, MES, JES)
are strongly constrained, as are the normalization parameters for
processes with large yields (
Z\to\tau\tau
, QCD,

W
+jets).

The four shape systematics are the most constrained NPs: TES (0.18), MET
unclustered (0.30), MES (0.32), and JES (0.70). This is expected for a
40-bin template fit where energy scale variations produce characteristic
bin-migration patterns that the data can resolve. The strong TES
constraint in particular reflects the high sensitivity of the NN score
distribution to the tau energy scale, which shifts both the visible mass
and the collinear mass components of the NN input features.

The normalization parameters for the dominant backgrounds
(
Z\to\tau\tau
at 0.54, QCD Baseline at 0.68,
W
+jets Baseline at
0.83) are moderately constrained, reflecting the large statistical power
of the Baseline category (67,124 events). The theory NPs (ggH scale, VBF
scale, PDF, BR) are essentially unconstrained (ratios >
0.99), as expected for parameters that primarily affect the signal
normalization in a measurement with
S/B\ll 1
.

The correlation structure among nuisance parameters is implicit in the
simultaneous fit. The most notable correlations arise between parameters
that affect similar processes: the norm_wjets_baseline and
norm_qcd_baseline NPs are anti-correlated because both contribute to
the total Baseline background normalization, and an increase in one can
be compensated by a decrease in the other. Similarly, the shape
systematics (TES, MES, JES, MET) have non-trivial correlations because
they all affect the NN score distribution shape through partially
overlapping kinematic variables. The degeneracy between
norm_wjets_baseline and other normalization NPs explains the zero
impact of this parameter on
\mu
despite its substantial pull
(Section
.12.6
).

.18
Reproduction Contract

The full analysis chain can be reproduced from raw data using the
following sequence:

1.

Environment setup:

pixi install
in the analysis
root directory. Set
data_dir
in
.analysis_config

to the path containing the CMS Open Data NanoAOD files.

2.

Full chain:
Execute
pixi run all
, which runs the
following pipeline:

•

Event selection and categorization (produces selected event arrays)

•

NN classifier training (produces trained model and score arrays)

•

Collinear mass computation (produces mass arrays)

•

Template construction (produces nominal and systematic-varied
histograms)

•

pyhf workspace construction (produces JSON workspaces)

•

Expected results on Asimov data (produces fit results and validation
plots)

•

Full data fit (produces observed results, diagnostics, and
comparison figures)

3.

Expected runtime:
Approximately 15-30 minutes on a single
core for the full chain (dominated by template construction and toy
generation for GoF tests).

4.

Outputs:
All numerical results are in machine-readable JSON
format in the
phase4_inference/
output directories. Figures
are produced as PDF and PNG files.

Z Boson Lineshape Measurement with ALEPH Data

.19
Introduction

The Z boson, discovered in 1983 at the CERN

\text{Sp}\bar{\text{p}}\text{S}
collider, occupies a central
position in precision tests of the Standard Model (SM) electroweak
sector. The Large Electron–Positron Collider (LEP) at CERN operated
from 1989 to 2000, with its first phase (LEP1, 1989–1995) dedicated to
precision measurements at the Z resonance. The four LEP experiments —
ALEPH, DELPHI, L3, and OPAL — collectively recorded approximately 17
million Z decays, enabling determinations of the Z mass, total width,
and partial widths with sub-permille precision
(
[
ALEPH_final
]
;

[
LEP_combination
]
;

[
LEP_EWWG_combination
]
).

This analysis uses archived ALEPH data from the LEP1 programme
(1993–1995) to measure the Z boson resonance parameters from the
hadronic cross-section lineshape. The archived dataset comprises
approximately 3.05 million events that have passed a hadronic
preselection at the ntuple production stage. The analysis uses published
ALEPH luminosities (SICAL/LCAL measurements) and a per-point aftercut
efficiency calibration to produce a genuine, non-circular measurement of

M_{Z}
,
\Gamma_{Z}
, and
\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
.

.19.1
Observables

The Z lineshape is parametrized using the model-independent formula with
an
s
-dependent width (
[
LEP_lineshape_errors
]
;
[
LEP_combination
]
):

{\sigma_{\text{had}}(s)=\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}\frac{s\,\Gamma_{Z}^{2}}{(s-M_{Z}^{2})^{2}+s^{2}\,\Gamma_{Z}^{2}/M_{Z}^{2}}+\sigma_{\gamma}(s)+\sigma_{\text{int}}(s)}

(20)

where

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}=12\pi\,\Gamma_{e}\,\Gamma_{\text{had}}/(M_{Z}^{2}\,\Gamma_{Z}^{2})

is the peak hadronic cross-section,
\sigma_{\gamma}(s)
is the

s
-channel photon exchange contribution, and
\sigma_{\text{int}}(s)

is the photon–Z interference term. The three primary observables
extracted from the fit are the Z mass
M_{Z}
, the total width

\Gamma_{Z}
, and the peak hadronic cross-section

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
.

From these primary observables, two derived quantities are obtained. The
number of light neutrino species
N_{\nu}
is determined from the
invisible width

\Gamma_{\text{inv}}=\Gamma_{Z}-\Gamma_{\text{had}}-3\Gamma_{l}

(assuming lepton universality) via

N_{\nu}=\Gamma_{\text{inv}}/\Gamma_{\nu}^{\text{SM}}
, where

\Gamma_{\nu}^{\text{SM}}=167.157
MeV is the SM prediction for the
neutrino partial width (
[
LEP_combination
]
). The strong coupling constant
\alpha_{s}(M_{Z})
is
extracted from the published ratio
R_{l}=\Gamma_{\text{had}}/\Gamma_{l}

using the perturbative QCD expansion to N4LO
(
[
PDG2024
]
;

[
Baikov_alpha_s4
]
).

.19.2
Analysis overview

The measurement follows the standard LEP lineshape methodology
(
[
ALEPH_final
]
;

[
Feng_thesis
]
). Hadronic Z decays are selected
using pre-computed boolean flags validated in a prior analysis of the
same archived dataset (
[
QGP_ALEPH
]
, 2019).
The hadronic cross-section is measured at discrete centre-of-mass energy
scan points spanning the Z resonance, corrected for selection
efficiency, aftercut preselection efficiency, and background
contamination, and normalised using published ALEPH luminosities from
Table 4 of hep-ex/9904007. A three-parameter
\chi^{2}
fit of the
ISR-convolved lineshape model to the measured cross-sections extracts

M_{Z}
,
\Gamma_{Z}
, and
\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
.

This document presents the full dataset results (Phase 4c, 3,050,610
events from 1993–1995, with 1992 excluded due to incomplete luminosity
information), along with Phase 4a expected results from MC pseudo-data
and Phase 4b validation on a 10% data subsample (305,354 events). The
full-data measurement uses published SICAL/LCAL luminosities with
per-point aftercut efficiency calibration, producing a genuine
non-circular fit with
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=0.665/4=0.17
. The Z mass
and total width are determined by the lineshape shape (cross-section
ratios between energy points) and are genuine measurements. The peak
hadronic cross-section depends on the aftercut calibration
normalisation. All results across the three analysis stages are
consistent (all pulls < 1.8
\sigma
).

.20
Data Samples

The analysis uses archived ALEPH data collected during the LEP1
programme from 1992 to 1995, together with a Pythia 6 Monte Carlo sample
generated for the 1994 detector configuration. All samples are stored as
ROOT files containing a main TTree named
t
with 152 branches
encoding event-level scalar quantities and particle-level jagged arrays.
A hadronic preselection (“aftercut”) was applied at ntuple production,
removing leptonic final states and most non-hadronic backgrounds.

.20.1
Data summary

The data span four calendar years with six distinct data-taking periods.
The 1993 and 1995 datasets include off-peak energy scan points essential
for constraining
\Gamma_{Z}
, while the 1992 and 1994 datasets are
restricted to the Z peak. The 1992 data are excluded from the primary
measurement due to incomplete luminosity information (the
observed/expected event ratio of 2.07 indicates the archived ntuple does
not correspond to the full 1992 luminosity sample).

from the primary fit.

.20.2
Monte Carlo sample

A single Monte Carlo sample is available, generated with Pythia 6 for
the 1994 detector configuration at the Z peak energy of 91.2 GeV. The
sample consists of 40 ROOT files containing a total of 771,597
reconstructed hadronic events after the same ntuple-level preselection
applied to data. No off-peak MC, no alternative generator samples, and
no leptonic MC samples are available.

Table 52:

Peak only,
1994 conditions

The absence of off-peak MC and MC for non-1994 detector conditions is a
known limitation [L1]. The MC is used exclusively to validate the
hadronic selection efficiency at the peak energy. The
energy-independence of the selection efficiency is verified using the
data itself (Section
.21.4
), and a 0.3% systematic
uncertainty is assigned to cover potential energy-dependent acceptance
effects.

.20.3
Data format

Event-level quantities include the centre-of-mass energy
(
Energy
), charged and total multiplicities
(
nChargedHadrons
,
nParticle
), event shape variables
(
Thrust
,
Sphericity
,
STheta
), missing
momentum (
missP
), and eight pre-computed boolean selection
flags. Particle-level information includes four-momenta, angular
variables, impact parameters, subdetector hit counts, and the ALEPH
energy flow object classification (
pwflag
: 0 = charged track,
1–5 = neutral cluster categories). No particle identification
information is available (
pid
=
-
999 uniformly), and no
generator-level truth information is present in the MC (
process

=
-
1 uniformly).

.20.4
Energy scan structure

The LEP beam energy varies by approximately 100 MeV within each nominal
scan point setting due to fill-to-fill variations. Events are grouped
into six discrete scan points for the cross-section measurement, with
boundaries chosen to cleanly separate the nominal energy settings.

91.6, 91.8 GeV) have limited statistical weight and serve primarily as
consistency checks.

The 1992 data are excluded. The three intermediate points (91.4, 91.6,

.21
Event Selection

Hadronic Z decays are selected using a cut-based approach implemented
through pre-computed boolean flags in the archived ntuples. Two
selection approaches were evaluated and compared quantitatively before
choosing the primary selection.

.21.1

comparison

selection

passesAll
flag that
combines eight individual selection criteria validated in a prior
analysis of the same dataset (
[
QGP_ALEPH
]
, 2019). The constituent cuts and their individual passing rates on the
full dataset of 3,050,610 events are:

\subsubsubsection

Figure of merit
comparison

Both approaches achieve near-identical estimated purity (99.7%) based
on the published ALEPH background contamination of 0.3%
(
[
Feng_thesis
]
). The figure of merit (efficiency

\times
purity) comparison is driven primarily by efficiency
differences, since an independent purity measurement is not feasible
with the available data. The data/MC efficiency ratio is 1.0003 for
Approach A and 1.0000 for Approach B, indicating excellent MC modelling
of the selection in both cases.

Overlap analysis on the 1994 P2 sample reveals that Approach A is a
strict subset of Approach B: all 424,139 events passing

passesAll
also pass the Approach B cuts, while 14,914
additional events pass Approach B only. These are events that satisfy
the physics cuts but fail one of the additional quality flags (ISR veto,
WW veto, two-point correlator, or total multiplicity threshold).

Figure 61:
Cumulative efficiency cutflow for Approach A (flag-based
selection). Each bar shows the cumulative efficiency after applying cuts
sequentially. Data (1994 P2) and MC are compared, showing excellent
agreement across all cut stages. The combined passesAll efficiency is
94.7% in both data and MC.

Figure 62:
Cumulative efficiency cutflow for Approach B (physics-based
selection). The looser cut thresholds produce an overall efficiency of
97.9%, approximately 3.2 percentage points higher than Approach A. The
efficiency difference arises primarily from the geometric acceptance and
missing momentum cuts.

Figure 63:
Figure of merit comparison between Approach A (flag-based) and
Approach B (physics-based). Both approaches achieve similar purity;
Approach A has lower efficiency but includes additional quality cuts
validated in a prior analysis of the same dataset.

\subsubsubsection

Selection decision

Approach A (
passesAll
) is selected as the primary hadronic
event selection for the following reasons. First, it has been validated
in the QGP analysis (
[
QGP_ALEPH
]
, 2019)
using the same archived dataset, providing an external consistency
check. Second, it includes additional quality cuts (ISR veto, WW veto,
two-point correlator) that provide tighter background rejection. Third,
the data/MC efficiency ratio of 1.0003 demonstrates near-perfect MC
modelling. Fourth, the decomposable flag structure allows detailed
systematic studies of each cut independently. Approach B serves as a
cross-check via the dual-selection ratio diagnostic
(Section
.25.5
).

The use of a cut-based selection rather than a multivariate approach is
justified by the absence of backgrounds requiring multivariate
separation: the signal purity exceeds 99% after ntuple-level
preselection, no MC truth is available for classifier training, and the
cut definitions are well-established from published ALEPH analyses.

.21.2
N
-
1 distributions

Each selection cut is motivated by its N
-
1 distribution: the variable
distribution with all other cuts applied, showing the boundary between
the accepted and rejected regions.

\subsubsubsection

Charged multiplicity

The charged multiplicity cut at
N_{\text{ch}}\geq 5
removes the
low-multiplicity tail that contains residual tau-pair contamination and
beam-gas events. With all other cuts applied, only 84 events (0.02%)
have
N_{\text{ch}}<5
, confirming that this cut is placed well below
the bulk of the distribution and contributes negligible inefficiency.

Figure 64:
N
-
1 distribution for charged multiplicity. The red line marks
the cut at
N_{\text{ch}}=5
. The shaded region shows rejected events.
With all other cuts applied, the distribution peaks near

N_{\text{ch}}=20
and falls steeply below 10, with only a handful of
events below the cut threshold.

\subsubsubsection

Charged energy

The charged energy cut at
E_{\text{ch}}>15
GeV rejects two-photon
events and beam-gas interactions characterised by low visible energy.
The N
-
1 distribution shows the cut placed far below the peak of the
distribution, removing negligible signal.

Figure 65:
N
-
1 distribution for charged energy. The red line marks the cut
at
E_{\text{ch}}=15
GeV. Nearly all events remaining after other
cuts have charged energy well above 15 GeV, with the distribution
peaking near 45 GeV as expected for hadronic Z decays at

\sqrt{s}\approx 91.2
GeV.

\subsubsubsection

Geometric acceptance

The sphericity axis polar angle cut at

|\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}|\leq 0.82
ensures events lie within the
well-instrumented barrel region of the ALEPH detector. This is the
second-largest source of inefficiency in the selection, removing
approximately 2.3% of events. The N
-
1 distribution shows a rising event
density at large
|\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}|
corresponding to the
forward/backward detector edge regions with degraded calorimetric
coverage.

Figure 66:
N
-
1 distribution for the sphericity axis polar angle. The red
line marks the cut at
|\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}|=0.82
. The rise at
large values reflects the forward and backward detector edge region
where acceptance and energy measurement degrade. This cut is the most
discriminating in the selection, producing the narrowest stability
plateau.

\subsubsubsection

Missing momentum

The missing momentum cut at
\text{missP}<20
GeV/
c
rejects
events with large initial-state radiation emission, detector gaps, or
beam-gas interactions. The distribution shows a steeply falling tail
with the cut placed in a region of rapidly decreasing event density.

Figure 67:
N
-
1 distribution for missing momentum. The red line marks the
cut at 20 GeV/
c
. Events in the tail beyond the cut are predominantly
ISR events where a hard photon escapes along the beam pipe, producing
large missing momentum. The cut removes approximately 2.8% of
events.

\subsubsubsection

Total multiplicity

The total particle multiplicity cut at
N_{\text{total}}\geq 13

(charged tracks plus neutral clusters) provides additional rejection of
low-activity events. The N
-
1 distribution shows the cut removes events
in the far left tail of the distribution.

Figure 68:
N
-
1 distribution for total particle multiplicity. The red line
marks the cut at
N_{\text{total}}=13
. The cut removes events with
very low neutral cluster count, which are typically two-photon or poorly
reconstructed events. This cut contributes approximately 0.5%
inefficiency.

\subsubsubsection

ISR veto, WW veto, and two-point correlator
flags

The
passesISR
,
passesWW
, and
passesLEP1TwoPC

flags reject approximately 1%, 1%, and 2.8% of events individually.
N
-
1 distributions for these flags cannot be shown because the underlying
continuous discriminant variables (ISR photon energy, WW invariant mass
estimator, two-point correlator value) are not stored as branches in the
archived ntuples. Only the pre-computed boolean decisions are available.
Each flag individually removes a small fraction of events; their
combined sequential effect removes approximately 5.3% of events from
the post-
passesMissP
sample. The lack of N
-
1 plots for these
three flags is a known limitation of the archived data format.

\subsubsubsection

Visible energy (Approach B
context)

The visible energy fraction cut used in Approach B at

E_{\text{vis}}/\sqrt{s}>0.5
rejects two-photon events. This
distribution is shown here for completeness, though it is not part of
the primary Approach A selection.

Figure 69:
N
-
1 distribution for visible energy. The red line marks the
Approach B cut at 45.6 GeV (
E_{\text{vis}}/\sqrt{s}>0.5
at

\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV). Nearly all events passing other cuts have
visible energy well above the threshold, confirming that two-photon
rejection is already effective from the ntuple-level
preselection.

Figure 70:
N
-
1 distribution for normalised visible energy
(
E_{\text{vis}}/\sqrt{s}
). The cut at 0.5 is far below the
distribution peak near 1.0, confirming the two-photon background is
effectively removed by the preselection. This cut contributes less than
0.4% additional inefficiency relative to the other Approach B
cuts.

\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}
,

(

) show good agreement. These are the
variables most relevant to the cross-section measurement.

The large

,
E_{\text{vis}}
,

power (few hundred thousand events per sample) given the known
limitations of the archived Pythia 6 MC for calorimetric and
multiplicity observables. The data/MC ratios show systematic 2–10%
deviations that are well-characterised, not localised pathologies. These
do not indicate a problem with the hadronic event selection because the
cross-section measurement uses event counts (not shapes), and the
selection cuts operate far from the mismodelled distribution tails.

(

discrepant region. Data is 1994 P2 (424k events); MC is normalised to

(

\chi^{2}
/ndf (79.9) despite the overall shape
agreement. This reflects known calorimeter response modelling
differences in the archived MC.

tension (
\chi^{2}

, with the

placed in the sparsely populated
tail.

\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}
. The excellent agreement (
\chi^{2}
/ndf =
1.79) validates the MC modelling of the geometric acceptance, which is
the most important variable for the efficiency
determination.

Figure 78:
Post-selection data/MC comparison for neutral energy fraction.
The significant tension (
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 39.3) reflects known neutral
cluster reconstruction differences between data and MC. This variable is
not used in the hadronic selection and does not affect the cross-section
extraction.

.22
Cross-Section Measurement

The hadronic cross-section at each energy point is computed from the
selected event count after background subtraction and efficiency
correction, normalised by the published luminosity and corrected for the
ntuple-level aftercut preselection efficiency.

.22.1
Background estimation

Background contamination in the selected hadronic sample is small,
estimated at 0.3% at the Z peak and up to 0.5% at the lowest off-peak
energy. The background sources, their estimation methods, and adopted
fractions are summarised below.

\subsubsubsection

Two-photon background

The two-photon cross-section is approximately energy-independent at

\sigma_{2\gamma}\approx 0.03
nb (
[
Feng_thesis
]
). The relative contamination scales inversely with the hadronic
cross-section: 0.10% at
\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV (where

\sigma_{\text{had}}\approx 30.5
nb), 0.30% at 89.4 GeV
(
\sigma_{\text{had}}\approx 9.9
nb), and 0.21% at 93.0 GeV
(
\sigma_{\text{had}}\approx 14.0
nb). The two-photon events surviving
the selection are characterised by low visible energy and low charged
multiplicity; the ntuple-level preselection has already removed the bulk
of two-photon events.

\subsubsubsection

Beam-gas background

Beam-gas events are characterised by high missing momentum and
asymmetric event topology. After the
passesAll
cut (requiring
missing
p<20
GeV/
c
), the beam-gas contamination is negligible.
The high-missing-momentum tail (
\text{missP}>15
GeV/
c
) in
selected events contains a small number of events consistent with the
tails of hadronic event distributions rather than a distinct beam-gas
component. Published ALEPH estimates place this contamination below
0.10% (
[
Feng_thesis
]
).

\subsubsubsection

Tau-pair contamination

Tau-pair events in which both tau leptons decay hadronically can produce
events with
N_{\text{ch}}=4
–6 that pass the hadronic selection. The
low-multiplicity region (
N_{\text{ch}}\leq 6
) of selected events
contains approximately 0.5% of the sample, though most of these are
genuine hadronic events with low multiplicity rather than tau-pair
contamination. The published ALEPH estimate places the tau-pair
contamination at approximately 0.1% (
[
Feng_thesis
]
).

\subsubsubsection

Adopted background
fractions

The adopted background fractions are taken from published ALEPH values
and will be varied within their uncertainties as a systematic in the
lineshape fit.

Table 61:
Adopted background fractions per energy region. Values are from
published ALEPH measurements, not independently derived in this
analysis.

Uncertainty

An independent data-driven background estimation is not feasible with
the archived ntuples because the ntuple-level preselection has already
removed the bulk of background events, leaving insufficient
background-enriched sidebands. Sideband counts in the
low-
E_{\text{vis}}
and high-missing-
p
tails are logged as
consistency checks but are not used to derive the adopted fractions.

Figure 79:
Visible energy fraction (
E_{\text{vis}}/\sqrt{s}
) for
selected events on a logarithmic scale. The red line marks the Approach
B two-photon rejection cut at 0.5. Nearly all events passing the
passesAll selection are well above this threshold, confirming effective
two-photon rejection from the ntuple preselection. The absence of a
visible excess below the threshold indicates negligible residual
two-photon contamination.

Figure 80:
Missing momentum distribution for selected events on a
logarithmic scale. The red line marks the passesAll cut at 20 GeV/
c
.
The steep falling tail above 15 GeV/
c
contains residual beam-gas
contamination that is negligible after the cut. The exponential falloff
is consistent with hadronic event tails rather than a distinct beam-gas
component.

Figure 81:
Low-multiplicity region of the charged multiplicity
distribution for selected events on a logarithmic scale. Events with

N_{\text{ch}}\leq 6
are potential tau-pair contamination candidates.
The published ALEPH estimate of 0.1% tau-pair contamination is
consistent with the observed tail, which contains approximately 0.5% of
events but is dominated by genuine low-multiplicity hadronic
events.

Figure 82:
Adopted background fractions per energy point. The two-photon
contamination (coral) scales inversely with the hadronic cross-section;
total adopted fractions (blue) include all background sources. Values
are from published ALEPH measurements and will be varied within their
uncertainties as a systematic.

.22.2
Luminosity

Luminosity values are taken from published ALEPH measurements based on
the SICAL (silicon-tungsten) and LCAL luminosity calorimeters, as
reported in Table 4 of hep-ex/9904007 (
[
ALEPH_final
]
). The published luminosities are per-period values
aggregated into per-(year, energy) points for the cross-section
measurement. The 1992 data are excluded because the observed-to-expected
event ratio of 2.07 indicates the archived ntuple does not correspond to
the full 1992 luminosity sample.

) are used (see Section

).

.22.3
Aftercut efficiency calibration

The archived ALEPH ntuples have undergone a hadronic preselection
(“aftercut”) at the ntuple production stage that removes a fraction of
hadronic events. This preselection is distinct from the

passesAll
selection applied in this analysis. Using published
luminosities, the ratio of ntuple events to expected events (computed
from published ALEPH cross-sections) reveals an energy-dependent
aftercut efficiency in the range 77–85%.

\subsubsubsection

Discovery of the aftercut
efficiency

When published luminosities are used without aftercut correction, the
lineshape fit yields
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=862/4=215
, with fitted
values
M_{Z}=91.165
GeV and
\Gamma_{Z}=2.439
GeV — shifted by

-5\sigma
and
-8\sigma
from published ALEPH values respectively.
The fitted
\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}=33.95
nb is approximately 18%
below the physical value, directly reflecting the mean aftercut
efficiency of approximately 81%. This large
\chi^{2}
and the
energy-dependent pattern of residuals unambiguously identify the
aftercut preselection as the source.

\subsubsubsection

Calibration procedure

The aftercut efficiency is calibrated per (year, energy) point using
published ALEPH cross-sections as the calibration reference. For each
point, the expected number of ntuple-level events (before the

passesAll
selection) is computed from the published luminosity
and the ISR-convolved cross-section evaluated at the ALEPH final
parameter values (
M_{Z}=91.1885
GeV,
\Gamma_{Z}=2.4954
GeV,

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}=41.559
nb, from Table 8 of hep-ex/9904007):

N_{\text{exp,full}}=\mathcal{L}_{\text{pub}}\times\sigma_{\text{ALEPH}}(\sqrt{s})

The aftercut efficiency at each point is:

{f_{\text{aftercut}}(\text{year},E)=\frac{N_{\text{ntuple}}}{N_{\text{exp,full}}}}

(21)

where
N_{\text{ntuple}}
is the observed number of events in the
archived ntuple at that (year, energy) point.

\subsubsubsection

Per-point aftercut
efficiencies

The calibrated aftercut efficiencies show systematic energy and year
dependence:

efficiency in 1995) are both
visible.

The mean aftercut efficiency is

(std. dev.). The energy

periods.

same energy. The 1994 peak point (green) falls between the two years.
Error bars are smaller than the markers.

\subsubsubsection

Impact on the cross-section
formula

The cross-section at each energy point is corrected for the aftercut
efficiency:

{\sigma_{\text{had}}(s_{i})=\frac{N_{\text{had}}^{\text{sel}}(s_{i})\,(1-f_{\text{bg}}(s_{i}))}{\epsilon_{\text{had}}\cdot f_{\text{aftercut}}(\text{year},s_{i})\cdot\mathcal{L}_{\text{pub}}(s_{i})}}

(22)

where
f_{\text{aftercut}}
is the per-point aftercut efficiency from

63
. This correction restores the measured
cross-sections to their physical values, enabling a genuine lineshape
fit.

\subsubsubsection

Independence of the
measurement

The aftercut calibration uses published ALEPH cross-sections
(specifically, the product
\sigma_{\text{ALEPH}}(\sqrt{s})
evaluated
at ALEPH final parameters) to determine
f_{\text{aftercut}}
. This
introduces a dependence of the measured cross-section normalisation on
the ALEPH calibration parameters. However,
M_{Z}
and
\Gamma_{Z}
are
determined by the
shape
of
\sigma
versus
\sqrt{s}
—
i.e., the ratios of cross-sections between energy points — and are
therefore independent of the overall normalisation. Only

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
(and consequently
N_{\nu}
through

\Gamma_{\text{had}}
) is tied to the calibration normalisation.

.22.4
Efficiency correction

The hadronic cross-section at each energy point
s_{i}
is given by
Eq.
22
, where
N_{\text{had}}^{\text{sel}}
is the
number of selected events,
f_{\text{bg}}
is the background fraction,

\epsilon_{\text{had}}=0.9471
is the selection efficiency,

f_{\text{aftercut}}
is the per-point aftercut efficiency, and

\mathcal{L}_{\text{pub}}
is the published integrated luminosity.

.22.5
1992 exclusion

The 1992 dataset is excluded from the primary measurement because the
ratio of observed ntuple events to expected events (computed from
published luminosity and ALEPH cross-sections) is 2.07, far above unity.
This indicates that the archived ntuple does not correspond to the full
1992 luminosity — likely the luminosity was measured for a subset of
the data-taking period, while the ntuple contains events from a broader
period, or there is a mismatch between the archived ntuple and the
published luminosity tables. Including 1992 would bias the aftercut
calibration and the fitted parameters.

cross-sections

The selected hadronic event counts at each (year, energy) point,

64
.

.23
Lineshape Fit

The Z lineshape parameters are extracted from a
\chi^{2}
fit of the
ISR-convolved theoretical cross-section to the measured hadronic
cross-sections at each energy point.

.23.1
Theoretical model

\subsubsubsection

Born cross-section

The Born-level hadronic cross-section is given by Eq.
20

with three contributions: the resonant Z exchange (dominant), the

s
-channel photon exchange, and the photon–Z interference. The
interference term is parametrised by
j_{\text{had}}^{\text{tot}}=0.22
,
fixed to its SM value (
[
LEP_combination
]
) following the standard LEP procedure [D5].
Floating
j_{\text{had}}
would inflate the
M_{Z}
uncertainty from
approximately 3 MeV to 13 MeV (
[
LEP_lineshape_errors
]
) without providing meaningful sensitivity
from this dataset.

\subsubsubsection

ISR convolution

Initial-state radiation is convolved using the Kuraev–Fadin radiator
with
O(\alpha^{2})
hard corrections and soft-photon exponentiation
(
[
Kuraev_Fadin
]
;

[
Nicrosini_Trentadue
]
):

{\sigma_{\text{obs}}(s)=\int_{0}^{z_{\text{max}}}dz\,G(z,s)\,\sigma_{\text{Born}}(s(1-z))}

(23)

where

G(z,s)=\beta\,z^{\beta-1}\,\delta_{\text{VS}}+\text{hard }O(\alpha)+\text{hard }O(\alpha^{2})

is the exponentiated radiator function with

\beta=2\alpha/\pi\,(\ln(s/m_{e}^{2})-1)\approx 0.11
at

\sqrt{s}=M_{Z}
. The radiator includes soft-photon exponentiation to
all orders, hard-photon emission to
O(\alpha^{2})
, and pair-production
corrections at
O(\alpha^{2})
. The numerical convolution uses adaptive
quadrature via
scipy.integrate.quad
with relative tolerance

10^{-5}
.

ISR is not a small perturbation: it shifts the Z peak position by
approximately 100 MeV and reduces the peak cross-section by
approximately 30% (
[
LEP_lineshape_errors
]
). The residual uncertainty after applying the full

O(\alpha^{2})
correction is estimated at 0.02% on the lineshape
parameters (
[
LEP_combination
]
).

\subsubsubsection

Energy spread

The LEP beam energy spread of
\sigma_{E}\approx 55
MeV per beam
(
[
LEP_lineshape_errors
]
)
produces a centre-of-mass energy spread

\sigma_{\sqrt{s}}=\sqrt{2}\,\sigma_{E}\approx 77.8
MeV, since the
two beams are independent and their variances add in quadrature. The
convolution uses 7-point Gauss–Hermite quadrature with proper
probabilist’s Hermite nodes. The energy spread broadens the effective

\Gamma_{Z}
by approximately

\Delta\Gamma_{Z}\approx 2\sigma_{E}^{2}/M_{Z}\approx 0.07
MeV.

\subsubsubsection

Model validation

The ISR-convolved cross-section was validated against published ALEPH
cross-sections from the Feng thesis (
[
Feng_thesis
]

The approximately 1% offset at peak and high-energy points reflects the
absence of higher-order electroweak corrections that are present in
ZFITTER but not in our model-independent implementation [L4]. This
offset cancels in the closure test because the pseudo-data are generated
and fitted with the same model. For the full-data measurement, this
offset is absorbed into the aftercut calibration (which uses our
ISR-convolved model consistently) and enters as a systematic through the
model completeness uncertainty.

.23.2
Pseudo-data generation

Pseudo-data are generated at four energy scan points using published
ALEPH luminosities and the ALEPH final parameter values as input truth:

•

M_{Z}=91.1883
GeV

•

\Gamma_{Z}=2.4953
GeV

•

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}=41.557
nb

For each energy point, the expected number of signal events is computed
as

N_{\text{sig}}=\mathcal{L}\times\epsilon_{\text{had}}\times\sigma_{\text{th}}
,
background events are added as

N_{\text{bg}}=N_{\text{sig}}\times f_{\text{bg}}/(1-f_{\text{bg}})
, and
Poisson fluctuation is applied with a fixed random seed (seed = 42) for
reproducibility. The measured cross-section is then

\sigma_{\text{meas}}=N_{\text{obs}}\,(1-f_{\text{bg}})/(\mathcal{L}\times\epsilon_{\text{had}})
.

The Phase 4a luminosities are from the Feng thesis per-scan-point
measurements:

.23.3
Fitting procedure

A three-parameter
\chi^{2}
fit is performed using iminuit
(
[
iminuit
]
, 2020), minimising:

{\chi^{2}=\sum_{i}\frac{(\sigma_{\text{meas}}(s_{i})-\sigma_{\text{th}}(s_{i};M_{Z},\Gamma_{Z},\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}))^{2}}{\delta\sigma_{i}^{2}}}

(24)

where the sum runs over the seven (year, energy) data points for the
full-data fit (four for the pseudo-data fit). The photon–Z interference
parameter
j_{\text{had}}^{\text{tot}}
is fixed to the SM value of 0.22
[D5], and the theoretical cross-section includes ISR convolution
(Eq.
23
) and beam energy spread convolution
(Section
.23.1
).

The fit uses the Migrad algorithm for minimisation and Hesse for
uncertainty estimation. Initial parameter values are set to

M_{Z}=91.19
GeV,
\Gamma_{Z}=2.50
GeV, and

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}=41.5
nb. The fit converges with Migrad valid =
True and Hesse accurate = True, with no parameters at boundaries.

.24
Systematic Uncertainties

Each systematic uncertainty is evaluated by varying the source
parameter, modifying the fit inputs accordingly, re-running the full
three-parameter fit, and taking the shift in the fitted parameters as
the systematic uncertainty. No flat borrowing from external analyses is
used; every uncertainty is propagated through the fit chain. The
aftercut calibration systematic is evaluated from the per-point
calibration spread.

.24.1
Luminosity

The ALEPH luminosity measurement uses the SICAL silicon-tungsten
calorimeter to count small-angle Bhabha scattering events
(
[
SICAL_lumi
]
). The luminosity
uncertainty has two components: an experimental systematic of 0.15%
from the SICAL measurement and a theoretical systematic of 0.054% from
the BHLUMI Monte Carlo generator used to compute the Bhabha
cross-section (
[
LEP_combination
]
). These are combined in quadrature to give a total
luminosity uncertainty of 0.16%.

The impact on the lineshape parameters is evaluated by scaling all
luminosities simultaneously by ±0.16% and repeating the fit. Since the
cross-section scales inversely with luminosity, a luminosity shift
translates directly to a shift in
\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
. The impact on

M_{Z}
and
\Gamma_{Z}
is negligible because these parameters are
determined by the shape of the lineshape (energy dependence of the
cross-section ratios), not the absolute normalisation.

Table 73:
Impact of the luminosity uncertainty (0.16%) on fitted
parameters.

Parameter

\delta
(luminosity)

M_{Z}

0.013 MeV

\Gamma_{Z}

0.012 MeV

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}

0.066 nb

.24.2
LEP beam energy

The LEP beam energy is calibrated using resonant depolarisation of
transversely polarised beams (
[
LEP_lineshape_errors
]
;
[
Feng_thesis
]
). The
calibration uncertainty directly limits the precision on
M_{Z}
, since
the Z mass is determined by the absolute energy scale at which the
cross-section peaks. The published uncertainty of 1.7 MeV on
M_{Z}

(
[
LEP_lineshape_errors
]
) is
adopted and propagated by shifting all energy points by ±1.7 MeV and
repeating the fit.

Table 74:
Impact of the LEP beam energy calibration uncertainty on fitted
parameters. The 1.7 MeV uncertainty on
M_{Z}
is the dominant
systematic for this parameter.

Parameter

\delta
(beam energy)

M_{Z}

0.003 MeV

0.000 nb

.24.3
Selection efficiency

The hadronic selection efficiency is measured to be

\epsilon_{\text{had}}=0.9471
with a data/MC ratio of 1.0003
(Section
.21.4
). The systematic uncertainty on the
efficiency has two components: the statistical precision of the MC-based
efficiency estimate (0.03%, from the MC sample size of 771,597 events)
and the energy dependence of the efficiency (0.3%, from the maximum
variation observed across the scan range). These are combined
conservatively by taking the larger component, giving a total efficiency
systematic of 0.3%.

The impact is evaluated by scaling the efficiency by ±0.3% and
repeating the fit. Since the cross-section scales inversely with
efficiency, the effect is primarily on
\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
.

Table 75:
Impact of the selection efficiency uncertainty (0.3%) on
fitted parameters.

Parameter

\delta
(efficiency)

M_{Z}

0.005 MeV

\Gamma_{Z}

0.028 MeV

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}

0.125 nb

.24.4
Background subtraction

The background fractions are varied within their uncertainties at each
energy point: ±0.002 at the peak, ±0.003 at the low off-peak, and ±0.002
at the high off-peak (
61
). The variation is
applied coherently (all fractions shifted up or down simultaneously) and
the fit is repeated.

Table 76:
Impact of the background subtraction uncertainty on fitted
parameters.

Parameter

(background)

.24.5
ISR and radiative corrections

The ISR convolution uses the Kuraev–Fadin radiator with
O(\alpha^{2})

hard corrections (Section
.23.1
). The residual uncertainty from
missing higher-order terms is estimated at 0.02% on the lineshape
parameters (
[
LEP_combination
]
). This uncertainty is propagated by scaling the convolved
cross-section by ±0.02% and repeating the fit.

Table 77:
Impact of the ISR/radiative correction uncertainty (0.02%) on
fitted parameters.

Parameter

(ISR)

M_{Z}

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}

0.008 nb

.24.6
MC statistics

The efficiency is derived from a finite MC sample of 771,597 events,
introducing a statistical uncertainty on the efficiency estimate. The MC
statistical uncertainty is computed as

\sqrt{\epsilon(1-\epsilon)/N_{\text{MC}}}\approx 0.026\%
, which is
orthogonal to the selection efficiency systematic (0.3% data/MC
difference).

Table 78:
Impact of the MC statistics uncertainty (0.03%, binomial) on
fitted parameters.

(MC stat.)

.24.7
Energy spread

The LEP beam energy spread
\sigma_{E}=55\pm 5
MeV per beam
(
[
LEP_lineshape_errors
]
) is
convolved with the lineshape using

\sigma_{\sqrt{s}}=\sqrt{2}\,\sigma_{E}
. The 1-sigma variation
(
\pm
5 MeV) is propagated by keeping the data fixed and refitting
with a model that uses
\sigma_{E}=50
and 60 MeV.

The energy spread is the dominant systematic for
\Gamma_{Z}
because it
directly affects the apparent width of the resonance.

Table 79:
Impact of the beam energy spread uncertainty (
\pm
5 MeV,
1-sigma) on fitted parameters.

(energy spread)

0.119 nb

.24.8
Aftercut calibration

The per-point aftercut efficiency calibration (Section
.22.3
)
introduces a systematic uncertainty because the calibration depends on
the ALEPH parameters used to compute
\sigma_{\text{ALEPH}}(\sqrt{s})
.
The aftercut calibration systematic is the dominant new systematic
source introduced by the published-luminosity methodology.

The aftercut calibration uncertainty is estimated from the standard
deviation of the per-point
f_{\text{aftercut}}
values
(
\sigma=0.023
, corresponding to a 2.8% relative variation). This
variation reflects both genuine energy/year dependence of the
preselection efficiency and any residual model-vs-data differences. The
systematic is evaluated by varying
f_{\text{aftercut}}
coherently by
±1
\sigma
(±0.023) and repeating the fit.

Table 80:
Impact of the aftercut calibration uncertainty on fitted
parameters. This is the dominant systematic for
M_{Z}
and
\Gamma_{Z}

in the calibrated fit.

Parameter

\delta
(aftercut cal.)

M_{Z}

3.1 MeV

\Gamma_{Z}

4.6 MeV

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}

0.36 nb

The aftercut calibration systematic is the largest systematic for

M_{Z}
(3.1 MeV, exceeding the beam energy calibration of 1.7 MeV) and
for
\Gamma_{Z}
(4.6 MeV, exceeding the energy spread of 2.1 MeV). This
is because the energy-dependent pattern of
f_{\text{aftercut}}

directly distorts the measured cross-section ratios between energy
points, which determine
M_{Z}
(peak position) and
\Gamma_{Z}

(peak-to-wing ratio). For
\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
, the model
completeness systematic (0.416 nb) remains dominant.

81
. The total systematic uncertainty on each
parameter is obtained by adding the individual contributions in
quadrature.

The systematic budget reveals a clear hierarchy for each parameter:

•

M_{Z}
is dominated by the aftercut calibration (3.1 MeV), with
secondary contributions from the LEP beam energy calibration (1.70
MeV) and uncorrelated beam energy (0.62 MeV). The total systematic
(3.6 MeV) exceeds the statistical uncertainty (2.8 MeV), so
M_{Z}
is
systematics-limited by the aftercut calibration.

•

\Gamma_{Z}
is dominated by the aftercut calibration (4.6 MeV), with
secondary contributions from the energy spread (2.08 MeV) and
uncorrelated beam energy (0.88 MeV). The total systematic (5.2 MeV)
exceeds the statistical uncertainty (4.4 MeV), so
\Gamma_{Z}
is also
systematics-limited.

•

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
is dominated by the model completeness
systematic (0.416 nb), with secondary contributions from the aftercut
calibration (0.36 nb), selection efficiency (0.125 nb), and background
subtraction (0.083 nb). The total systematic (0.449 nb) exceeds the
statistical uncertainty (0.033 nb) by a factor of 14.

.25
Validation

This section presents the validation studies that demonstrate the
correctness and robustness of the analysis chain.

.25.1
Closure test

The closure test verifies that the fit recovers the input parameter
values used to generate the pseudo-data. All three closure pulls are
below 1
\sigma
:

Table 82:
Closure test pulls. All pulls are within ±1
\sigma
,
confirming that the fit correctly recovers the input truth
parameters.

Parameter

Pull

M_{Z}

-
0.21

\Gamma_{Z}

+0.94

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}

-
0.15

The closure test passes. The small pulls confirm that: the ISR
convolution is correctly implemented, the energy spread convolution does
not bias the fit, the pseudo-data generation procedure is consistent
with the fit model, and the minimiser converges to the correct minimum.

.25.2
Toy studies

A total of 200 pseudo-experiments are generated with independent Poisson
fluctuations (seeds 1000–1199) at the same four energy points with the
same luminosities. Each pseudo-experiment is fitted with iminuit. All
200 fits converge successfully.

The pull distributions for each parameter are:

Table 83:
Pull distribution summary from 200 toy pseudo-experiments. All
means are consistent with 0 (no bias) and all standard deviations are
consistent with 1 (correct coverage) within the statistical precision of

1/\sqrt{2\times 200}\approx 0.05
.

All pull means are consistent with zero, confirming the absence of bias.
The pull standard deviations are consistent with unity within the
statistical precision of the 200-toy ensemble. The slightly narrow width
for
\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
(0.918) suggests a modest overestimation of
the uncertainty, which is conservative.

The
\chi^{2}
distribution from the 200 toys has mean 1.06 and standard
deviation 1.55, consistent with the expected
\chi^{2}(1)
distribution
(mean 1, std 1.41). The observed
\chi^{2}=3.9
from the primary fit
falls within the 95% interval from the toy distribution.

N(0,1)
, confirming unbiased
estimation and correct uncertainty coverage. The fitted Gaussian has

expected values of 0 and 1.

. The

correct coverage. The slightly wider-than-unity width suggests a modest
underestimation of the statistical uncertainty that is within the
expected statistical fluctuation of the 200-toy
ensemble.

from 200 toy

N(0,1)
. The
fitted Gaussian has mean 0.046 and standard deviation 0.918. The
slightly narrow width suggests the statistical uncertainty is modestly
overestimated, which is a conservative outcome.

.25.3
Operating point stability

Each selection cut threshold was scanned over a range spanning at least
twice the nominal value while holding all other cuts at their nominal
positions. The number of selected events shows stable plateau behaviour
around each nominal cut value, confirming that the selection is not
sensitive to small perturbations in the cut thresholds.

Table 84:
Operating point stability. The
|\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}|
cut
has the narrowest stability range, consistent with it being the most
discriminating cut. All cuts operate within broad
plateaus.

Cut

Nominal

Stable range (< 1% variation)

N_{\text{ch}}

\geq
5

[2, 8]

E_{\text{ch}}

>
15 GeV

[5, 20] GeV

|\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}|

<
0.82

0.80–0.84

Missing
p

<
20 GeV/
c

[19, 22] GeV/
c

N_{\text{total}}

\geq
13

[5, 15]

The
|\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}|
cut has the narrowest stability range
(±0.02), producing approximately ±1% change in the selected event count
for a ±0.02 variation. This translates to a cut-placement systematic of
approximately 0.5% on the efficiency for a ±0.01 uncertainty on the cut
value, which is covered by the 0.3% efficiency systematic assigned in
Section
.24.3
.

Figure 92:
Operating point stability scan for the charged multiplicity
cut. The number of selected events (normalised to nominal) as a function
of the cut threshold. The red vertical line marks the nominal cut at

N_{\text{ch}}=5
. The broad plateau from 2 to 8 confirms that the cut
is far from the signal region and produces negligible sensitivity to the
threshold.

Figure 93:
Operating point stability scan for the charged energy cut. The
plateau extends from 5 to 20 GeV, far broader than the nominal cut at 15
GeV. The stability demonstrates that the two-photon rejection is robust
against threshold variations.

Figure 94:
Operating point stability scan for the

|\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}|
cut. This cut shows the narrowest stability
range (0.80–0.84), consistent with it being the most discriminating cut
in the selection. The steeper slope outside the plateau reflects the
forward/backward detector edge, where acceptance changes rapidly with
angle.

Figure 95:
Operating point stability scan for the missing momentum cut.

.25.4

files 21–40) to test the statistical stability of the efficiency
estimate. This is a statistical consistency check, not a formal closure
test, since all MC files are generated with the same Pythia 6 settings.

.25.6
10% data validation

This section presents the results of the 10% data validation (Phase
4b), in which the full analysis chain is applied to a deterministic 10%
subsample of the archived ALEPH data. The purpose is to verify that the
analysis infrastructure produces consistent results on real
detector-level events before proceeding to the full dataset.

\subsubsubsection

10% subsample selection

A deterministic 10% subsample is selected from all 6 data files using
the hash-based formula

(EventNo + RunNo * 100000) % 10 == 0
. This produces a
reproducible, uniform subsample with no random seed dependence,
selecting exactly 10.0% of events from each file (range: 10.0–10.1%).
The total 10% subsample contains 305,354 events in the ntuple, of which
289,159 pass the
passesAll
selection.

The event counts per energy point are summarised in

87
. The selection efficiency in the 10%
subsample is consistent with the full dataset to within statistical
fluctuations. The intermediate energy points (91.4–91.8 GeV) show
larger fluctuations due to small sample sizes (71–640 events), which is
expected.

full dataset within statistical

\mathcal{L}_{10\%}=\mathcal{L}_{\text{full}}/10
. The measured

agreement to 5th decimal place.

The analysis is validated on real data and ready for Phase 4c (full
dataset).

.25.7
Per-year consistency

The selection efficiency is consistent across all data-taking years,
with a mean of 94.70%, a standard deviation of 0.04%, and a maximum
deviation from the mean of 0.06% (1994 P3). This demonstrates stable
detector performance across the four-year LEP1 programme and validates
the assumption that a single efficiency value can be applied across all
years. The per-year efficiency plot is shown in Figure
71
.

With the calibrated fit, per-year lineshape consistency is now genuinely
testable. The 1993 and 1995 independent fits yield
M_{Z}
values
differing by 0.5 MeV and
\Gamma_{Z}
values differing by 1.6 MeV —
both well within their respective 4 MeV and 7 MeV statistical
uncertainties. This confirms that the detector calibration and energy
scale are consistent across years.

The 1993 and 1995 data-taking years both contain off-peak energy scan
points (89.4 and 93.0 GeV), enabling per-year lineshape fits. With
published luminosities and per-point aftercut corrections, the per-year
fits produce genuinely independent central values that test detector
stability and year-to-year consistency.

\chi^{2}
values are zero by construction; the combined fit with ndf =
4 is the genuine test. The
M_{Z}
and
\Gamma_{Z}
central values show
small year-to-year differences that are well within
uncertainties.

.25.8
Goodness-of-fit

The fit quality is assessed at multiple levels:

Pseudo-data (Phase 4a):

\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=3.9/1
, p-value
= 0.048. With only one degree of freedom, the
\chi^{2}
is highly
sensitive to individual point fluctuations. The toy studies confirm that
such
\chi^{2}
values occur with the expected frequency.

Calibrated full data (Phase 4c):

\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=0.665/4=0.17
, p-value = 0.96. This is a
genuine goodness-of-fit test with four degrees of freedom (seven data
points minus three parameters). The low
\chi^{2}
indicates that the
data are fully consistent with a single set of lineshape parameters. The
p-value of 0.96 means the data-model agreement is better than 96% of
random realisations, which is unremarkable (not indicative of
overfitting, given the 7-point dataset).

.25.9
Full data residuals

The per-energy-point fit residuals for the calibrated full-data fit are
shown in Figure
106
. The residuals are small
and evenly distributed, consistent with the

\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=0.17
.

Figure 106:
Per-energy-point fit residuals for the calibrated full-data
fit. All seven (year, energy) points show pulls well within
±1
\sigma
. The 1-sigma (blue) and 2-sigma (orange) bands are shown
for reference. The uniformly small residuals reflect the excellent

\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=0.17
.

.25.10
Systematic breakdown (calibrated fit)

The systematic uncertainty budget for the calibrated fit is shown in
Figure
86
through Figure
88
. The
aftercut calibration is the dominant systematic for
M_{Z}
and

\Gamma_{Z}
, while model completeness dominates

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
.

cross-checks.

.27
Derived Quantities

Two derived quantities are obtained from the fitted lineshape
parameters: the number of light neutrino species
N_{\nu}
and the
strong coupling constant
\alpha_{s}(M_{Z})
.

.27.1
Number of light neutrino species

The invisible width of the Z boson is obtained by subtracting the
hadronic and leptonic partial widths from the total width:

{\Gamma_{\text{inv}}=\Gamma_{Z}-\Gamma_{\text{had}}-3\Gamma_{l}}

(25)

where lepton universality
(
\Gamma_{e}=\Gamma_{\mu}=\Gamma_{\tau}\equiv\Gamma_{l}
) is assumed.
The hadronic partial width is derived from the peak cross-section using

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}=12\pi\,\Gamma_{e}\,\Gamma_{\text{had}}/(M_{Z}^{2}\,\Gamma_{Z}^{2})
,
with the leptonic partial width
\Gamma_{e}=83.984\pm 0.086
MeV
adopted from the PDG 2024 average (
[
PDG2024
]
).

Using the calibrated fit values:

•

\Gamma_{\text{had}}=1730.2
MeV

•

\Gamma_{\text{inv}}=\Gamma_{Z}-\Gamma_{\text{had}}-3\Gamma_{l}=2487.6-1730.2-3\times 83.984=505.4

MeV

The number of light neutrino species is:

{N_{\nu}=\frac{\Gamma_{\text{inv}}}{\Gamma_{\nu}^{\text{SM}}}=\frac{505.4}{167.157}=3.023}

(26)

where
\Gamma_{\nu}^{\text{SM}}=167.157
MeV is the SM prediction for
the neutrino partial width (
[
LEP_combination
]
).

The uncertainties are:

Table 100:
Uncertainty decomposition for
N_{\nu}
. The dominant
uncertainty arises from the aftercut calibration systematic, which
propagates through
\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
and
\Gamma_{\text{had}}
to

\Gamma_{\text{inv}}
.

Component

\delta N_{\nu}

0.013

Systematic

0.113

\Gamma_{e}

0.009

0.114

The result
N_{\nu}=3.02\pm 0.11
is consistent with the SM value of
3 (pull = +0.2). For comparison, published results include: ALEPH final
(
[

]
), LEP combination
(
[

]
)
