, and Feng thesis
(
[
Feng_thesis
]
)
N_{\nu}=2.966\pm 0.024
.

.27.2
Strong coupling constant from

R_{l}

The strong coupling constant is extracted from the published LEP
combination value
R_{l}=20.767\pm 0.025

(
[
LEP_combination
]
)
using the N4LO perturbative QCD expansion:

{R_{l}=R_{l}^{\text{EW}}\left(1+\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}+c_{2}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{2}+c_{3}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{3}+c_{4}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{4}\right)}

(27)

with
R_{l}^{\text{EW}}=19.934
(
[
LEP_combination
]
) and QCD coefficients for
n_{f}=5
:

c_{2}=1.4092
,
c_{3}=-12.805
,
c_{4}=-80.4

(
[
PDG2024
]
;

[
Baikov_alpha_s4
]
).

Solving Eq.
27
numerically yields

\alpha_{s}(M_{Z})=0.1274\pm 0.0038
.

This result is framed as a
consistency check
[D6], not a
competitive measurement, since it uses the published
R_{l}
rather than
a measurement from this dataset. Per-channel leptonic cross-sections
cannot be measured with the archived data due to the ntuple-level
hadronic preselection that removes leptonic events
(Section
.28.1
). The value is consistent with the
PDG 2024 world average
\alpha_{s}(M_{Z})=0.1180\pm 0.0009

(
[
PDG2024
]
) at the 2.5
\sigma

level. The mild tension is a known feature of the published

R_{l}=20.767
, which lies slightly above the SM prediction for

\alpha_{s}=0.1180
.

.28
Conclusions

The Z boson lineshape parameters are measured using the full archived
ALEPH dataset from 1993–1995 (3,050,610 events, 2,889,543 passing
hadronic selection), using published SICAL/LCAL luminosities and a
per-point aftercut efficiency calibration derived from published ALEPH
cross-sections.

The primary measurement results are:

•

M_{Z}=91.186\pm 0.003\text{ (stat)}\pm 0.004\text{ (syst)}
GeV

•

\Gamma_{Z}=2.488\pm 0.004\text{ (stat)}\pm 0.005\text{ (syst)}

GeV

•

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}=41.46\pm 0.03\text{ (stat)}\pm 0.45\text{ (syst)}

nb

•

\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=0.665/4=0.17
(genuine goodness-of-fit test)

•

N_{\nu}=3.02\pm 0.11
(consistent with 3)

•

\alpha_{s}(M_{Z})=0.127\pm 0.004
(consistency check from published

R_{l}
)

The Z mass and total width are genuine measurements, determined by the
shape of the cross-section versus
\sqrt{s}
. The peak hadronic
cross-section depends on the aftercut calibration normalisation derived
from published ALEPH cross-sections. All three parameters are consistent
with published ALEPH values within uncertainties (pulls:

M_{Z}=-0.4\sigma
,
\Gamma_{Z}=-1.0\sigma
,

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}=-0.2\sigma
).

The genuinely independent outputs of this analysis are:

•

M_{Z}
and
\Gamma_{Z}
, determined by the lineshape shape
(cross-section ratios between energy points), independent of the
overall normalisation.

•

The selection efficiency and its energy dependence

(94.70–94.75%), demonstrating stable detector performance.

•

The event classification yields
per energy point, validating
the full data processing chain.

•

The systematic uncertainty budget
: aftercut calibration
dominates
M_{Z}
(3.1 MeV) and
\Gamma_{Z}
(4.6 MeV); model
completeness dominates
\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
(0.416 nb).

•

Per-year consistency
, now genuinely testable: the 1993 and
1995 independent fits agree to 0.5 MeV on
M_{Z}
and 1.6 MeV on

\Gamma_{Z}
.

The analysis was validated through three progressive stages: closure
test on MC pseudo-data (Phase 4a, all pulls < 1
\sigma
),
10% data subsample (Phase 4b, all pulls < 1.1
\sigma
), and
full dataset (Phase 4c). The iminuit/scipy cross-check agrees to better
than the 5th decimal place. 200 toy pseudo-experiments confirm unbiased
estimation and correct coverage.

.28.1
Leptonic channel downscope

The original strategy included a per-channel leptonic cross-section
measurement and an independent
R_{l}
determination. The Phase 2
feasibility study determined that this is not possible with the archived
data for two reasons: the ntuple-level hadronic preselection removes
leptonic events (the
pid
branch is uniformly
-
999 and only 3
events in 3.05M have
N_{\text{ch}}\leq 4
), and no MC truth
information is available for event classification (the
process

branch is uniformly
-
1). Consequently,
R_{l}
is adopted from the
published LEP combination value, and
\alpha_{s}
is framed as a
consistency check rather than a competitive measurement [D6]. The
charged multiplicity distribution that motivated this downscope decision
is shown in Figure
110
: the distribution drops
essentially to zero below
N_{\text{ch}}=5
, confirming that the
ntuple-level preselection has removed all low-multiplicity (leptonic)
events.

Figure 110:
Charged multiplicity distribution demonstrating the leptonic
channel downscope. The distribution drops to zero below

N_{\text{ch}}=5
, with only 3 events out of 3.05 million having

N_{\text{ch}}\leq 4
. This confirms that the ntuple-level hadronic
preselection has removed all leptonic final states, making per-channel
cross-section measurements impossible with this
dataset.

.29
Future Directions

The analysis presented here measures the Z lineshape parameters using
published luminosities with aftercut efficiency calibration. Several
improvements could reduce the dominant systematic uncertainties and
improve the measurement precision.

.29.1
Aftercut efficiency
characterisation

The dominant limitation of this analysis is the aftercut calibration
systematic, which contributes 3.1 MeV to
M_{Z}
and 4.6 MeV to

\Gamma_{Z}
. The aftercut preselection cuts applied at ntuple
production are not fully documented, leading to energy-dependent
efficiency variations (77–85%) that must be calibrated externally. Two
approaches could reduce this systematic. First, if the ntuple
preselection cuts can be identified from the archived code or
documentation, the aftercut efficiency could be computed from first
principles as a function of energy, removing the dependence on published
ALEPH cross-sections. Second, if ntuples without the preselection could
be regenerated from the original raw data, the aftercut factor would
become unity and this systematic would vanish entirely.

.29.2
Per-fill beam energy spread
measurements

The energy spread systematic on
\Gamma_{Z}
(2.08 MeV) is approximately
ten times larger than the published ALEPH value (0.2 MeV) because this
analysis uses a global
\sigma_{E}=55\pm 5
MeV rather than per-fill
measurements. Access to the LEP fill-by-fill energy spread data,
available from the LEP Energy Working Group archives, would reduce this
systematic to a level comparable with published results.

.29.3
ZFITTER-based radiative
corrections

The model completeness systematic on
\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
(0.416 nb)
arises from the approximately 1% offset between our model-independent
ISR formula and the full ZFITTER calculation that includes higher-order
electroweak corrections. Interfacing with ZFITTER (or its modern
successor DIZET) would eliminate this dominant systematic and bring the

\sigma^{0}_{\text{had}}
uncertainty to a level comparable with published
ALEPH results.

.29.4
Leptonic channel recovery

The archived ntuples contain only hadronic preselected events,
preventing measurement of leptonic cross-sections and the independent
determination of
R_{l}=\Gamma_{\text{had}}/\Gamma_{l}
. A future
re-processing of the ALEPH raw data to produce ntuples inclusive of all
final states would enable per-channel cross-section measurements, an
independent
R_{l}
extraction, and a competitive
\alpha_{s}(M_{Z})

determination.

.29.5
Alternative MC generators

The single available Pythia 6 MC sample, generated for 1994 peak-energy
conditions only, limits the assessment of MC model dependence.
Generating events with modern generators (Pythia 8, Herwig 7, Sherpa) at
all LEP1 scan energies would enable proper evaluation of hadronisation
model uncertainties and energy-dependent acceptance corrections.

.30
Circular-Luminosity Cross-Check

As a cross-check, the lineshape fit was also performed using
back-calculated luminosities derived from event counts and SM
cross-sections, as in the original Phase 4c analysis. This procedure is
circular: since

\mathcal{L}=N_{\text{sel}}(1-f_{\text{bg}})/(\epsilon\cdot\sigma_{\text{theory}})
,
the measured cross-section
\sigma_{\text{meas}}=\sigma_{\text{theory}}

at every energy point, and the fit recovers the input ALEPH parameters
by construction with
\chi^{2}=0
.

The back-calculated luminosity results are:

calibrated measurement. The zero pulls versus ALEPH are algebraic
consequences of the circular derivation. This cross-check validates that
the analysis infrastructure correctly processes the data and that the
fit code converges.

Machine-Readable Results

The complete machine-readable results are stored in JSON format in the

phase5_documentation/outputs/results/
directory:

•

phase4a_results.json
: Expected results from MC pseudo-data
(fit parameters, covariance, closure pulls, systematics, toys, derived
quantities).

•

phase4c_results.json
: Full data calibrated results (fit
parameters with stat+syst, covariance matrix, scipy cross-check,
aftercut calibration per-point values, comparisons to published
ALEPH/LEP with pulls, per-year consistency, viability checks, derived
quantities, energy points used with aftercut corrections).

•

phase4c_calibrated_results.json
: Duplicate of the above for
compatibility.

•

phase4c_published_lumi_results.json
: Intermediate results
using published luminosities without aftercut correction (chi2/ndf =
215, documenting the uncorrected fit).

•

systematic_shifts.json
: Per-systematic impact on each
parameter (full data), including raw up/down shifts and symmetrised
values.

•

cross_sections.json
: Measured cross-sections per energy
point (full data) with event counts, luminosities, and statistical
uncertainties.

•

derived_quantities.json
: Derived quantities (
N_{\nu}
,

\alpha_{s}
) with full uncertainty breakdown.

All JSON structures follow the schema documented in the respective
inference artifacts.

Pre-Selection Data/MC Comparisons

Pre-selection data/MC comparisons from Phase 2, using 1994 P2 data
versus all MC normalised to the data integral, are shown below. These
demonstrate the level of agreement between data and the Pythia 6 MC
before the hadronic selection is applied.

The
\chi^{2}
/ndf values range from 1.71 (
\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}
,
excellent agreement) to 4165 (hemisphere mass, very poor agreement). The
variables most relevant to the hadronic selection
(
\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}
and missing momentum) show the best
agreement, while fragmentation-sensitive variables (hemisphere mass,
neutral energy fraction) show the expected tensions from Pythia 6
modelling limitations at high statistics.

Figure 111:
Pre-selection data/MC comparison for charged multiplicity. The
Pythia 6 MC reproduces the overall shape of the multiplicity
distribution, with 2–5% deviations in the tails that are
characteristic of known hadronisation modelling
differences.

Figure 112:
Pre-selection data/MC comparison for visible energy. The MC
peak position is slightly shifted relative to data, reflecting
calorimeter response modelling differences in the archived MC. The
overall normalisation and shape are adequate for efficiency
estimation.

Figure 113:
Pre-selection data/MC comparison for thrust. Moderate tension
(
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 6.67) reflects event shape modelling differences.
Thrust is not used in the hadronic selection and does not affect the
cross-section measurement.

Figure 114:
Pre-selection data/MC comparison for missing momentum.
Acceptable agreement (
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 3.66) validates the MC modelling
of the missing momentum distribution, which is relevant for the
passesAll cut.

Figure 115:
Pre-selection data/MC comparison for neutral energy fraction.
The significant tension (
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 41.86) reflects known neutral
cluster reconstruction differences between data and MC in the archived
samples.

Figure 116:
Pre-selection data/MC comparison for leading track momentum.
The MC shows moderate tension (
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 8.90) in the
high-momentum tail, reflecting fragmentation modelling
differences.

Figure 117:
Pre-selection data/MC comparison for
\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}
.
Excellent agreement (
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 1.71) confirms reliable MC
modelling of the geometric acceptance distribution, which is the most
important variable for the efficiency
determination.

Figure 118:
Pre-selection data/MC comparison for sphericity. Significant
tension (
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 6.34) reflects event shape modelling
differences. Sphericity is not used directly in the cross-section
extraction.

Figure 119:
Pre-selection data/MC comparison for acollinearity. Poor
agreement (
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 11.33) reflects back-to-back topology
modelling differences. This variable is not used in the hadronic
selection.

Figure 120:
Pre-selection data/MC comparison for hemisphere mass. Very poor
agreement (
\chi^{2}
/ndf > 4000) reflects the sensitivity
of this proxy variable (hemisphere defined by leading particle, not
thrust axis) to jet fragmentation details in the MC. This variable is
not used in the analysis.

The variable ranking from Phase 2, based on the data/MC
\chi^{2}
/ndf
metric across all candidate selection variables, is shown in
Figure
121
. This ranking informed the selection
design in Phase 3 by identifying which variables have reliable MC
modelling (low
\chi^{2}
/ndf) and which suffer from known Pythia 6
limitations.

Figure 121:

\chi^{2}

\chi^{2}

(

,
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 1.71) and missing momentum
(

.36.3
Systematic covariance

The systematic covariance is computed from the per-source shifts

\delta_{k}
using

V^{\text{syst}}_{ij}=\sum_{k}\delta_{ki}\,\delta_{kj}
, where the sum
runs over all systematic sources including the aftercut calibration. The
per-source shifts are tabulated in the machine-readable file

results/systematic_shifts.json
. The total systematic
uncertainties are:
\delta M_{Z}^{\text{syst}}=3.59
MeV,

\delta\Gamma_{Z}^{\text{syst}}=5.18
MeV,

\delta\sigma^{0}_{\text{had,syst}}=0.449
nb. These are obtained by
adding the individual per-source contributions in quadrature
(
81
).

Lund Jet Plane Measurement with ALEPH Data

.37
Introduction

The Lund jet plane is a two-dimensional representation of the radiation
pattern inside jets, mapping each emission along the primary
Cambridge/Aachen (C/A) declustering chain to coordinates that reflect
the emission’s transverse momentum scale and angular separation. First
proposed by Dreyer, Salam, and Soyez
(
[
Dreyer:2018nbf
]
), the Lund plane
provides direct, theoretically motivated access to the perturbative QCD
splitting functions and the transition to non-perturbative dynamics. The
primary Lund plane density has been calculated to next-to-leading
logarithmic (NLL) accuracy including running-coupling and two-loop
effects (
[
Lifson:2020gua
]
), enabling
quantitative comparisons between data and first-principles QCD
predictions.

The ATLAS Collaboration (
[
1
]
) and CMS Collaboration
(
[
CMS:2024mlf
]
) have measured the
Lund plane density in proton-proton collisions at
\sqrt{s}=13
TeV
using high-transverse-momentum anti-
k_{t}
jets. However, no
measurement of the Lund plane density exists in electron-positron
collisions. This analysis presents the first such measurement, using
hadronic Z decay events recorded by the ALEPH detector at LEP at

\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV.

The
e^{+}e^{-}
environment offers several unique advantages for studying
QCD radiation patterns. The initial state is a colour singlet with
precisely known centre-of-mass energy, free from pileup and underlying
event contamination that complicate measurements at hadron colliders.
The quark-antiquark pair from
Z\to q\bar{q}
decay provides a
theoretically clean starting point for QCD showering, with direct
sensitivity to the
C_{F}
colour factor through the
q\to qg

splitting kernel. Unlike proton-proton collisions where gluon-initiated
jets contribute substantially, the hemispheres in
e^{+}e^{-}
Z decays
are almost exclusively quark-initiated, offering a complementary probe
of the Lund plane structure.

The closest precursors at LEP are the DELPHI measurements of
differential splitting rates for quark and gluon jets
(
[
DELPHI:2004splitting
]
,

[
DELPHI:2004splittingb
]
), which are effectively
one-dimensional projections of the Lund plane, and the ALEPH subjet
structure studies (
[
ALEPH:subjet
]
) using the
Durham algorithm. This analysis extends these measurements to the full
two-dimensional Lund plane, providing substantially more information
about the shower structure and enabling direct comparison with the NLL
analytic calculation of (
[
Lifson:2020gua
]
). Such a comparison in the perturbative region
(
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})\gtrsim 0
) would constitute the first

e^{+}e^{-}
test of the NLL Lund plane prediction.

.37.1
Observable definition

The measured observable is the primary Lund jet plane density:

{\rho(\ln(1/\Delta\theta),\,\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV}))=\frac{1}{N_{\text{hemi}}}\frac{d^{2}N_{\text{splittings}}}{d\ln(1/\Delta\theta)\,d\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})}}

(28)

where the sum runs over all primary splittings in all thrust
hemispheres, and
N_{\text{hemi}}
is the total number of hemispheres
(twice the number of events passing selection). The Lund plane
coordinates are defined through the C/A declustering procedure described
in Section
.40
.

For each C/A declustering step along the primary (harder) branch, the
coordinates are:

•

Opening angle:

\Delta\theta=\arccos(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot\hat{p}_{2})
, where

\hat{p}_{i}
are the unit three-momentum vectors of the two subjets.

•

Transverse momentum of the splitting:

k_{t}=|\vec{p}_{\text{soft}}|\cdot\Delta\theta
, where

\vec{p}_{\text{soft}}
is the three-momentum of the softer subjet
(defined by smaller
|\vec{p}|
). This uses the small-angle
approximation
\sin(\Delta\theta)\approx\Delta\theta
, which is
the standard convention in the Lund plane literature
(
[
Dreyer:2018nbf
]
) and is what the
FastJet (
[
Cacciari:2011ma
]
) Lund
declustering computes natively.

The Lund plane coordinates are
x=\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
and

y=\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})
. The “harder” subjet (followed at each
declustering step) is defined by larger
|\vec{p}|
; in the rare case
of exactly equal momenta, the subjet with more constituents is followed.

.37.2
Adaptation for e+e- collisions

The Lund plane formalism was originally developed in the context of
hadron-collider jets defined by the anti-
k_{t}
algorithm with radius
parameter
R
, where the angular coordinate is
\ln(R/\Delta R)
. In

e^{+}e^{-}
collisions, the adaptation is as follows. “Jets” are
replaced by thrust hemispheres, defined by splitting the event along the
thrust axis computed from charged particles. The angular coordinate
becomes
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
, where
\Delta\theta
is the
three-dimensional opening angle between subjets. The transverse momentum
uses total momentum
|\vec{p}|
rather than transverse momentum

p_{T}
relative to the beam axis, since in
e^{+}e^{-}
the natural
momentum variable is the total three-momentum. The C/A clustering uses
the
e^{+}e^{-}
angular distance metric

d_{ij}=2(1-\cos\theta_{ij})
via FastJet’s

ee_genkt_algorithm
with
p=0
, rather than the
hadron-collider metric
\Delta R^{2}=\Delta y^{2}+\Delta\phi^{2}
. This
adaptation is consistent with the theoretical framework of
(
[
Dreyer:2018nbf
]
), which defines the

e^{+}e^{-}
Lund plane in these coordinates.

.38
Data Samples

.38.1
The ALEPH detector

The ALEPH detector (
[
ALEPH:1995detector
]
) operated at the LEP electron-positron collider at
CERN from 1989 to 2000. The central tracking system consists of a
silicon vertex detector (VDET), an inner tracking chamber (ITC), and a
large time projection chamber (TPC), all immersed in a 1.5 T axial
magnetic field from a superconducting solenoid of 5.3 m diameter. The
TPC, with inner radius 0.33 m and outer radius 1.77 m, provides up to 21
space points per track over an active length of
2\times 2.2
m. The
combined tracking system achieves a momentum resolution of

\sigma(1/p_{T})=6\times 10^{-4}
(GeV/
c
)
-1
for
high-momentum tracks at 45 GeV/
c

(
[
ALEPH:colorfactors
]
), with the
TPC alone providing
\sigma(1/p_{T})=1.2\times 10^{-3}

(GeV/
c
)
-1
(
[
ALEPH:EEC
]
). The
angular acceptance of the TPC covers approximately

|\cos\theta|<0.95
, corresponding to polar angles between
approximately 18 and 162 degrees.

The archived data used in this analysis were processed and validated in
the context of a search for collective effects in
e^{+}e^{-}
collisions
(
[
Badea:2019qgp
]
), which uses the same data
format, event selection flags, and track quality definitions employed
here.

.38.2
Data samples

The data were recorded by ALEPH during LEP1 running at the Z pole
(
\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV) from 1992 to 1995. The data are stored in ROOT
format with a common tree structure. Table
107

summarises the data samples.

Table 107:

corresponding to different detector configurations, including a VDET
upgrade mid-year.

.38.3
Monte Carlo samples

The Monte Carlo simulation uses Pythia 6
(
[
Sjostrand:2006za
]
) as the event
generator with the standard ALEPH detector simulation. The MC sample
consists of 40 files containing a total of 771,597 events, corresponding
to the 1994 detector configuration. Each file contains two trees:

t
(reconstructed level, with the same branch structure as data)
and
tgen
(generator level, with additional truth-level
information). The trees have one-to-one event correspondence, enabling
event-by-event matching for response matrix construction.

Table 108:
Monte Carlo sample summary. Only one generator is available in
the archived dataset, which is the most significant limitation of this
analysis (Limitation L1).

Generator

N_{\text{gen}}

Notes

Z\to q\bar{q}
(hadronic)

Pythia 6

771,597

1994 config, full
ALEPH sim

The data-to-MC event ratio is 3,050,610 / 771,597 = 3.95. While data
statistics are ample, the MC statistics are the limiting factor for
response matrix precision and systematic evaluation. The MC energy is
91.20 GeV, consistent with the data energy of 91.28 GeV; this minor
difference (0.09%) has negligible impact on the Z-pole kinematics.

A single generator (Pythia 6) is available for detector-level
correction. This is a significant limitation (L1) that prevents the
standard multi-generator evaluation of model dependence at detector
level. The mitigation strategy is described in Section
Section
.42.2
.

.39
Event Selection

The event selection follows the standard ALEPH hadronic event selection
(
[
ALEPH:hadronic
]
;

[
Badea:2019qgp
]
), supplemented by a thrust
requirement to ensure well-defined hemisphere topology. Two
qualitatively different track selection approaches were evaluated; the
primary approach uses the pre-computed ALEPH quality flags, while the
alternative uses explicit cuts on raw tracking variables. The
alternative approach serves as a systematic cross-check (Section
Section
.42.1
).

.39.1
Event-level selection

Events are required to satisfy the following criteria:

1.

Standard hadronic selection
(
passesAll
): This
pre-computed flag requires at least five good charged tracks, total
charged energy exceeding 15 GeV (approximately 33% of

\sqrt{s}/2
), sphericity polar angle

|\cos\theta_{\text{sph}}|<0.82
, total missing momentum below 20
GeV/
c
, and no hard initial-state radiation (ISR)
(
[
ALEPH:hadronic
]
). These
requirements efficiently select
Z\to q\bar{q}
events while
rejecting
\tau^{+}\tau^{-}
, two-photon, and beam-gas backgrounds to
below 0.3%.

2.

Thrust requirement
(
T_{\text{charged}}>0.7
): The
charged-particle thrust must exceed 0.7, ensuring a well-defined
thrust axis and unambiguous hemisphere assignment. Events with very
low thrust (
T<0.7
) have nearly spherical topology where the
hemisphere definition becomes unstable. This cut removes only 0.5% of
events beyond
passesAll
.

3.

Minimum track multiplicity
(
\geq 2
selected tracks): At
least two tracks must pass the track-level selection. This is
automatically satisfied by all events passing the above criteria.

.39.2
Track selection: Approach A (primary)

The primary track selection uses the pre-computed ALEPH quality flags:

•

pwflag == 0
: Identifies charged particle-flow tracks,
excluding photon conversions, neutral hadron deposits, and
calorimeter-only objects.

•

highPurity == 1
: Requires high-quality track
reconstruction, encoding composite requirements on the number of TPC
hits, impact parameters, and angular acceptance that go beyond the
individually published cuts.

•

p>0.2
GeV/
c
: Minimum track momentum, the standard ALEPH
threshold below which tracking resolution degrades significantly
(
\sigma(1/p)\propto 1/p
) and spurious splittings can arise.

•

Charge
\neq 0
: Selects charged particles only.

These flags encode the standard ALEPH track quality criteria: at least 4
TPC coordinates, transverse impact parameter
|d_{0}|<2
cm,
longitudinal impact parameter
|z_{0}|<10
cm, and angular acceptance

|\cos\theta|<0.95
(
[
ALEPH:hadronic
]
). The
highPurity
flag additionally encodes
requirements not fully documented in the public metadata, as
demonstrated by the selection comparison in Section
Section
.39.4
.

The average number of selected tracks per event is 17.4 for both data
and MC, indicating excellent modelling of the track multiplicity.

.39.3
Track selection: Approach B
(cross-check)

As a qualitatively different alternative, explicit cuts on the raw
tracking variables are applied:

•

Number of TPC coordinates
n_{\text{TPC}}\geq 4

•

Transverse impact parameter
|d_{0}|<2.0
cm

•

Longitudinal impact parameter
|z_{0}|<10.0
cm

•

Angular acceptance
|\cos\theta|<0.95

•

p>0.2
GeV/
c

•

Charge
\neq 0

This approach bypasses the pre-computed flags entirely and applies the
published ALEPH track quality criteria directly. It serves as a
cross-check that the pre-computed flags encode what is assumed and
reveals any additional hidden requirements.

.39.4
Selection comparison

The two track selection approaches were compared on 50,000 data events.
The results are summarised in Table
109
.

pass A but not B, while 50,614 tracks (5.8%) pass B but not

Approach A is a near-strict subset of Approach B, with only 11 tracks in
A not in B. Approach B selects 50,614 additional tracks (5.8%) that
pass the raw quality cuts but fail
highPurity
, demonstrating
that the
highPurity
flag encodes requirements beyond the
published track quality criteria. The extra tracks in Approach B are
predominantly low-momentum tracks that contribute additional soft
splittings in the Lund plane. The Lund plane density from the two
approaches was compared on 10,000 events, with Approach B producing
approximately 5% more splittings per hemisphere (5.29 vs 5.03).

Approach A is chosen as the primary selection because it uses the
validated ALEPH pre-computed flags encoding the full track quality
criteria, while Approach B’s additional tracks have lower quality and
could introduce systematic effects. The difference between the two
approaches is assigned as the tracking efficiency systematic uncertainty
(Section
.42.1
).

,

\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})\sim-1
to

, corresponding to the

) but with slightly higher density throughout,

non-

where statistics are limited and the extra soft tracks from Approach B
have the largest relative impact.

. The data and MC efficiencies agree at each stage

.39.6
Generator-level fiducial selection

At generator level, the fiducial selection for the particle-level result
is:

•

T_{\text{charged}}>0.7
(thrust computed from stable charged
particles; the
passesAll
flag is not defined at generator
level)

•

Track selection:
pwflag == 0
, charge
\neq 0
,

p>0.2
GeV/
c
(the
highPurity
flag is a
reconstruction-level quality indicator and is not applied at generator
level)

The particle-level definition includes all stable charged particles
(
c\tau>10
mm) from the
Z\to q\bar{q}
decay chain:

\pi^{\pm}
,
K^{\pm}
,
p/\bar{p}
,
e^{\pm}
,
\mu^{\pm}
, and
charged decay products of
K_{S}^{0}
and
\Lambda/\bar{\Lambda}
. ISR
photon conversions are excluded. The same hemisphere splitting and C/A
declustering procedure as at reconstruction level is applied.

.39.7
Data/MC comparison of selection
variables

Before constructing the response matrix and applying corrections, the
data/MC agreement was validated for all kinematic variables entering the
event selection and observable calculation. These comparisons use the
full data sample and the MC sample normalised to the data event count
(scale factor 3.95), after applying the event and track selection.

The charged thrust distribution (Figure
125
) shows
excellent agreement between data and MC across the entire range from

T=0.7
to
T\approx 1
, with the data/MC ratio consistent with
unity within approximately 2%. The distribution peaks strongly near

T=1
(pencil-like, two-jet events) with a long tail toward lower
thrust values corresponding to multi-jet topologies. This variable
directly enters the event selection through the
T>0.7
requirement
and defines the hemisphere axis used for the Lund plane construction.

Figure 125:
Charged thrust distribution comparing data (points) and MC
(histogram). The data/MC ratio in the lower panel is consistent with
unity within approximately 2% across the full thrust range, confirming
excellent modelling of the event topology.

The charged hadron multiplicity (Figure
126
) is
well-modelled, with a mean of approximately 18 tracks per event in both
data and MC. The ratio is flat near unity across the full range from 5
to 40 tracks. This variable determines the number of splittings
available per hemisphere and the statistical population of the Lund
plane.

Figure 126:
Distribution of the number of high-purity charged hadrons per
event for data and MC. Agreement is excellent across the full
multiplicity range, with the ratio flat near unity. The mean
multiplicity is approximately 18 for both samples.

The track momentum distribution (Figure
127
) spans five
orders of magnitude from the 0.2 GeV/
c
threshold to the kinematic
limit near 45 GeV/
c
, with data/MC agreement within 2–3%
throughout. A slight data excess at very low momenta (
p<0.3

GeV/
c
) is visible but small. This variable enters the Lund plane
construction through the
k_{t}
definition.

Figure 127:
Track momentum (
p
) distribution on a logarithmic

y
-axis. Data and MC agree across five orders of magnitude, with the
ratio consistent with unity within 2–3%. A slight excess in data at
very low momenta is visible but does not impact the
analysis.

The track transverse momentum (Figure
128
) shows similarly
excellent agreement over the full range.

Figure 128:
Track transverse momentum (
p_{T}
) distribution for data and
MC. Excellent agreement is observed over the full kinematic range, with
the data/MC ratio consistent with unity within statistical
uncertainties.

The track polar angle distribution (Figure
129
) shows
the expected sinusoidal shape reflecting the cylindrical detector
geometry. A slight data deficit relative to MC is visible at very
forward and backward angles (
\theta<0.4
or
\theta>2.7
rad),
corresponding to the edges of the TPC acceptance where tracking
efficiency drops. This angular variable directly affects the opening
angle
\Delta\theta
in the Lund plane coordinates.

Figure 129:
Track polar angle (
\theta
) distribution. The sinusoidal
shape reflects the detector acceptance. Data and MC agree well overall,
with a slight data deficit at the extreme forward and backward angles
reflecting the edges of the TPC coverage.

The track azimuthal angle (Figure
130
) is flat within
statistical fluctuations for both data and MC, confirming uniform
azimuthal acceptance as expected for a cylindrically symmetric detector.

Figure 130:
Track azimuthal angle (
\phi
) distribution. Both data and MC
are flat across the full range, confirming uniform azimuthal acceptance.
The data/MC ratio is consistent with unity throughout.

In summary, data/MC agreement is excellent across all selection and
kinematic variables. The ratios are consistent with unity within 2–3%
for all distributions, with no significant systematic trends. This level
of agreement provides confidence that the MC simulation accurately
describes the data and is suitable for constructing the correction
procedure.

.40
C/A Declustering and Lund Plane
Construction

The construction of the Lund plane proceeds in three steps: hemisphere
definition, Cambridge/Aachen clustering, and primary Lund declustering.

.40.1
Hemisphere definition

Each event is split into two hemispheres along the thrust axis. The
thrust axis direction is taken from the pre-stored charged-particle
thrust variables (
TTheta_charged
,
TPhi_charged
) in
the data files. Each selected charged track is assigned to the
hemisphere containing it based on the sign of the dot product of the
track momentum with the thrust axis unit vector:

\text{Hemisphere}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\vec{p}\cdot\hat{n}_{\text{thrust}}>0\\
2&\text{if }\vec{p}\cdot\hat{n}_{\text{thrust}}\leq 0\end{cases}

For MC events, hemispheres are defined independently at both
reconstruction and generator level using the respective thrust axes. The
reco hemisphere is matched to the gen hemisphere on the same side of the
gen-level thrust axis by computing the dot product of each reco
hemisphere’s momentum sum with the gen-level thrust axis. Events where
the reco and gen thrust axes differ by more than
\pi/4
are flagged
as ambiguous; this occurs in only 0.05% of events (82 out of 181,191 in
the Phase 3 MC processing) and these events are excluded from the
response matrix.

.40.2
Cambridge/Aachen clustering

Each hemisphere’s selected charged tracks are reclustered using the
Cambridge/Aachen algorithm implemented in FastJet
(
[
Cacciari:2011ma
]
) via the Python
interface. The
e^{+}e^{-}
variant is used, with the angular distance
metric
d_{ij}=2(1-\cos\theta_{ij})
, accessed through FastJet’s

ee_genkt_algorithm
with the generalised-
k_{t}
exponent

p=0
and jet radius
R=\pi
. This metric is the natural choice
for
e^{+}e^{-}
collisions, where the full angular range is accessible
and rapidity-azimuth coordinates are not appropriate
(
[
Dokshitzer:1991wu
]
;

[
Wobisch:1998wt
]
).

.40.3
Primary Lund declustering

The primary Lund plane is extracted using FastJet’s

exclusive_jets_lund_declusterings(1)
method, which
iteratively declusters the C/A tree by following the harder branch at
each step. At each declustering step, the method records the opening
angle
\Delta\theta
and transverse momentum

k_{t}=|\vec{p}_{\text{soft}}|\cdot\Delta\theta
of the splitting. The
Lund plane coordinates
(\ln(1/\Delta\theta),\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV}))

are computed for each splitting.

The average number of primary splittings per hemisphere is 5.0 at
reconstruction level and 5.5 at generator level (the difference reflects
tracking inefficiency reducing the number of resolved splittings). Of
all reconstruction-level splittings, approximately 84% fall within the
analysis binning range of
0\leq\ln(1/\Delta\theta)\leq 6
and

-3.5\leq\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})\leq 2.5
. Splittings outside this
range (predominantly at very large angles,
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)<0
)
are not included in the measurement.

.40.4
Prototype Lund plane

The prototype Lund plane was validated on 1,000 data events (951 passing
selection) before proceeding to full-statistics processing. Figure
Figure
131
shows the resulting two-dimensional density at
reconstruction level. The expected triangular structure is clearly
visible, with the kinematic boundary running diagonally from the
upper-left to the lower-right corner. The highest density appears at
moderate angles and low to moderate
k_{t}

(
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)\sim 1\text{--}2
,

\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})\sim-1
to
0
), corresponding to the
transition between the perturbative and non-perturbative regimes.

Figure 131:
Prototype reconstruction-level primary Lund plane density from
951 data events (1,889 hemispheres, 9,410 splittings). The
characteristic triangular structure with the kinematic boundary along
the diagonal is clearly visible. The plane extends to large angles
(small
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
) as expected for
e^{+}e^{-}
where
hemispheres cover approximately
\pi/2
in
angle.

Figure 132:
Prototype generator-level primary Lund plane density from 985
MC events (1,966 hemispheres, 10,882 splittings). The structure is very
similar to the reconstruction-level plane, with slightly more splittings
per hemisphere (5.5 vs 5.0) reflecting the broader acceptance at
generator level. The overall shape consistency between reco and gen
levels is encouraging for the unfolding step.

The generator-level prototype (Figure
132
) shows a
very similar structure to the reconstruction level, with the expected
slight increase in splitting multiplicity due to the absence of tracking
inefficiency. The qualitative agreement between the two levels confirms
that the detector effects to be corrected are moderate and
well-localised in the Lund plane.

.40.5
Full-statistics reconstruction-level Lund
plane

The full Phase 3 processing produced reconstruction-level Lund planes
from 169,412 data events and 181,191 MC events (a subset of the full
samples for Phase 3 validation; the full statistics are used in the
observed result, Section
.43
). The data and MC
reconstruction-level Lund planes are shown in Figures
Figure
133
and Figure
134
, with their ratio in
Figure
135
.

Figure 133:
Reconstruction-level primary Lund plane density from 169,412
data events. The characteristic triangular structure is clearly resolved
with high statistics, showing the full kinematic range accessible at

\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV.

Figure 134:
Reconstruction-level primary Lund plane density from 181,191 MC
events. The structure is very similar to data, confirming that the
Pythia 6 Monte Carlo provides an accurate description of the
reconstruction-level Lund plane.

Figure 135:
Ratio of data to MC reconstruction-level Lund plane density.
The ratio is within 10–20% of unity across most of the populated
region, with larger deviations at the kinematic boundaries where
statistics are limited. This level of agreement supports the use of the
MC for constructing the response matrix and efficiency
corrections.

The one-dimensional projections (Figures
136
and
Figure
137
) provide a more quantitative view of the data/MC
agreement. Both projections show agreement within a few percent in the
densely populated regions.

Figure 136:
Reconstruction-level projection of the Lund plane density onto
the
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
axis. Data points with statistical
uncertainties are compared to the MC histogram. Good agreement is
observed across the full range, with the data/MC ratio consistent with
unity within a few percent.

Figure 137:
Reconstruction-level projection onto the

\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})

the populated region. The distribution peaks near

\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})\approx-0.5
and falls off steeply at high

k_{t}
, reflecting the perturbative suppression of hard
emissions.

.41
Detector Corrections

The reconstruction-level Lund plane density is corrected to the
charged-particle-level fiducial phase space defined in Section
Section
.39.6
using iterative Bayesian unfolding (IBU)
(
[
DAgostini:1994fjx
]
) as the primary
method, with bin-by-bin (BBB) correction as a cross-check. This section
describes the response matrix construction, the unfolding procedure, and
the validation tests.

.41.1
Response matrix

\subsubsubsection

Splitting matching

Reconstruction-level and generator-level primary Lund splittings are
matched by proximity in Lund coordinates: for each reconstruction-level
splitting, the closest generator-level splitting in

(\ln(1/\Delta\theta),\ln(k_{t}))
space is identified within a maximum
distance threshold of 1.5 units. Greedy matching (closest pairs matched
first) is used with no double-matching. This approach follows the
recommendation of (
[
1
]
) and the conventions for unfolding measurements with
variable-multiplicity observables, where index-based matching (first
reco splitting to first gen splitting) can produce artificially poor
response matrices.

The matching performance on the Phase 3 MC sample (181,191 events) is
summarised in Table
111
.

Table 111:
Splitting matching performance. The matching rate is 94.7% for
reconstruction-level and 86.6% for generator-level splittings.
Unmatched reco splittings are treated as fakes; unmatched gen splittings
enter the efficiency correction.

Metric

Value

Reco hemispheres

360,555

Gen hemispheres

360,555

Total reco splittings

1,840,755

Total gen splittings

2,013,566

Matched reco-gen pairs

1,744,040

Matching rate (reco)

94.7%

Matching rate (gen)

86.6%

Unmatched reco (fakes)

96,715 (5.3%)

Unmatched gen (misses)

269,526 (13.4%)

The 5.3% fake rate at reconstruction level is small and concentrated at
the kinematic boundaries. The 13.4% miss rate at generator level
reflects tracking inefficiency and resolution effects that merge or lose
soft splittings at reconstruction level.

\subsubsubsection

Binning

The response matrix uses a
12\times 12
binning:

•

\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
: 12 uniform bins from 0 to 6

•

\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})
: 12 uniform bins from
-3.5
to
2.5

This gives 144 total bins in the flattened one-dimensional
representation used for unfolding. The bin width of 0.5 in each
coordinate is chosen to balance resolution (the detector smearing is
smaller than the bin width in the densely populated central region)
against statistical population per bin in the MC sample.

\subsubsubsection

Response matrix properties

The response matrix, normalised per generator-level bin to show
migration probabilities, is shown in Figure
138
.
Table
112
summarises its key properties.

Table 112:
Response matrix properties. The high diagonal fraction and
moderate condition number indicate a well-conditioned problem suitable
for iterative unfolding.

Property

Value

Diagonal fraction

78.6%

Condition number (normalised)

101

Mean efficiency (populated bins)

80.5%

Mean per-bin diagonal fraction

74.9%

The diagonal fraction of 78.6% indicates that the majority of
splittings remain in their true bin after detector effects. This is
substantially better than the 49% obtained with index-based matching in
an early cross-check, confirming the importance of angular matching for
this variable-multiplicity observable. The condition number of 101 is
moderate, indicating that the matrix inversion underlying IBU is
numerically stable.

Figure 138:
Response matrix normalised per generator-level bin, showing
migration probabilities. The strong diagonal structure confirms that
detector effects cause limited migration between bins, with off-diagonal
entries concentrated near the diagonal. The matrix is displayed as a

144\times 144
grid corresponding to the flattened
12\times 12

2D binning.

The diagonal fraction as a function of position in the Lund plane
(Figure
139
) shows that the response is best in the
central, densely populated region (diagonal fraction exceeding 80%) and
degrades at the kinematic boundaries.

Figure 139:
Diagonal fraction per 2D bin across the Lund plane. The
diagonal fraction exceeds 60% in the densely populated central region
and drops at the edges where statistics and detector resolution are
limited. Bins with very low diagonal fraction correspond to the
kinematic boundaries where efficiency also drops.

The matching efficiency (Figure
140
) shows a similar
pattern, with efficiency above 80% in the central region and dropping
below 50% at the kinematic boundaries (very large angles or very low

k_{t}
).

Figure 140:
Matching efficiency as a function of position in the Lund
plane. Efficiency exceeds 80% in the central region and drops below
50% at the kinematic boundaries. Bins with efficiency below 10% are
excluded from the quality gate in the closure test and will receive
enlarged uncertainties in the final result.

.41.2
Iterative Bayesian unfolding

The primary correction method is iterative Bayesian unfolding (IBU)
(
[
DAgostini:1994fjx
]
), also known as the
D’Agostini method. The procedure iteratively updates the estimated true
distribution using Bayes’ theorem, with the response matrix providing
the likelihood of observing a given reconstruction-level bin given a
true bin. The MC generator-level distribution serves as the initial
prior.

After unfolding, a bin-by-bin efficiency correction is applied:

\rho_{\text{corrected}}(x,y)=\frac{\rho_{\text{unfolded}}(x,y)}{\epsilon(x,y)}

where the efficiency

\epsilon(x,y)=N_{\text{gen,matched}}(x,y)/N_{\text{gen}}(x,y)

corrects for generator-level splittings that are not reconstructed. The
result is then normalised per hemisphere.

The number of iterations is optimised using the closure test (Section
Section
.41.3
). The nominal choice is 32 iterations, which is
higher than the 4 iterations used by ATLAS
(
[
1
]
) and the 8–12
used by CMS (
[
CMS:2024mlf
]
). The
increased iteration count is needed because the flattened
two-dimensional problem (144 bins) has slower convergence than the
one-dimensional problems considered in those analyses.

.41.3
Closure test

The closure test applies the full unfolding chain (IBU with the MC truth
prior, followed by efficiency correction) to MC reconstruction-level
data and compares the result to the MC generator-level truth. This tests
the internal consistency of the correction procedure. The comparison
uses a quality gate requiring generator-level bin content exceeding 100
entries and efficiency above 10%, which selects 84 of the 144 bins.

Table 113:
Closure test results as a function of IBU iteration count. The
test passes at 32 iterations with
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=0.88
and

p=0.77
, well above the required threshold of

p>0.05
.

.

A flat prior (uniform across all bins) was also tested. It does not
converge within 64 iterations, with
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=112
at 64
iterations. This quantifies the prior dependence, which is assigned as a
component of the unfolding systematic uncertainty (Section
Section
.42.4
).

.41.4
Stress test

The stress test evaluates the unfolding procedure’s ability to recover a
modified truth distribution. The generator-level Lund plane is
reweighted by linear tilts of 5%, 10%, 20%, and 50% in each
coordinate independently. The correspondingly reweighted
reconstruction-level distribution is unfolded with the original
(un-reweighted) MC prior at 32 iterations and compared to the reweighted
truth.

\subsubsubsection

Baseline results

The baseline stress test shows large absolute
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}

values (exceeding 3,600) and a mean absolute deviation of approximately
36% for all tilt configurations. Crucially, the deviation is nearly
independent of the tilt magnitude, indicating that the dominant
contribution comes from the bin-by-bin efficiency correction baseline
bias in edge bins, not from failure to resolve the tilt.

Table 114:

The nearly constant deviation across tilt magnitudes indicates that the
dominant contribution is from the efficiency correction baseline, not
from failure to resolve the tilt.

\subsubsubsection

Remediation attempts

Three independent remediation approaches were attempted:

Remediation 1: Tighter efficiency gate (
\epsilon>50\%
, 79
bins).
Restricting the quality gate to bins with efficiency above 50%
reduces
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}
from approximately 4,500 to approximately
525 and the mean deviation from 36% to 18.6%. The improvement confirms
that the edge bins with low efficiency are the dominant source of bias,
but the test still does not pass (
p<0.05
).

Remediation 2: Coarser
8\times 8
binning (39 qualifying
bins).
Rebinning to
8\times 8
improves the response matrix
diagonal fraction to 83.6% but worsens
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}
to
approximately 4,900 because the larger bins still contain pathological
edge regions and the increased per-bin statistics make the residual bias
more significant.

Remediation 3: Boundary exclusion (62 interior bins).
Removing
the outermost ring of bins reduces
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}
to
approximately 850 and the mean deviation to 22%, but the test still
fails.

Summary of stress test remediation attempts. None achieves

p>0.05
in the absolute sense, but the progressive improvement with
tighter quality gates localises the bias to edge

\subsubsubsection

Relative tilt tracking diagnostic

A diagnostic test was performed to separate the baseline efficiency
correction bias from the tilt-tracking ability. The ratio of the tilted
unfolded result to the nominal unfolded result was compared to the ratio
of the tilted truth to the nominal truth. This relative comparison
cancels the common efficiency correction bias. The relative diagnostic
gives
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}<0.01
and mean deviation below 0.03% for
all tilt magnitudes and directions, demonstrating that the IBU correctly
resolves shape differences even though the absolute stress test fails.

The stress test failure is therefore entirely attributable to the
bin-by-bin efficiency correction baseline and is quantified as the
non-closure systematic (Section
.42.8
). For the
final result, bins with efficiency below 50% are assigned enlarged
uncertainties.

Figure 144:
Stress test results for tilts in the
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)

direction. Dashed lines show the tilted truth; markers show the unfolded
result. The offset between unfolded and truth is constant across tilt
magnitudes, confirming it originates from the efficiency correction
baseline rather than failure to track the tilt.

Figure 145:
Stress test results for tilts in the
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})

direction. The same pattern as Figure
144
is
observed: constant offset from efficiency correction, with the tilt
direction correctly resolved.

.42
Systematic Uncertainties

Nine sources of systematic uncertainty are evaluated. Each is propagated
through the full analysis chain: the varied input is passed through
event and track selection, hemisphere splitting, C/A clustering, Lund
plane construction, and unfolding (or BBB correction), and the resulting
shift relative to the nominal result is assigned as the systematic
uncertainty. All systematics show bin-dependent shifts; no flat shifts
were observed. Table
116
provides an overview
with the uncertainties evaluated relative to the observed full-data
density.

Table 116:

statistical vs 19.8% systematic). The two dominant sources — tracking
efficiency and MC model dependence — together account for
approximately 84% of the total systematic
variance.

The relative systematic shifts differ slightly from the Phase 4a values
(which were relative to the expected MC density). The tracking
efficiency systematic (9.7% vs 14.1% in 4a) and MC model systematic
(8.6% vs 9.6%) are smaller relative to data because the data density
is higher than the MC density in the regions where these systematics are
largest (low
k_{t}
).

.42.1
Tracking efficiency

The tracking efficiency systematic is the largest uncertainty in this
analysis, with a mean relative impact of 9.7%. It is evaluated by
comparing the nominal Lund plane density (Approach A, using the

highPurity
flag) to the alternative selection (Approach B,
which removes the
highPurity
requirement and applies explicit
raw-variable cuts). The Approach B reconstruction-level density is
corrected using bin-by-bin factors and compared to the nominal corrected
result. The full difference is assigned as a one-sided systematic
uncertainty.

The
highPurity
flag removal adds approximately 6% more tracks,
primarily at low momentum. These additional tracks preferentially
populate the low-
k_{t}
region of the Lund plane where the density is
steeply falling, amplifying the effect: a 6% increase in tracks
translates to a 9.7% mean shift in the corrected density. The shift is
largest at low
k_{t}
and moderate angles, as shown in Figure
Figure
146
.

This estimate is conservative compared to the tracking efficiency
systematics reported by ATLAS (1–3%)
(
[
1
]
) and CMS
(~1%) (
[
CMS:2024mlf
]
). Those analyses vary individual track reconstruction
efficiencies, while this analysis varies the entire track quality
definition by toggling the
highPurity
flag. The more aggressive
variation reflects the genuine uncertainty in the ALEPH track quality
definition for archived data where the full flag definition is not
publicly documented.

Figure 146:
Relative shift map for the tracking efficiency systematic
across the Lund plane. The shift is largest at low
k_{t}
(bottom of
the plane) where the extra non-
highPurity
tracks
contribute additional soft splittings. Typical shifts are 10–20% in
the central region and larger at the kinematic
boundaries.

.42.2
MC model dependence

The MC model dependence systematic accounts for the uncertainty in the
Pythia 6 Monte Carlo simulation used for the detector-level correction.
Since only one generator is available in the archived dataset
(Limitation L1), the evaluation relies on histogram-level reweighting
and JETSET parameter variations, which provide a lower bound on the true
model dependence. The mean relative impact is 8.6%.

Four variations are considered:

1.

Linear tilt of 10% in
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
:
The
generator-level Lund plane is reweighted by a linear function with a
10% slope across the angular coordinate. The response matrix is
recomputed from the reweighted MC, and the nominal
reconstruction-level distribution is unfolded with the modified
correction. Mean relative shift: 2.4%.

2.

Linear tilt of 10% in
\ln(k_{t})
:
Same procedure in the

k_{t}
coordinate. Mean relative shift: 2.4%.

3.

JETSET
\sigma_{q}
variation (PARJ(21), 11%):
The string
transverse momentum width is varied by reweighting in
\ln(k_{t})
by
11%, corresponding to the published uncertainty on

\sigma_{q}=0.36\pm 0.04
GeV (
[
ALEPH:hadronic
]
). Mean relative shift: 2.6%.

4.

JETSET angular parameter variation (PARJ(41), 40%):
The Lund
symmetric fragmentation function parameter
a
is varied by
reweighting in
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
by 40%, corresponding to the
uncertainty on
a=0.5\pm 0.2
. Mean relative shift: 9.4%.

The envelope of all four variations gives 8.6% relative to the observed
data density, dominated by the PARJ(41) angular parameter variation.
This parameter has a large relative uncertainty (40%) and directly
controls the angular distribution of emissions in the Lund fragmentation
model.

These reweighting variations are a lower bound on the true MC model
dependence, since one-dimensional reweighting cannot capture
multi-dimensional correlation differences between generators. The
standalone Pythia 8 (Monash tune) comparison presented in Section
Section
.44.3
confirms that the data-MC discrepancy is not
specific to the archived Pythia 6 tune: Pythia 8 also underestimates the
soft radiation rate, with the mean data/Pythia 8 ratio of 1.33 across
quality bins.

Figure 147:
Relative shift map for the MC model dependence (envelope of
four variations). The shift is largest at the edges of the populated
region and in the high-angle, low-
k_{t}
corner where the Lund
fragmentation parameters have the strongest
effect.

.42.3
Heavy flavour

The heavy-flavour systematic accounts for the different Lund plane
structure in hemispheres containing B-hadron decays compared to
light-quark hemispheres. At the Z pole,
Z\to b\bar{b}
accounts for
approximately 22% of hadronic decays. B-hadron decay products produce
splittings in the Lund plane that are physical but not QCD radiation,
and their modelling depends on the heavy-flavour decay tables in the
Monte Carlo. The mean relative impact is 1.5%.

The evaluation uses generator-level B-hadron PID tagging in MC:
hemispheres are tagged as b-enriched if the generator-level record
contains a B-hadron (PDG ID 500-599 or 5000-5999) among the particles in
that hemisphere. This provides 100% purity by construction, with a
b-tagged fraction of 8.3% (consistent with the expected

Z\to b\bar{b}
fraction after accounting for hemisphere splitting).
The systematic is assigned as
|(\rho_{b}-\rho_{l})|\times f_{b}
per
bin, where
\rho_{b}
and
\rho_{l}
are the gen-level Lund plane
densities in b-tagged and light-tagged hemispheres respectively, and

f_{b}
is the b-tagged fraction.

The shift is largest at low
k_{t}
where B-meson decay products
contribute additional soft splittings that are not QCD radiation, as
shown in Figure
148
.

Figure 148:
Relative shift map for the heavy-flavour systematic. Positive
shifts at low
k_{t}
and moderate angles indicate excess soft
splittings from B-hadron decays in b-enriched hemispheres. The effect is
concentrated in the non-perturbative region
(
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})<0
) where decay products contribute
significantly.

.42.4
Unfolding method

The unfolding method systematic combines three components into an
envelope with a mean relative impact of 3.6%.

The first component is the
IBU vs BBB comparison
(0.4%). The
difference between the primary IBU result at 32 iterations and the BBB
cross-check result is very small, indicating that the two methods agree
well for this well-conditioned response matrix (diagonal fraction
78.6%). This small difference provides confidence that the unfolding
result is not method-dependent.

The second component is the
prior dependence
(4.5%). The flat
prior gives a 4.5% mean shift relative to the MC truth prior at 32
iterations. A reweighted prior (20% tilt in
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
)
gives a negligible 0.01% shift. The flat prior fails to converge even
at 64 iterations, so the 4.5% shift at 32 iterations represents the
residual prior dependence and dominates this systematic.

The third component is the
iteration scan
(0.9%). The envelope
over 16, 24, 40, and 48 iterations (relative to the nominal 32) gives a
mean shift of 0.9%, confirming that the result is stable in the 24–48
iteration range.

Figure 149:
Relative shift map for the unfolding method (envelope of IBU vs
BBB, prior dependence, and iteration scan). The shift is concentrated in
edge bins where the response matrix has lower diagonal fraction and the
flat prior deviates most from the MC truth.

.42.5
Statistical uncertainty

The statistical uncertainty is evaluated using 500 Poisson bootstrap
toys. For each toy, the full-data reconstruction-level histogram is
Poisson-fluctuated and unfolded with IBU at 32 iterations using the MC
truth prior and efficiency correction. The covariance matrix is computed
from the ensemble of 500 unfolded results. The mean relative statistical
uncertainty is 0.8%, making it negligible compared to the systematic
uncertainties.

The statistical uncertainty decreased from 7.2% (10% data) to 0.8%
(full data), consistent with the factor of approximately 55 increase in
the number of splittings (28.9M vs 524k). The measurement is entirely
systematics-dominated.

.42.6
Event selection

The event selection systematic is evaluated by varying the thrust cut
from the nominal 0.70 to 0.65 and 0.75. The looser cut (0.65) admits
more spherical events where the hemisphere definition is less clean; the
tighter cut (0.75) restricts the sample to more collimated events. The
full analysis chain is re-run for each variation. The mean relative
impact is 0.7%.

Table 117:
Event selection systematic from thrust cut variation. The 0.75
cut has a larger effect because it removes more events than the looser
cut.

Cut

Mean relative shift

T>0.65

0.3%

T>0.75

0.8%

Envelope

0.7%

The thrust variation at 0.75 has a larger effect than 0.65 because it
removes more events and changes the sample composition, while 0.65
includes most of what 0.70 already selects. The shift pattern shows
structure at large angles where the thrust cut affects the hemisphere
population.

Figure 150:
Relative shift map for the event selection systematic (thrust
cut variation). The shift shows structure at large angles
(
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)<1
) where the thrust cut most affects the
hemisphere population. The overall impact is small (< 1%)
across most of the plane.

.42.7
Angular resolution

The angular resolution systematic accounts for the finite precision of
track direction measurements in the TPC, which directly affects the
opening angle
\Delta\theta
in the Lund plane coordinates. The TPC
achieves an angular resolution of approximately 1 mrad for high-momentum
tracks, degrading for low-momentum tracks due to multiple scattering.
The mean relative impact is 0.8%.

The evaluation smears track angles by the TPC angular resolution in MC
reconstruction-level events, re-runs the hemisphere splitting and C/A
declustering, and compares the resulting Lund plane density to the
nominal. The shift is largest at large angles
(
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)<1
) where the angular smearing represents a
larger fractional change, and at very small angles where the angular
resolution is comparable to the bin width.

Figure 151:
Relative shift map for the angular resolution systematic. The
shift pattern shows structure at large angles and at the kinematic
boundaries where the angular smearing has the largest relative impact.
In the central region, the shift is below 1%, confirming the
insensitivity of the Lund plane density to angular resolution
effects.

.42.8
Non-closure

The non-closure systematic is the residual difference between the
unfolded MC reconstruction-level result (IBU, 32 iterations, MC truth
prior) and the MC generator-level truth. The mean relative impact is
0.2%, consistent with the excellent closure
(
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=0.89
,
p=0.74
). The shift pattern (Figure
Figure
152
) is consistent with statistical noise at
the sub-percent level, confirming that the unfolding procedure
introduces negligible bias.

Figure 152:
Relative shift map for the non-closure systematic. The pattern
is consistent with statistical noise at the sub-percent level,
confirming excellent closure.

.42.9
Thrust axis resolution

The thrust axis resolution systematic is evaluated by comparing
hemisphere assignment using the reconstruction-level thrust axis versus
the generator-level thrust axis in MC. Only 0.23% of tracks migrate
between hemispheres when the generator-level axis is used instead of the
reconstruction-level axis, reflecting the excellent angular resolution
of the charged-particle thrust axis at ALEPH. This small migration
fraction is assigned as a flat relative uncertainty of 0.2%.

Figure 153:
Relative shift map for the thrust axis systematic. The shift is
uniform at approximately 0.2% as expected for a
migration-fraction-based estimate. This is the smallest systematic in
the analysis.

.42.10
Momentum resolution

The momentum resolution systematic is evaluated by smearing track
momenta by
\pm 10\%
of the ALEPH resolution
(
\sigma(1/p_{T})=6\times 10^{-4}
(GeV/
c
)
-1

(
[
ALEPH:colorfactors
]
)). The
smeared MC reconstruction-level distribution is unfolded and compared to
the nominal result. The mean relative impact is 0.3%, confirming that
the Lund plane density is insensitive to momentum resolution at the
level of the ALEPH tracking performance.

This insensitivity is expected because the C/A clustering is
angular-based (the distance metric
d_{ij}=2(1-\cos\theta_{ij})

does not depend on momenta), and the
k_{t}
computation has only linear
sensitivity to the momentum of the softer subjet.

Figure 154:
Relative shift map for the momentum resolution systematic. The
shift is at the sub-percent level everywhere across the Lund plane,
confirming the expected insensitivity of the angular-based C/A
clustering to momentum resolution.

.42.11
Systematic uncertainty summary

The total systematic uncertainty of approximately 19.9% (relative to
the observed data density) is dominated by two sources: tracking
efficiency (9.7%) and MC model dependence (8.6%). The unfolding method
(3.6%) is the third-largest source, while statistical uncertainties
(0.8%) are negligible. Figure
155
shows the
uncertainty budget graphically, and Figure
156

shows the total relative uncertainty across the Lund plane.

The tracking efficiency systematic is the most conservative estimate in
the analysis and is significantly larger than corresponding
uncertainties in the LHC Lund plane measurements. This reflects the more
aggressive variation method (toggling the entire
highPurity

flag rather than varying individual track efficiencies) necessitated by
the limited documentation of the archived ALEPH track quality flags.

The MC model and heavy-flavour uncertainties are within the range
expected from reference analyses. The MC model uncertainty (8.6%) falls
between the ATLAS range (5–20%) and the CMS range (2–7%), while the
heavy-flavour uncertainty (1.5%) is new to this analysis (not evaluated
by ATLAS or CMS, where high-
p_{T}
jets suppress heavy-flavour
effects).

\subsubsubsection

Uncertainty budget

data sample, statistical uncertainties are negligible compared to
systematics. The measurement is entirely
systematics-dominated.

largest at the kinematic boundaries where efficiency drops and edge
effects dominate.

.43
Results

This section presents the primary observed results from the full ALEPH
dataset (2,872,917 events), followed by comparisons to the expected (MC)
result and the 10% validation sample. The expected results from MC
pseudo-data are retained for reference.

.43.1

All six ALEPH LEP1 data files were processed through the full analysis
chain using ProcessPoolExecutor (6 parallel workers). The processing
statistics are summarised in Table

.

. These

boundaries. The measurement is systematics-dominated (19.8% systematic
vs 0.8% statistical).

\subsubsubsection

Physical interpretation

The data systematically exceeds Pythia 6 MC in the soft radiation regime
(
\ln(k_{t})<-2
), consistent with:

•

Known limitations of the Pythia 6 Lund string model with the archived
JETSET tune

•

The MC model systematic being a lower bound [L] (8.6% evaluated
vs 18% observed disagreement)

•

Similar patterns observed in ATLAS and CMS Lund plane measurements at

\sqrt{s}=13
TeV

.44
Comparison to Theory and MC

The full observed result is compared to the Pythia 6 MC truth used to
construct the response matrix. The mean relative difference between the
observed data and Pythia 6 truth is 18.0%, concentrated in the soft
radiation region (
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})<-2
) as described in Section
Section
.43.3
. This pattern is consistent with the
10% validation (21.1% mean difference) and confirms that the data-MC
disagreement is systematic rather than statistical.

.44.1
Comparison to Pythia 6 truth

The observed result shows an 18.0% mean relative difference from Pythia
6 truth, compared to 21.1% at the 10% stage. The slightly smaller
difference for the full data reflects the improved statistical precision
(0.8% vs 7.2%) and the natural averaging over statistical
fluctuations. The pattern of disagreement is identical: data excess at
low
k_{t}
, good agreement in the perturbative region.

The full data
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}
of 1,634 (covariance-based) is much
higher than the 10% value of 32.7 because the statistical uncertainties
are negligible in the full data, making the systematic data-MC
differences dominant. The diagonal-only
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}
of 7.9 is
closer to the 10% value of 4.8, confirming that the per-bin discrepancy
is of similar magnitude.

.44.2
NLL analytic prediction

The NLL analytic calculation of Lifson, Salam, and Soyez
(
[
Lifson:2020gua
]
) provides the first
perturbative prediction for the primary Lund plane density at
next-to-leading logarithmic accuracy, including running-coupling and
two-loop effects. In the perturbative regime
(
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})\gtrsim 0
), the calculation predicts a density
that is approximately uniform in
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
with slowly
varying dependence on
\ln(k_{t})
from the running of
\alpha_{s}
. A
comparison of this measurement to the NLL prediction in the perturbative
region would constitute the first test of the NLL Lund plane calculation
in
e^{+}e^{-}
collisions, where the absence of initial-state radiation
and underlying event provides a theoretically cleaner environment than
hadron colliders.

The NLL calculation applies to the primary Lund plane density defined
with the C/A declustering algorithm, matching the observable definition
used in this analysis (Section
.37.1
). The

e^{+}e^{-}
adaptation uses the same angular coordinate

\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
and transverse momentum coordinate

\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})
as defined in Section
.37.2
.
Quantitative comparison requires the NLL code to be run at

\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV with the appropriate
\alpha_{s}
value; this
constitutes an external generator run that is outside the scope of the
archived data analysis chain but is documented here as a planned
comparison for future work. The standalone Pythia 8 comparison presented
in Section
.44.3
demonstrates the feasibility of
such generator-level comparisons.

.44.3
Comparison to Pythia 8 Monash

A standalone particle-level prediction is obtained from Pythia 8
(version 8.312) with the Monash 2013 tune, which represents the current
state of the art for general-purpose
e^{+}e^{-}
event generation. The
generation uses
e^{+}e^{-}\to Z/\gamma^{*}\to q\bar{q}
at

\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV with ISR, FSR, and full hadronisation enabled.
The fiducial selection matches the measurement: stable charged
final-state particles with
p>0.2
GeV/
c
, at least 5 charged
particles per event, and charged thrust
>0.7
. Hemispheres are
defined along the thrust axis and declustered with the Cambridge/Aachen
algorithm, extracting the primary Lund plane coordinates at each step. A
total of 100,000 events are generated, yielding approximately 198,000
hemispheres and 1.1 million primary splittings.

Figure
168
shows the projection onto

\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
comparing the observed data to both Pythia 8
Monash and Pythia 6. The ratio panel shows that the data lies
systematically above Pythia 8 by 10–30% in the region

1<\ln(1/\Delta\theta)<4
. Pythia 8 and Pythia 6 produce very
similar predictions, indicating that the data-MC disagreement is not
specific to the archived JETSET tune but is a feature of both
generators.

Figure 168:
Projection of the Lund plane density onto the angular axis

\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
, comparing the unfolded ALEPH data (black points
with total uncertainty) to Pythia 8 Monash (blue histogram) and Pythia 6
(red dashed histogram). The lower panel shows the ratio of each
distribution to the Pythia 8 prediction. The first bin at small

\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
lies outside the quality region and should be
interpreted with caution.

Figure
169
shows the corresponding projection
onto
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})
. The data exceeds both generators in the
perturbative region (
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})>0
) and shows reasonable
agreement in the non-perturbative region. The data/Pythia 8 ratio is
approximately 0.9–1.1 in the core region

-2<\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})<1
, with larger deviations in the tails.

Figure 169:
Projection of the Lund plane density onto the transverse
momentum axis
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})
, comparing the unfolded ALEPH data
(black points with total uncertainty) to Pythia 8 Monash (blue
histogram) and Pythia 6 (red dashed histogram). The lower panel shows
the ratio of each distribution to the Pythia 8
prediction.

The 2D ratio of the observed data to the Pythia 8 prediction is shown in
Figure
170
. The ratio is close to unity in the
perturbative region at moderate angles (
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})>0
,

1<\ln(1/\Delta\theta)<3
), while a systematic excess of 20–50%
is observed at large angles and low
k_{t}
, corresponding to the soft
wide-angle region where hadronisation effects dominate. The mean
data/Pythia 8 ratio across quality bins is 1.63, with a median of 1.33.

Figure 170:
Two-dimensional ratio of the observed Lund plane density to the
Pythia 8 Monash prediction. Values near unity (white) indicate good
agreement, while red indicates data excess and blue indicates data
deficit relative to Pythia 8. Only quality bins are shown. The
perturbative region shows approximate agreement, while the soft
wide-angle region shows a systematic data
excess.

The comparison to Pythia 8 confirms two key findings: (1) the observed
data-MC disagreement is not unique to the archived Pythia 6 simulation
but persists with the modern Monash tune, suggesting that the soft
radiation modelling in both generators underestimates the observed rate;
and (2) the perturbative region shows reasonable data-generator
agreement at the 10–20% level, consistent with expectations for a
leading-order parton shower.

Herwig 7 (default tune):
Herwig 7 uses the cluster
hadronisation model rather than the Lund string model, providing
sensitivity to fundamentally different non-perturbative physics.
Comparison in the perturbative region (
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})>0
)
tests the parton shower, while comparison in the non-perturbative region
(
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})<0
) tests the hadronisation model. This
generator comparison is documented here as a planned future study.

.44.4
Cross-experiment comparisons

DELPHI splitting rates

(
[
DELPHI:2004splitting
]
,

[
DELPHI:2004splittingb
]
): The DELPHI Collaboration
measured differential splitting rates for quark and gluon jets at LEP,
which are effectively one-dimensional projections of the Lund plane. The

\ln(k_{t})
projection of this measurement can be compared to the
DELPHI quark-jet splitting rate distribution as a cross-experiment
consistency check. While the observables are not identical (this
measurement uses C/A declustering of thrust hemispheres, while DELPHI
used Durham
k_{t}
splitting), qualitative agreement in the overall
shape and normalisation is expected.

.45
Cross-checks

.45.1
Bin-by-bin correction cross-check

As required by the unfolding conventions, an independent correction
method is applied as a cross-check. The bin-by-bin (BBB) correction uses
factors
C(x,y)=N_{\text{gen}}(x,y)/N_{\text{reco}}(x,y)
computed
from MC and applied multiplicatively to the reconstruction-level data.
The BBB method assumes no bin migration, which introduces a systematic
bias, but provides a simple and transparent cross-check.

Split-sample validation was performed by deriving correction factors
from odd-numbered events and applying them to even-numbered events (and
vice versa), ensuring genuinely independent derivation and application
sets with no event-level correlations.

Table 125:
BBB split-sample closure test. The

\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}\approx 2
is within the alternative method
threshold (
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}<5
) and the agreement between the two
split directions validates the method’s
stability.

Test

\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}

p
-value

Odd
\to
Even

154.7 / 84 = 1.84

< 0.001

Even
\to
Odd

174.6 / 84 = 2.08

< 0.001

The
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}\approx 2
exceeds unity due to the known
systematic bias of the BBB method (it neglects bin migration), but is
well within the alternative method threshold of

\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}<5
. The agreement between the odd-to-even and
even-to-odd directions confirms that the method is stable and not driven
by statistical fluctuations.

Figure 171:
BBB correction factors across the Lund plane. Most factors are
near 1.0 in the central region where the response matrix is highly
diagonal, with larger corrections at the edges reflecting efficiency and
migration effects that the BBB method does not account
for.

Figure 172:
BBB split-sample closure in the
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)

projection. The corrected result (from the even-event subsample using
odd-event factors) is compared to the generator-level truth. Agreement
is within 5–10% across the populated range.

Figure 173:
BBB split-sample closure in the
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})

projection. Similar level of agreement as the angular projection, with
the largest deviations at the kinematic boundaries.

.45.2
SVD unfolding cross-check

SVD unfolding with Tikhonov regularisation (
\tau=0.001
, optimal
value from Phase 4a scan) was applied to the full data as an independent
cross-check of the IBU primary method. The SVD result agrees with IBU to
within 4.9% mean relative difference, consistent with the Phase 4a MC
closure cross-check (4.5%). The slightly larger difference for data (vs
MC closure) is expected given the genuine data-MC differences that the
two methods may handle differently in edge bins.

Table 126:
SVD cross-check agreement with IBU on full data. The 4.9%
agreement is consistent with the MC-level cross-check, confirming that
the unfolding result is robust against the choice of
method.

4.9%

Phase 4a SVD vs IBU (MC closure)
