.45.3
Iteration scan

The stability of the IBU result as a function of the number of
iterations was assessed. The closure test (Table
113
)
shows that the result converges at approximately 32 iterations and
remains stable through 64 iterations. The iteration scan component of
the unfolding systematic (Section
.42.4
) confirms
that the result varies by less than 1% between 24 and 48 iterations,
providing confidence that the nominal choice of 32 iterations is in the
convergence plateau.

.45.4
Prior dependence

The dependence of the unfolded result on the initial prior was tested
with three alternatives to the nominal MC truth prior:

1.

Flat prior:
A uniform distribution across all bins. This
gives a 4.5% mean shift and does not converge within 64 iterations,
indicating significant prior dependence for extreme prior choices.

2.

Reweighted prior (20% tilt in
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)
):
This
gives a negligible 0.01% shift, showing that the result is robust
against moderate prior variations that preserve the qualitative shape
of the truth.

These results indicate that the IBU converges to a prior-independent
result for reasonable priors (within ~20% of the truth)
but retains sensitivity to extreme priors (flat). The 4.5% flat-prior
shift is included in the unfolding systematic envelope.

.46
Conclusions

This analysis note presents the first measurement of the primary Lund
jet plane density in electron-positron collisions, using 2,872,917
hadronic Z decay events recorded by the ALEPH detector at

\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV across all six LEP1 data-taking periods
(1992–1995). The measurement is based on Cambridge/Aachen declustering
of charged-particle thrust hemispheres (5.72 million hemispheres, 28.9
million primary splittings), corrected to stable charged-particle level
using iterative Bayesian unfolding with 32 iterations.

The observed Lund plane density exhibits the characteristic triangular
structure expected from QCD radiation, with the highest density at
moderate angles (
\ln(1/\Delta\theta)\sim 1
–
2
) and moderate
transverse momentum (
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})\sim-1
to
0
). The
result spans 83 quality bins out of 144 total in the
12\times 12

binning.

The total uncertainty on the measurement is approximately 19.9%,
dominated by systematic sources:

•

Tracking efficiency
(9.7%): The largest systematic,
reflecting the difference between the two track selection approaches
and the limited documentation of the archived ALEPH

highPurity
flag.

•

MC model dependence
(8.6%, lower bound [L]): Evaluated
via JETSET parameter variations and histogram reweighting, constrained
by the availability of only one generator (Pythia 6) for
detector-level correction.

•

Unfolding method
(3.6%): The IBU vs BBB, prior dependence,
and iteration scan envelope.

•

Statistical
(0.8%): Negligible with the full dataset. The
measurement is entirely systematics-dominated.

Nine sources of systematic uncertainty have been evaluated, all with
full propagation through the analysis chain. The covariance matrix
(statistical plus systematic) is positive semi-definite with a condition
number of
1.9\times 10^{8}
, suitable for quantitative comparisons
with theory predictions.

The observed data show an 18.0% mean relative difference from the
Pythia 6 MC truth, concentrated in the soft radiation region
(
\ln(k_{t}/\text{GeV})<-2
). This pattern is:

•

Physically expected:
The Pythia 6 Lund string model with the
archived JETSET tune was not optimised for the Lund plane observable.

•

Consistent with LHC results:
ATLAS and CMS observed similar
data-MC differences in the non-perturbative region.

•

Robust:
The full data result is statistically consistent with
the 10% validation sample (
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=1.15
,

p=0.17
), and the SVD unfolding cross-check confirms the primary
IBU result to within 4.9%.

The analysis chain has been validated through:

•

Closure test:
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=0.89
,
p=0.74

•

Stress test: relative tilt tracking to 0.03% precision

•

BBB split-sample cross-check:
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}\approx 2

•

Full vs 10% data consistency:
p=0.17

•

SVD vs IBU agreement: 4.9%

A standalone Pythia 8 (Monash 2013 tune) particle-level prediction at

\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV with matched fiducial selection confirms that
the observed data-MC excess is not specific to the archived Pythia 6
tune: both generators underestimate the data in the soft wide-angle
region, with mean data/Pythia 8 ratios of 1.33 (median) across quality
bins. The perturbative region shows 10–20% agreement between data and
both generators. Comparisons to the NLL analytic calculation of Lifson,
Salam, and Soyez (
[
Lifson:2020gua
]
), to
Herwig 7 particle-level predictions, and to DELPHI splitting rate
measurements (
[
DELPHI:2004splitting
]
,
[
DELPHI:2004splittingb
]
) are documented as
planned comparisons for future work.

Full numerical results (per-bin density values, covariance matrix, and per-source shift vectors) are available as a JSON file in the analysis repository (
analysis_aleph_lund_plane
).

References

R_{b}
,
R_{c}
, and
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
Measurement with ALEPH Data

.47
Introduction

The partial width ratio

R_{b}=\Gamma(Z\to b\bar{b})/\Gamma_{\text{had}}
and the b-quark
forward-backward asymmetry
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
at the Z pole are
precision electroweak observables that probe the
Zb\bar{b}
coupling
vertex. At tree level,
R_{b}
depends on the weak isospin and
hypercharge of the b quark, but at one loop the
Zb\bar{b}
vertex
receives large corrections proportional to
m_{t}^{2}/M_{W}^{2}
from the
top quark in the vertex triangle diagram. This makes
R_{b}
uniquely
sensitive to vertex corrections involving the top quark, independent of
oblique corrections that cancel in the ratio. The Standard Model (SM)
predicts
R_{b}\approx 0.2155
(
[
lephf_combination
]
). Any deviation would signal
non-standard
Zb\bar{b}
couplings or new physics in the
third-generation sector.

The forward-backward asymmetry
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
in

e^{+}e^{-}\to b\bar{b}
at the Z pole arises from the interference
between vector and axial-vector couplings. The differential
cross-section is

{\frac{d\sigma}{d\cos\theta}\propto 1+\cos^{2}\theta+\frac{8}{3}A_{\text{FB}}^{b}\cos\theta}

(29)

where
\theta
is the angle between the incoming electron and the
outgoing b quark. At the Z pole,

A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}=\frac{3}{4}A_{e}A_{b}
where

A_{f}=2g_{V}^{f}g_{A}^{f}/((g_{V}^{f})^{2}+(g_{A}^{f})^{2})
and

g_{V}^{f}/g_{A}^{f}=1-4|Q_{f}|\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{eff}}^{\text{lept}}
.
The b-quark asymmetry is among the most precise single determinations of

\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{eff}}^{\text{lept}}
at LEP
(
[
lep_afb_combination
]
).

This analysis re-examines these observables using the archived ALEPH
open data, comprising approximately 3.05 million hadronic Z decays
collected during 1992–1995 at
\sqrt{s}\approx 91.2
GeV. The
primary physics deliverable is
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
extracted via the
jet charge asymmetry method, with
R_{b}
as a secondary measurement.
The derived quantity
\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{eff}}^{\text{lept}}
is
obtained from
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
.

The b-quark tagging uses a signed impact parameter track probability
method based on the jet-signed
d_{0}
, exploiting the long B-hadron
lifetime (
c\tau\approx 450\ \mu
m). The forward-backward asymmetry
extraction employs a multi-working-point (multi-WP) fit of the charge
flow asymmetry
\langle Q_{\text{FB}}\rangle
versus

\cos\theta_{\text{thrust}}
, simultaneously determining the b-quark
asymmetry, efficiency ratios, and charge separation from the data.

This analysis note presents the full data results alongside the MC
expected results and 10% data validation, completing the staged
validation protocol. The full analysis chain was applied to all
2,567,321 on-peak events after human-approved unblinding.

\subsubsubsection

Analysis methodology
overview

The analysis proceeds through the following stages. First, the data and
MC samples are inventoried and validated, with off-peak LEP scan events
removed by an energy cut. A generator-level flavour tagger is developed
from displaced vertex clustering in the MC, achieving moderate b-purity
(~47% by neutrino proxy) that is adequate for approximate
labelling but insufficient for precision efficiency determination. A
subsequent regression introduced per-hemisphere neutrino truth labels
from the generator-level MC: high-energy neutrinos (
E_{\nu}>3
GeV)
from semileptonic B decays tag one hemisphere, and the opposite
hemisphere provides an unbiased b-enriched sample for measuring
per-flavour tagging efficiencies. This “opposite-hemisphere method”
replaces the external efficiency ratio fallback and enables a
competitive
R_{b}
measurement. An impact parameter resolution model is
fitted per silicon-hit category and calibrated with data/MC scale
factors.

The b-tagging uses a signed impact parameter track probability method:
the geometric
d_{0}
stored in the open data is converted to a
physics-signed quantity using the hemisphere jet direction, and the
per-track significance is converted to a track probability from the
negative-side (prompt-dominated) distribution. The hemisphere tag
variable
\chi_{\text{signed}}
is the sum of negative
log-probabilities for tracks with positive significance. Three tagging
approaches are compared (IP track probability, cut-based displaced track
counting, BDT), with the IP track probability selected as primary based
on superior double-tag excess at all working points.

The
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
extraction uses a multi-working-point fit of the
charge flow asymmetry
\langle Q_{\text{FB}}\rangle
versus

\cos\theta_{\text{thrust}}
at 6
\chi_{\text{signed}}
thresholds,
simultaneously determining the b-quark asymmetry, efficiency ratios, and
charge separation from the data. The
R_{b}
extraction uses the
double-tag counting method with per-flavour efficiencies measured from
neutrino truth labels via the opposite-hemisphere method. Systematic
uncertainties are evaluated for 8 sources on
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}

(with the
\kappa
parameter dependence dominating at 0.017) and 4
sources on
R_{b}
(with the hemisphere correlation
C_{b}
dominating
at 0.012). The analysis follows a staged validation protocol: MC
expected results (Phase 4a), 10% data validation (Phase 4b), and full
data (Phase 4c).

\subsubsubsection

Validation targets

All validation targets are taken from published measurements. The
published ALEPH values are the primary comparison, with LEP combined
averages and SM predictions as additional benchmarks.

.48
Data and Monte Carlo Samples

.48.1
Data samples

The analysis uses archived ALEPH open data from LEP1 running at
center-of-mass energies near the Z pole (
\sqrt{s}\approx 91.2
GeV).
The data were collected during four running years (1992–1995), with the
1994 data split into three sub-periods (P1, P2, P3) corresponding to
different detector configurations.

Each event in the open data has passed the standard ALEPH hadronic event
selection, encoded in the
passesAll
boolean flag. This
selection requires at least 5 good charged tracks with
\geq
4–5 TPC
hits, distance of closest approach (DCA) less than 2 cm in

r
-
\phi

z
,

|\cos\theta|<0.95
–
0.96
, and total charged energy exceeding
20% of
\sqrt{s}
. The selection efficiency is approximately 95% and
is flavour-independent to
{\sim}
1%
(
[
aleph_rb_precise
]
).

The event schema contains 151 branches per event, including event-level
quantities (event number, run number, year, center-of-mass energy, event
selection flags), track-level jagged arrays (kinematics, quality flags,
impact parameters, vertex positions), beam spot information, thrust axis
quantities, event shape variables, and jet clustering results for
multiple algorithms.

.48.2
Monte Carlo sample

A single MC sample is available, corresponding to the 1994 detector
configuration. The MC was generated using the standard ALEPH simulation
chain (based on JETSET/PYTHIA for physics generation and GEANT for
detector simulation) at fixed
\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV.

The MC contains three trees per file:
t
(reconstructed events
matching data schema),
tgen
(generator-level particles for
events passing reconstruction), and
tgenBefore
(generator-level
particles for all generated events before selection). The
generator-level trees contain stable final-state particles in the ALEPH
internal particle code scheme (not PDG codes): 1 = photon, 2/3 =

e^{+}
/
e^{-}
, 5/6 =
\mu^{+}
/
\mu^{-}

\bar{p}
, 58–63
= neutrinos.

A critical constraint of the open data MC is the absence of explicit
truth-level quark flavour labels: the
bFlag
field is uniformly

-999
and the
process
field is
-1
for all MC events. B
and D hadrons have been decayed before storage, so no explicit
heavy-flavour hadron identification is available at the generator level.
This has significant consequences for the analysis, as discussed in
Section
.51.1
.

.48.3
MC sample splitting

The 40 MC files are split into two independent subsets for unbiased
validation:

•

Efficiency derivation sample
(files 001–020): 365,386
events, used for all efficiency, correction, and calibration
determinations

•

Closure test sample
(files 021–040): 364,979 events, used
exclusively for the independent closure test

This splitting ensures the closure test (Section
.55
) is a
genuine validation of the extraction method, not an algebraic tautology.

.48.4
MC validation

The MC describes the data well across all key distributions. The thrust
distribution shows data/MC agreement within 5% across the full range.
The charged track multiplicity has a mean of approximately 17.5 tracks
per event in both data and MC. The energy distribution reveals that the
1993 and 1995 data contain off-peak LEP scan points, necessitating an
energy cut (
|E-91.2|<0.5
GeV) that removes 15.8% of the total
data.

Figure 174:
Center-of-mass energy distribution for all data periods. The
1993 and 1995 samples contain off-peak LEP scan points, visible as tails
below 91.0 GeV and above 91.5 GeV. The on-peak energy cut

|E-91.2|<0.5
GeV (vertical dashed lines) removes these events,
reducing the usable sample by 15.8%.

Figure 175:
Thrust distribution for data (all years combined) and MC
(area-normalized). The MC reproduces the data distribution within

{\sim}5\%
across the full thrust range, from the pencil-like regime (

T\to 1
) to the spherical regime (
T\to 0.5
). The good agreement
validates the use of the thrust axis for hemisphere definition and
angular measurements.

Figure 176:
Charged track multiplicity per event for data and MC. Both
distributions peak near 17–18 tracks per event, with the MC reproducing
the data shape and mean to better than 5%. This validates the MC
modeling of the hadronic event topology.

.49
Event Selection

.49.1
Preselection

The hadronic event selection applies the following criteria
sequentially:

1.

passesAll
: the standard ALEPH hadronic event selection flag,
encoding track quality, multiplicity, and energy requirements.
Efficiency
\approx
95%, flavour-independent to
{\sim}
1%.

2.

Thrust fiducial cut
:
|\cos\theta_{T}|<0.9
, where

\theta_{T}
is the polar angle of the thrust axis. This restricts the
analysis to the central detector region where tracking and vertexing
are well-calibrated.

3.

Data quality cut
: Events with
bFlag = -1
are
excluded from data (small fraction,
{\sim}
0.25%). These events have
an unidentified quality flag that does not appear in the documented
hadronic selection.

4.

On-peak energy cut
:
|E-91.2|<0.5
GeV, removing
off-peak LEP scan events from 1993 and 1995. This is essential for the

A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
measurement, which has strong
\sqrt{s}

dependence near the Z pole.

Figure 177:
Polar angle of the thrust axis,
\cos\theta_{T}
, for data and
MC. The distribution is approximately uniform in
\cos\theta_{T}
within
the acceptance, falling off at
|\cos\theta_{T}|\to 1
due to the
detector geometry. The fiducial cut at
|\cos\theta_{T}|<0.9

(vertical dashed lines) retains the well-instrumented central
region.

The cumulative cutflow is shown in
130
.

.49.2
Track selection

For b-tagging variables, tracks must satisfy:

•

Charged track:
q\neq 0
,
pwflag == 0
(charged particle
flag)

•

High quality:
highPurity == 1

•

Valid impact parameter:
d_{0}\neq-999
(excluding sentinel values)

•

Minimum momentum:
p>0.2
GeV

•

VDET mapping:
nvdet = -127
is mapped to

nvdet = 0
(sentinel for tracks without VDET reconstruction;
affects
{\sim}
36% of tracks)

For the jet charge measurement, only the charged track and quality
requirements are applied (
q\neq 0
,
pwflag == 0
,

highPurity == 1
), without the impact parameter or VDET
requirements.

.49.3
Data quality findings

Several data quality features were identified during exploration and are
handled as follows:

nvdet = -127 sentinel.
Approximately 36% of tracks across all
data years and MC carry
nvdet = -127
, a sentinel value for
tracks not processed by the VDET reconstruction. These tracks are
treated as
nvdet = 0
for impact parameter resolution
purposes.

Impact parameter sentinels.
Approximately 36% of tracks have

d_{0}=-999
, indicating no valid impact parameter measurement. These
tracks are excluded from all b-tagging variables but included in jet
charge calculations.

bFlag = -1 events.
A small fraction of data events (3.1% in
1994P1, 0.01% in 1995, none in other periods) carry

bFlag = -1
. These are excluded as a precaution.

passesAll rate.
The hadronic selection efficiency ranges from
93.9% (1994P1) to 95.0% (1993), consistent with the
{\sim}
95%
efficiency quoted in published ALEPH analyses
(
[
aleph_rb_precise
]
).

.50
Variable Survey

Prior to constructing the b-tag, a survey of candidate discriminating
variables was performed to identify the most promising inputs and
validate the data/MC agreement for each. The survey used the approximate
generator-level flavour labels from the displaced vertex tagger
(described in Section
.51.1
below) to estimate
signal-vs-background separation, and compared data and MC distributions
for each variable.

.50.1

variables

discriminate b events from non-b events. The ROC AUC values are computed
against the approximate generator-level labels (which have only
~50–60% b-purity), so the absolute values are suppressed
relative to the true discrimination power. The ranking is nevertheless
informative.

.51
b-Tagging

.51.1
MC flavour tagger

The open data MC lacks explicit truth-level quark flavour labels. A
generator-level flavour tagger was developed using displaced vertex
clustering in the
tgenBefore
tree, which contains all generated
stable particles with their production vertex positions.

The tagger identifies B and D hadron decays through their displaced
vertex signatures: B hadrons (
c\tau\approx 450\ \mu
m) produce
clusters of multiple displaced vertex positions in the 0.05–1.0 cm
transverse distance range, while D hadrons
(
c\tau\sim 100
–
300\ \mu
m) produce fewer displaced vertices at
smaller distances, and light-quark events produce only

K_{S}^{0}
/
\Lambda
decays at much larger displacements (>1
cm).

The classification rule assigns events to flavour categories based on
the number of distinct vertex positions with at least one charged track
in the 0.05–1.0 cm transverse displacement range:
b
events
have
\geq 3
such vertex positions,
c
events have 1–2
positions, and
uds
events have zero.

Figure 182:
Generator-level flavour tagger composition. The tagged
fractions are approximately 18% b, 27% c, and 55% uds, compared to SM
expectations of 21.6%, 17.2%, and 61.2% respectively. The deviation
is due to the imperfect b/c separation of the displaced vertex
method.

\subsubsubsection

Tagger performance

The tagger achieves the following composition:

expectations.

)

Using neutrino content as a truth proxy (high-momentum neutrinos from B
semileptonic decays), the b-tagged purity is approximately 47% for
b-enriched events, with 35% contamination from uds events misidentified
as b. The b vs c discrimination is 73.8% by the neutrino proxy. The
approximate contamination matrix is:

flavour tagger, refined iteratively to reproduce the observed label
fractions given the SM truth

The tagger does
not
meet the >95% b vs c purity
requirement established in the Phase 1 strategy. The limitation arises
because the
tgenBefore
tree stores only stable particles after
B and D hadron decays, making it impossible to reconstruct the parent
heavy-flavour hadron identity. D hadron decays in c events produce
displaced vertex signatures similar to B hadron decays. This triggers
the Phase 1 fallback [D1]:
R_{c}
is fixed to the SM value
(
R_{c}=0.172\pm 0.0034
(
[
lephf_combination
]
)) and charm efficiency ratios
use external published values.

\subsubsubsection

Per-hemisphere neutrino truth
labels

A regression investigation identified that high-energy neutrinos in the

tgenBefore
tree provide a powerful per-hemisphere b-enrichment
signal, independent of the displaced vertex tagger. In semileptonic B
decays (
b\to c\ell\nu
, BR
\approx 10.6\%
per lepton species),
the neutrino carries a significant fraction of the B-hadron energy. By
splitting generator-level particles into hemispheres using the thrust
axis and requiring
E_{\nu}>3
GeV, approximately 27% of b
hemispheres are tagged while only 11% of c hemispheres (from

D\to\ell\nu X
) and
<1\%
of uds hemispheres pass the
selection. This yields an efficiency ratio

\varepsilon_{b}/\varepsilon_{c}\approx 2.4
, compared to

\approx 1.0
from the displaced vertex tagger.

The neutrino tag cannot be applied to data (neutrinos are invisible at
ALEPH), but it serves as a high-discrimination truth label for measuring
the per-flavour efficiencies of the reco-level
\chi_{\text{signed}}

tag. The key insight is the
opposite-hemisphere method
: in
events with exactly one neutrino-tagged generator hemisphere
(
E_{\nu}>3
GeV), the neutrino hemisphere itself is biased toward
semileptonic B decays (which have fewer reco tracks, reducing the

\chi_{\text{signed}}
efficiency). However, the
opposite

reco hemisphere is an unbiased sample of b hemispheres, since the two B
hadrons in a
b\bar{b}
event decay independently. This opposite
hemisphere provides an unbiased measurement of
\varepsilon_{b}
for the

\chi_{\text{signed}}
tag.

The event classification by neutrino content on the MC efficiency sample
(365,386 events) is:

sample. Events with exactly one neutrino hemisphere provide the
opposite-hemisphere b-enriched
sample.

.51.2
Impact parameter resolution
model

The impact parameter resolution is parametrized as

{\sigma_{d_{0}}=\sqrt{A^{2}+\frac{B^{2}}{p^{2}\sin^{3}\theta}}}

(30)

where
A
represents the intrinsic detector resolution and
B

parametrizes the multiple scattering contribution. The parameters are
fitted on MC using the negative-side
d_{0}
distribution
(prompt-dominated) in bins of
p\sin\theta
, separately for each

nvdet
category.

Table 136:

parameter hits the fit boundary for all
categories, indicating the intrinsic resolution is not
well-constrained.

has approximately unit-width Gaussian shape (std =
1.05), confirming the model works well for tracks with two VDET hits.
For
nvdet = 0
(10% of tracks), the model is inadequate (std
= 2.72) because these tracks have no silicon hit and the TPC-only
resolution is poorly described by the parametric form.

.51.3
Hemisphere construction and jet-signed impact
parameter

Events are divided into two hemispheres by the plane perpendicular to
the thrust axis. Each track is assigned to the forward
(
\vec{p}\cdot\hat{T}\geq 0
) or backward
(
\vec{p}\cdot\hat{T}<0
) hemisphere.

The stored
d_{0}
in the ALEPH open data carries a geometric sign from
the tracking reconstruction, not the physics-signed impact parameter
needed for lifetime tagging. The correct jet-signed
d_{0}
is computed
by projecting the impact parameter relative to the jet axis of the
hemisphere the track belongs to:

{d_{0,\text{physics}}=|d_{0}|\cdot\text{sign}(d_{0,\text{stored}}\cdot\text{cross})}

(31)

where the cross product factor is

\text{cross}=j_{x,\text{hemi}}\sin\phi_{\text{track}}-j_{y,\text{hemi}}\cos\phi_{\text{track}}

and the hemisphere jet direction is flipped for backward-hemisphere
tracks:

j_{x,\text{hemi}}=j_{x}\cdot\text{sign}(\vec{p}\cdot\hat{T})
.
This hemisphere flip ensures that every track’s physics-signed
d_{0}

is computed relative to the jet direction in its own hemisphere. A
positive value indicates the track’s point of closest approach is
downstream of the primary vertex along the jet axis, consistent with
originating from a displaced B or D decay vertex.

\subsubsubsection

Validation of the jet-signed impact
parameter

The corrected computation produces a clear lifetime asymmetry on MC:

Table 138:
Validation of the jet-signed
d_{0}
computation. The geometric
sign (left) produces no lifetime asymmetry, while the physics sign
(right) shows the expected 9% overall asymmetry and factor-of-two
positive tail excess from B-hadron
decays.

Quantity

Geometric
d_{0}
(wrong)

Jet-signed
d_{0}
(correct)

Overall asymmetry
(N_{+}-N_{-})/(N_{+}+N_{-})

0.0005

0.0905

Tail ratio
N(S>3)/N(S<-3)

1.008

1.881

Tail ratio
N(S>5)/N(S<-5)

1.000

2.097

Tail ratio
N(S>10)/N(S<-10)

1.002

2.177

The 9% overall asymmetry and the factor-of-two positive tail excess at

|S|>5
are exactly what is expected from the B-hadron lifetime at
the Z pole. This validates the jet-signing procedure.

Figure 187:
Positive/negative tail ratio versus significance threshold for
the jet-signed impact parameter. The ratio exceeds unity at all
thresholds, growing to 2.2 at
|S|>10
, demonstrating the B-hadron
lifetime asymmetry. Error bars are Poisson. This is the definitive
validation of the physics-signed
d_{0}

computation.

.51.4
IP significance and track
probability

The per-track significance is

S_{i}=d_{0,\text{physics},i}/\sigma_{d_{0},i}
using the resolution
model of Eq.
30
with data/MC scale factors applied.

The track probability
P(S)
is built from the negative-side
significance distribution, which is symmetric and resolution-dominated
for prompt tracks:

{P(S)=\frac{N(|S_{\text{neg}}|>S)}{N_{\text{neg,total}}}}

(32)

This gives the probability that a prompt track has
|S|>S
. For
displaced tracks with large positive significance,
P(S)
is small.

Figure 188:
Track probability function
P(S)
derived from the
negative-side significance distribution.
P(0)=1
,
P(1)=0.075
,

P(3)=0.030
. The steep fall from 0 to 1 reflects the Gaussian core
of the resolution, while the slower decay at large
S
captures the
non-Gaussian tails.

The hemisphere tag variable is the sum of negative log-probabilities for
tracks with positive physics-signed significance:

{\chi_{\text{signed}}=-\sum_{i:S_{i}>0}\log_{10}P(S_{i})}

(33)

Large
\chi_{\text{signed}}
values indicate hemispheres likely to
contain a B-hadron decay.

compared

Three tagging approaches were implemented and compared in Phase 3:

\chi_{\text{signed}}
of Eq.

is the standard ALEPH lifetime tag adapted for the open data
(

). The

b-enrichment:

\subsubsubsection

Approach C: XGBoost BDT

A gradient-boosted decision tree (XGBoost) was trained on 13
hemisphere-level features including track multiplicity, displaced track
counts at multiple thresholds, sum and maximum of track significances,
invariant mass of displaced tracks, and hemisphere jet charge. The BDT
achieved good separation on MC (AUC > 0.7 against the
approximate flavour labels), but all 13 input variables failed the
data/MC
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}<5
criterion, making the BDT unsuitable
for data application without extensive calibration. The data/MC
disagreement stems from the combination of imperfect IP resolution
modeling (particularly for nvdet = 0 tracks) and the single-year MC not
perfectly representing the multi-year data. The BDT feature importance
and score distributions are documented in the appendices
(Figure
217
, Figure
218
).

\subsubsubsection

Approach comparison and
selection

Approach A (
\chi_{\text{signed}}
) provides the best double-tag
excess at every tag efficiency, exceeding Approach B because it exploits
the physics-signed information (positive significance selects tracks
displaced downstream of the primary vertex). Approach C was rejected due
to poor data/MC modeling of the inputs. The quantitative comparison at
matched tag fractions shows that at
f_{t}\approx 0.015
(the tightest
usable point), Approach A achieves
E=2.01
while Approach B reaches
only
E=1.61
at comparable efficiency, a 25% improvement in the
double-tag excess that directly translates to better sensitivity for
both the
R_{b}
and
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
extractions.

Figure 189:
Approach comparison: double-tag excess versus per-hemisphere
tag fraction. The IP track probability method (
\chi_{\text{signed}}
,
blue circles) achieves higher excess than the displaced track count
(
N(|d_{0}|>500\ \mu
m), red squares) at every working point,
demonstrating superior b/non-b discrimination. The BDT approach was
rejected due to data/MC disagreement on the input
features.

Selected primary tag:

\chi_{\text{signed}}
(Approach A).

Cross-check tag:

N(|d_{0}|>500\ \mu
m) (Approach B).

comparison

The
\chi_{\text{signed}}
distribution shows good data/MC agreement
across the full range, validating the transfer of MC-derived corrections
to data.

Figure 190:
Distribution of the hemisphere b-tag variable

for data and MC (area-normalized). The MC
describes the data distribution within

{\sim}5\%
across the full range, from the untagged region (

) through the high-purity b-tag region (

).
The good agreement validates the physics-signed
d_{0}
computation and
the resolution model calibration.

\subsubsubsection

Cross-check: double-tag excess
growth

The double-tag excess
E=f_{d}/f_{t}^{2}
increases monotonically with
the
\chi_{\text{signed}}
cut threshold, demonstrating stable
b-enrichment that grows with tag purity. There is no evidence of
instability or sign changes.

Figure 191:
Double-tag excess versus
\chi_{\text{signed}}
cut
threshold. The excess grows monotonically from
E=1.08
at

\chi>2
to
E=2.83
at
\chi>10
, demonstrating increasing
b-purity at tighter working points. An excess
E>1
is direct
model-independent evidence that the tag preferentially selects

b\bar{b}
events.

.52
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}

Extraction

.52.1
Method overview

The
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
extraction uses a multi-working-point (multi-WP)
fit inspired by the simultaneous fit approach of ALEPH
(
[
aleph_afb
]
). The central idea
is to measure the slope of
\langle Q_{\text{FB}}\rangle
versus

\cos\theta_{\text{thrust}}
at multiple b-tag thresholds and fit a
model that simultaneously determines the b-quark asymmetry, the
efficiency ratios between flavours, and the charge separation ratio.

The procedure at each of 6
\chi_{\text{signed}}
thresholds (1.5,
2.0, 2.5, 3.0, 4.0, 5.0) is:

1.

Compute
\langle Q_{\text{FB}}\rangle
in 9 bins of

\cos\theta_{\text{thrust}}
for tagged events

2.

Fit the slope:

\langle Q_{\text{FB}}\rangle(\cos\theta)=a\cdot h(\cos\theta)

where
h(\cos\theta)=8\cos\theta/(3+\cos^{2}\theta)

3.

Measure the effective charge separation
|\delta_{\text{eff}}|
from
the variance difference

\text{Var}(Q_{\text{FB}})-\text{Var}(Q_{\text{sum}})

(
[
lep_afb_combination
]
)

4.

Constrain tag efficiencies from the hemisphere tag rate
f_{t}
and
double-tag rate
f_{d}

The combined fit extracts three parameters:
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
, the
efficiency ratio
r=\varepsilon_{\text{uds}}/\varepsilon_{c}
, and the
charge separation ratio
\delta_{b}/\delta_{c}
.

The slope at each working point is modeled as:

{a=\delta_{b}f_{b}A_{\text{FB}}^{b}(1-2\bar{\chi})+\delta_{c}f_{c}A_{\text{FB}}^{c}+\delta_{\text{uds}}f_{\text{uds}}A_{\text{FB}}^{\text{uds}}}

(34)

where
f_{q}
are the per-flavour fractions in the tagged sample,

\delta_{q}
are the per-flavour charge separations, and
\bar{\chi}
is
the B meson mixing parameter.

The correction chain from measured to pole asymmetry is:

1.

Multi-WP fit yields
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
(including mixing dilution in
the fit denominator)

2.

QCD correction:

A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}=A_{\text{FB}}^{b}/(1-C_{\text{QCD}})

.52.2
Jet charge definition

The jet charge in each hemisphere is defined as

{Q_{F(B)}=\frac{\sum_{i\in F(B)}q_{i}|p_{i}\cdot\hat{T}|^{\kappa}}{\sum_{i\in F(B)}|p_{i}\cdot\hat{T}|^{\kappa}}}

(35)

where
\hat{T}
is the thrust axis direction,
q_{i}
is the track
charge, and
\kappa
is a tunable parameter that controls the momentum
weighting. The charge flow is
Q_{\text{FB}}=Q_{F}-Q_{B}
and the
charge sum is
Q_{\text{sum}}=Q_{F}+Q_{B}
.

Three values of
\kappa
are studied: 0.3, 0.5, and 1.0. At low

\kappa
, the weighting is nearly democratic across all tracks,
maximizing the statistical precision but reducing the charge separation
because soft fragmentation products dilute the signal. At high

\kappa
, the leading track dominates, increasing the charge
separation but also the statistical fluctuation. The primary analysis
uses
\kappa=0.5
as a compromise; on the full data,

\kappa=0.3
provides the best-constrained fit due to the absence of
the near-flat likelihood direction that affects
\kappa=0.5
(see
Section
.59
).

The inclusive jet charge properties measured on data and MC are:

Table 141:
Inclusive jet charge flow properties for three
\kappa

values. The mean
Q_{\text{FB}}
is small
(~
-0.005
) as expected for an inclusive hadronic
sample. The width increases with
\kappa
as higher
\kappa
weights
more on the leading track. Data/MC agreement is excellent in both mean
and width.

.52.4

The MC efficiency sample (files 001–020, 365,386 events at

\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV) serves as pseudo-data. At the

\chi_{\text{signed}}>2
working point, 269,002 events pass the
b-tag (73.6%). The per-hemisphere tag fraction is
f_{t}=0.511
with
double-tag fraction
f_{d}=0.285
, giving an excess

E=f_{d}/f_{t}^{2}=1.09
.

.52.6
Slope fit results

The fit of
\langle Q_{\text{FB}}\rangle=a\cdot h(\cos\theta)
at
the primary working point (
\chi>2
,
\kappa=0.5
) yields:

a=-0.00427\pm 0.00065,\quad\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=6.1/8=0.76

The negative slope confirms the expected sign convention: the b-quark
hemisphere has more negative jet charge (
Q_{b}=-1/3
), so

\langle Q_{\text{FB}}\rangle<0
when
\cos\theta>0
(b quark
going forward).

Figure 195:

\langle Q_{\text{FB}}\rangle
as a

for MC pseudo-data with

\kappa=0.5
at the
\chi_{\text{signed}}>2
working point. The
red line shows the fit to the expected angular dependence

h(\cos\theta)=8\cos\theta/(3+\cos^{2}\theta)
. The antisymmetric
pattern demonstrates the forward-backward asymmetry signal. The fit
gives
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=6.1/8
(
p=0.64
).

.52.8
\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{eff}}

derivation

From
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}=\frac{3}{4}A_{e}A_{b}
with

A_{f}=2v_{f}a_{f}/(v_{f}^{2}+a_{f}^{2})
and

v_{f}/a_{f}=1-4|Q_{f}|\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{eff}}
, the effective weak
mixing angle is

\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{eff}}^{\text{lept}}=0.237\pm 0.005

This is 1.1 sigma from the world average (
0.2315
), consistent within
the analysis uncertainties.

.53
R_{b}

Extraction

.53.1
Method

The
R_{b}
extraction uses the double-tag counting method with
per-flavour efficiencies measured from the MC using the
opposite-hemisphere neutrino truth label method described in
Section
.51.1
. The per-flavour efficiencies
(
\varepsilon_{b}
,
\varepsilon_{c}
,
\varepsilon_{\text{uds}}
) and
hemisphere correlations (
C_{b}
,
C_{c}
,
C_{\text{uds}}
) are
determined on the MC efficiency sample (files 001–020), with
R_{c}

fixed to the SM value (
R_{c}=0.172

(
[
lephf_combination
]
)).

The extraction equations are:

{f_{s}=\varepsilon_{b}R_{b}+\varepsilon_{c}R_{c}+\varepsilon_{\text{uds}}R_{\text{uds}}}

(36)

{f_{d}=C_{b}\varepsilon_{b}^{2}R_{b}+C_{c}\varepsilon_{c}^{2}R_{c}+C_{\text{uds}}\varepsilon_{\text{uds}}^{2}R_{\text{uds}}}

(37)

where
f_{s}=N_{s}/(2N_{\text{had}})
,
f_{d}=N_{d}/N_{\text{had}}
,
and
R_{\text{uds}}=1-R_{b}-R_{c}
. Given the three measured
per-flavour efficiencies and hemisphere correlations,
R_{b}
is solved
from the single-tag equation. The double-tag equation provides a
cross-check.

The opposite-hemisphere method provides unbiased efficiency measurements
because the two B hadrons in a
b\bar{b}
event decay independently.
In events with exactly one neutrino-tagged generator hemisphere, the
reco hemisphere opposite to the neutrino tag is an inclusive
b-hemisphere sample, free of the semileptonic decay bias that would
reduce the
\chi_{\text{signed}}
efficiency on the neutrino side
(where the lost neutrino reduces the visible track multiplicity). The
charm and light-flavour efficiencies are measured from no-neutrino
events, with a composition correction accounting for the residual b
contamination.

.53.2
Efficiency inputs at the primary working
point

The primary working point is
\chi_{\text{signed}}>5
, chosen as the
optimal balance between b-purity and statistical power. The per-flavour
efficiencies and hemisphere correlations are:

.53.4
Systematic uncertainties on

R_{b}

The systematic uncertainty budget for
R_{b}
is dominated by the
hemisphere correlation
C_{b}
, which enters quadratically in the
double-tag equation and affects the interpretation of the
single-tag/double-tag consistency.

Table 147:
Systematic uncertainty budget for
R_{b}
. The hemisphere
correlation
C_{b}
dominates, contributing 84% of the total systematic
in quadrature.

Source

\Delta R_{b}

Fraction of total

0.01412

The
C_{b}
systematic arises because the hemisphere correlation is
measured on MC and may differ from data due to generator-dependent QCD
effects (gluon radiation correlating the two hemispheres). The
efficiency statistical uncertainty reflects the finite size of the
neutrino-tagged sample used for the
\varepsilon_{b}
measurement
(approximately 89,000 one-neutrino events). The
E_{\nu}
threshold
systematic is evaluated by varying the neutrino energy cut from 3 GeV to
2 GeV and 5 GeV, probing the sensitivity to the semileptonic decay
kinematics. The
R_{c}
systematic propagates the SM uncertainty on

R_{c}=0.172\pm 0.003
(
[
lephf_combination
]
).

.53.5
Operating point stability

The extracted
R_{b}
shows a downward trend with increasing
\chi

cut, converging toward the SM value at tighter working points. This
behaviour is expected: at looser cuts the light-flavour contamination is
larger and small errors in
\varepsilon_{\text{uds}}
have a larger
impact on
R_{b}
.

Figure 196:
Extracted
R_{b}
versus
\chi_{\text{signed}}
cut threshold
using the neutrino truth label efficiencies. The result converges toward
the SM value (blue dashed line) at tighter working points
(
\chi>5
). The published ALEPH value is shown as a green band. The
stability across
\chi\in[5,7]
validates the choice of

\chi>5
as the primary working point.

.53.6
Closure test

The closure test applies the extraction to the independent MC closure
sample (files 021–040, 364,979 events) using efficiencies derived from
the efficiency sample (files 001–020):

R_{b}^{\text{closure}}=0.2140\pm 0.0044\,(\text{stat}),\quad\text{pull vs SM}=-0.52\sigma

The pull is well within the 2-sigma criterion, confirming that the
opposite-hemisphere method correctly recovers
R_{b}
on an independent
MC sample.

Figure 197:
Closure test for the
R_{b}
extraction using neutrino truth
labels. The extracted
R_{b}
on the closure sample (black point) is
compared to the SM prediction (blue dashed) and published ALEPH value
(green band). The pull of
-0.5\sigma
confirms method
self-consistency.

.54.2
Per-systematic impact on

A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}

\subsubsubsection

Kappa parameter
dependence

The dominant systematic uncertainty arises from the choice of the
momentum weighting exponent
\kappa
in the jet charge definition. The
extraction is performed at three
\kappa
values (0.3, 0.5, 1.0),
yielding
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
values of 0.052, 0.070, and 0.086
respectively. The half-spread between the extreme values is taken as the
systematic:
\Delta A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}=0.017
. This is the largest
systematic source, at 0.6 times the statistical uncertainty. The
sensitivity to
\kappa
arises because different
\kappa
values
weight different momentum ranges, probing different aspects of the
fragmentation process. The multi-WP fit partially absorbs
\kappa

sensitivity into the fitted
\delta_{b}/\delta_{c}
ratio, reducing this
systematic compared to a single-WP extraction.

\subsubsubsection

Charm asymmetry
contamination

The charm forward-backward asymmetry
A_{\text{FB}}^{c}=0.0707

(
[
lephf_combination
]
) contaminates the measured slope through the

\delta_{c}f_{c}A_{\text{FB}}^{c}
term in Eq.
34
. The
charm fraction
f_{c}
in the tagged sample is approximately 8% at

\chi>2
. Varying
A_{\text{FB}}^{c}
by
\pm 0.015
(its
experimental uncertainty) shifts
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
by 0.0046. This
is the second-largest systematic at 0.16 times the statistical
uncertainty.

\subsubsubsection

Hadronization model

Only one MC generator (JETSET/PYTHIA, archived with the ALEPH data) is
available, precluding direct generator-to-generator comparisons
[L1]. The systematic is estimated from the published inter-generator
spread at LEP: the difference between JETSET (string hadronization) and
HERWIG (cluster hadronization) on
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
-related quantities
is approximately 0.001 (
[
aleph_rb_multitag
]
). This benchmark is adopted as the hadronization
systematic floor.

\subsubsubsection

Thrust axis angular
resolution

The thrust axis approximates the b-quark production direction but
suffers from angular smearing due to finite track multiplicity and gluon
radiation. The impact on
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
is 0.0005, taken from
the published ALEPH systematic (
[
aleph_afb
]
). This is negligible compared to the statistical
uncertainty.

\subsubsubsection

QCD correction

The QCD correction
C_{\text{QCD}}=0.036\pm 0.005
accounts for the
effect of hard gluon radiation on the thrust axis orientation relative
to the b-quark direction. Varying
C_{\text{QCD}}
within its
uncertainty shifts
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
by 0.0004. The correction is
computed following the methodology of Djouadi, Lampe, and Zerwas
(
[
qcd_afb_correction
]
).

\subsubsubsection

B meson mixing

The B meson mixing parameter
\bar{\chi}=0.1259\pm 0.0042

(
[
lephf_combination
]
) dilutes the measured asymmetry because

B^{0}
-
\bar{B}^{0}
oscillations can reverse the charge assignment.
The effect enters the extraction through the
(1-2\bar{\chi})
factor
in Eq.
34
. Propagating the
\bar{\chi}
uncertainty
directly through the extraction formula gives

\Delta A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}=|A_{\text{FB}}^{b}|\cdot 2\Delta\bar{\chi}/[(1-2\bar{\chi})(1-C_{\text{QCD}})]=0.0008
,
which is subdominant.

\subsubsubsection

Gluon splitting

The gluon splitting rates
g\to b\bar{b}
(0.254%) and

g\to c\bar{c}
(2.96%) affect the flavour composition of the tagged
sample. Varying these rates within their LEP convention uncertainties
(
\pm
50% and
\pm
30%, respectively,
(
[
lephf_combination
]
)) produces a shift of 0.0003 on

A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
.

\subsubsubsection

Delta ratio external
input

The
\delta_{\text{uds}}/\delta_{c}
ratio is fixed to
-0.227
from
the published ALEPH measurement (
[
aleph_afb
]
). Varying this ratio by
\pm 0.10
shifts

A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
by 0.0002.

develop the method (files 001–020).

Both pulls are within the 2-sigma

demonstrates method self-consistency.

.55.2
Goodness-of-fit

The
\chi^{2}
of the
\langle Q_{\text{FB}}\rangle
versus

\cos\theta
fit provides a direct goodness-of-fit measure:

\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=6.1/8=0.76

A toy-based p-value from 500 pseudo-experiments confirms the fit
quality:

p=0.648

The observed
\chi^{2}
falls well within the expected distribution (toy
mean: 8.1, median: 7.4), indicating the angular dependence model

h(\cos\theta)=8\cos\theta/(3+\cos^{2}\theta)
describes the data
adequately. The p-value is far from rejection thresholds.

Figure 200:
Goodness-of-fit:
\chi^{2}
distribution from 500 toy
pseudo-experiments (histogram) compared to the observed value (red
vertical line at
\chi^{2}=6.1
). The observed value falls well within
the expected distribution, with a toy-based p-value of 0.65. The
analytical
\chi^{2}(8)
distribution (blue dashed curve) is shown for
reference.

.56
Expected Results

This section presents expected results from MC pseudo-data. The 10%
data validation results are presented in Section
.58
.

.56.1
Primary result:

A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}

The primary physics result is the b-quark forward-backward asymmetry at
the Z pole, extracted via the multi-WP jet charge method at

\kappa=0.5
:

A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}=0.070\pm 0.029\,(\text{stat})\pm 0.018\,(\text{syst})

The total uncertainty is 0.034, dominated by the statistical component
(0.029). The systematic uncertainty is driven by the
\kappa

parameter dependence (0.017) and the charm asymmetry contamination
(0.005).

.56.2
Derived quantity:

\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{eff}}^{\text{lept}}

\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{eff}}^{\text{lept}}=0.237\pm 0.005\,(\text{stat})\pm 0.003\,(\text{syst})

This is 1.1 sigma from the world average (
0.2315
), consistent within
the analysis uncertainties. The precision is limited by the moderate
b-purity of the
\chi
-based tag.

.56.3
Secondary result:

R_{b}

Using the opposite-hemisphere neutrino truth label method at

\chi>5
on the MC pseudo-data sample:

R_{b}=0.2174\pm 0.0044\,(\text{stat})\pm 0.0141\,(\text{syst})

This is consistent with the SM value (
R_{b}^{\text{SM}}=0.2163
)
within the total uncertainty of 0.0148. The extraction uses per-flavour
efficiencies measured from the neutrino-tagged MC sample, with the
dominant systematic arising from the hemisphere correlation
C_{b}

(0.012). The
R_{b}
measurement is now a competitive secondary result
alongside the primary
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
.

.56.4
Results summary

Table 154:

and

within

. The

.56.5
Scaling to full data

The MC pseudo-data sample has 365,386 events, compared to 2,567,321
on-peak data events (
\sim 7\times
more). The statistical uncertainty
is expected to scale as
1/\sqrt{N}
, giving an expected statistical
precision on data of

\sigma_{\text{stat}}(A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b})_{\text{data}}\approx 0.029/\sqrt{7}\approx 0.011

This would yield a total uncertainty of approximately 0.021 (adding
systematics in quadrature), which is larger than the published ALEPH
precision (0.005) but still provides a meaningful measurement.

.56.6
Covariance matrix

The
9\times 9
covariance matrix for the

\langle Q_{\text{FB}}\rangle
measurement (9 bins in
\cos\theta
)
is provided in machine-readable form in

results/covariance.json
. The matrix is positive-definite with
condition number 327. The statistical component is diagonal
(uncorrelated bin-to-bin), while the systematic component has the
expected antisymmetric structure (positive covariance for same-sign

\cos\theta
bins, negative for opposite-sign bins), reflecting the
slope-like nature of the asymmetry signal.

.57
Comparison to Published Results

.57.1
Comparison framework

The expected
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
is compared quantitatively to three
reference values:

Table 155:

All comparisons are consistent within 1.0 sigma. The analysis correctly
extracts
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
from the loose b-tag, with uncertainties
that honestly reflect the limited sensitivity due to the moderate
b-purity (
f_{b}\approx 0.29
) achievable with the IP-only tag on the
open data.

The multi-WP fit determines
f_{b}\approx 0.29
at
\chi>2
, which
is much lower than the b-purities achieved by published ALEPH analyses
using the combined IP+mass tag (
f_{b}\sim 0.8
at comparable
efficiencies). This explains the larger statistical uncertainty compared
to published results: the asymmetry signal is diluted by the large non-b
contamination in the tagged sample, and the multi-WP fit must
simultaneously determine
f_{b}
from the data rather than relying on
high-purity MC-derived flavour labels.

average, and the SM prediction, all within the analysis uncertainties.
The uncertainty bands reflect the total (stat + syst) uncertainty for
each measurement.

.58
10% Data Validation

This section presents the 10% data validation, comparing results from a
random 10% subsample of the full data to the MC pseudo-data
expectations from Phase 4a.

.58.1
Subsample selection

A 10% subsample is selected using a fixed random seed (seed=42),
yielding 256,511 events from the total 2,567,321 on-peak data events
(10.0%). This subsample is approximately 70% the size of the MC
efficiency sample (365,386 events), so statistical uncertainties on the
10% data are comparable to the MC pseudo-data uncertainties.

.58.4
Diagnostic sensitivity: data versus
MC

The following comparisons are more sensitive to data/MC differences than
the final physics result, as required by the conventions for extraction
measurements.

\subsubsubsection

Tag rate comparison

Per-hemisphere tag rates are compared between 10% data and MC at
multiple working points:

Data tag rates are systematically 1–2% higher than MC at intermediate
working points (
\chi>2
through
\chi>5
), with pulls of
4–6
\sigma
. This reflects known data/MC differences in the track
impact parameter resolution and track multiplicity distributions between
the 1994 MC configuration and the multi-year data. At the tightest
working point (
\chi>8
), where the b-enrichment is highest, the
data/MC agreement is excellent (pull = 0.54
\sigma
).

The tag rate differences do not propagate to the
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}

extraction because the multi-WP fit is self-calibrating: it determines
the efficiency parameters from the data at each working point, absorbing
overall normalization shifts into the fitted
f_{b}
,
r
, and

\delta_{b}/\delta_{c}
parameters. The relevant quantity for the
asymmetry measurement is the slope of
\langle Q_{\text{FB}}\rangle

versus
\cos\theta
, which is a ratio-like quantity insensitive to
overall tag rate.

are systematically 1–2% higher than MC, producing pulls of
4–6
\sigma
at intermediate working points. This sensitivity
demonstrates that diagnostic tag rate comparisons detect data/MC
differences that are absorbed by the self-calibrating physics
extraction.

.59

The
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
measurement transitions from statistically
limited (4a: stat/syst = 1.6) to systematically limited (4c: stat/syst =
0.7). The dominant systematic is the
\kappa
dependence (0.017),
which arises from the intrinsic properties of the jet charge method. The

R_{b}
measurement is systematically limited at all stages, with the
hemisphere correlation
C_{b}
dominating (0.012).

.60
Conclusions

This analysis note presents the measurement of
R_{b}
,

A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
, and
\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{eff}}^{\text{lept}}

using the full sample of 2,567,321 on-peak hadronic Z decay events from
archived ALEPH open data at LEP1.

The primary result is:

A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}=0.069\pm 0.013\,(\text{stat})\pm 0.018\,(\text{syst})=0.069\pm 0.022\,(\text{total})

The corresponding effective weak mixing angle is:

\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{eff}}^{\text{lept}}=0.238\pm 0.004

Both results are consistent with the published ALEPH measurement (pull =

-1.1\sigma
), the LEP combined average (pull =
-1.4\sigma
), and
the Standard Model prediction (pull =
-1.6\sigma
). The full data
result is fully consistent with both the 10% data validation (pull =

0.15\sigma
) and the MC expected result (pull =
0.04\sigma
),
demonstrating the stability of the extraction across sample sizes.

The secondary
R_{b}
measurement uses the opposite-hemisphere neutrino
truth label method to determine per-flavour tagging efficiencies from
the MC, replacing the external efficiency ratio approach that dominated
the pre-regression uncertainty. The result on the full data sample is:

R_{b}=0.2122\pm 0.0016\,(\text{stat})\pm 0.0141\,(\text{syst})=0.2122\pm 0.0142\,(\text{total})

This is consistent with the SM value (
0.2163
) at
-0.3\sigma
and
the published ALEPH measurement (
0.2158\pm 0.0014
) at

-0.2\sigma
. The regression improved the
R_{b}
precision by a
factor of 10.5 (from
\pm 0.156
to
\pm 0.014
) and reduced the
bias from 47% to 1.9% of the SM value. The closure test passes at

-0.5\sigma
, and the 10% data validation yields a consistent result
(
R_{b}=0.201\pm 0.005
). The total
R_{b}
uncertainty of 0.014 is
approximately 10 times larger than the published ALEPH precision
(
\pm 0.0014
), reflecting the limitations of the IP-only tag and the
neutrino truth label sample size.

The
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
measurement on the full data sample achieves
a total precision of 0.022, which is approximately 4 times larger than
the published ALEPH precision (0.005) but represents a meaningful
extraction from open data using a self-calibrating method that requires
minimal external inputs. The dominant systematic (
\kappa
dependence,
0.017) is irreducible within the jet charge method and would require
alternative charge tagging techniques to reduce.

.60.1
Dominant limitations

1.

Absence of truth flavour labels
[A1]: The open data MC
stores only stable particles at the generator level, with B and D
hadrons already decayed. The per-hemisphere neutrino truth label
method partially overcomes this limitation for
R_{b}
, achieving

\varepsilon_{b}/\varepsilon_{\text{uds}}\approx 4.3
, but the

C_{b}
hemisphere correlation remains the dominant systematic.

2.

No secondary vertex mass
[L2]: The open data does not
provide explicit secondary vertex reconstruction, limiting the b-tag
to IP-based methods and reducing charm rejection.

3.

Single MC generator
[L1]: Only the archived ALEPH MC is
available, precluding direct hadronization model comparisons.

.61
Future Directions

The following improvements could further enhance the measurement:

1.

Per-year d0 calibration.
The 1992–1993 data predate the VDET
upgrade. Year-dependent
d_{0}
scale factors could improve the
per-period consistency.

2.

Tighter b-tag optimization.
Exploring multi-variable
combinations beyond the single
\chi_{\text{signed}}
variable could
improve b-purity without requiring the full BDT approach that failed
the data/MC quality gate.

3.

Alternative charge tagging.
Lepton-based charge tagging using
identified electrons and muons from semileptonic B decays would
provide an independent charge tag with different systematics,
potentially reducing the dominant
\kappa
dependence systematic.

4.

Combined extraction.
A simultaneous fit combining jet charge
with lepton-based methods would reduce the total uncertainty below the
current 0.022.

5.

Multi-WP fit regularization.
Constraining the
r
and

\delta_{b}/\delta_{c}
parameters to reduce the near-degeneracy
observed at
\kappa=0.5
on full data.

.62
Constraint and Limitation
Index

This appendix collects all constraints, limitations, and design
decisions introduced throughout the analysis, with their impact on the
final results and mitigations applied.

.62.1
Constraints

\subsubsubsection

[A1] No explicit truth flavour label in
MC

The open data MC does not contain truth-level quark flavour information.
The
bFlag
field is uniformly
-999
and the
process

field is
-1
. Event flavour must be inferred from generator-level
particle content.

•

Introduced:
Phase 1 (Strategy)

•

Impact:
Prevents clean determination of per-flavour tagging
efficiencies from a single truth label; the displaced vertex tagger
achieves only
{\sim}
47% b-purity

•

Mitigation:
Per-hemisphere neutrino truth labels from

tgenBefore
(
E_{\nu}>3
GeV) provide an independent
b-enrichment signal with
\varepsilon_{b}/\varepsilon_{c}\approx 2.4
.
The opposite-hemisphere method uses these to measure unbiased
per-flavour
\chi_{\text{signed}}
efficiencies, enabling a
competitive
R_{b}
extraction (
\pm 0.014
total).
R_{c}
remains
fixed to the SM value

\subsubsubsection

[A2] No per-track impact parameter
uncertainty

The open data ntuple does not contain a per-track
\sigma_{d_{0}}
. The
resolution must be parametrized from tracking detector hit information.

•

Introduced:
Phase 1 (Strategy)

•

Impact:
Limits the precision of the IP significance
computation, particularly for nvdet = 0 tracks

•

Mitigation:
Parametric resolution model fitted per nvdet
category (Phase 2); data/MC scale factors derived from negative-side

d_{0}
distributions; model validated to have unit-width
negative-side significance for nvdet = 2

.62.2
Limitations

\subsubsubsection

[L1] Single MC generator
available

Only the archived ALEPH MC (JETSET/PYTHIA) is available. No alternative
generator can be run on these data to assess hadronization model
dependence.

•

Introduced:
Phase 1 (Strategy)

•

Impact:
Hadronization systematic cannot be evaluated by
generator comparison; must rely on published inter-generator spreads

•

Mitigation:
Fragmentation parameters varied within published
uncertainties; systematic floor set to the published JETSET-HERWIG
spread (
\Delta A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}\approx 0.001
)

\subsubsubsection

[L2] No secondary vertex
mass

The open data does not provide explicit secondary vertex reconstruction,
limiting b-tagging to impact parameter methods.

•

Introduced:
Phase 1 (Strategy)

•

Impact:
Reduced charm rejection compared to published ALEPH
analyses (which used combined IP+mass tags); lower b-purity in tagged
sample (
f_{b}\approx 0.29
vs
\sim 0.8
)

•

Mitigation:
Multi-WP fit simultaneously determines
f_{b}

from data;
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
extraction is self-calibrating;
R_{b}

extraction uses external efficiency ratios

\subsubsubsection

[L3]
R_{b}
extraction
feasibility

Originally, the
R_{b}
extraction was deemed infeasible without
external efficiency ratios due to the displaced vertex tagger’s failure
to discriminate b from c (
\varepsilon_{b}/\varepsilon_{c}\approx 1.0
).
A regression analysis identified per-hemisphere neutrino truth labels as
a viable alternative.

•

Introduced:
Phase 3 (Processing); resolved by regression
(Phase 5 v2)

•

Impact:
Pre-regression:
R_{b}=0.317\pm 0.156

(essentially unmeasured). Post-regression:
R_{b}=0.2122\pm 0.0142

(competitive secondary measurement)

•

Mitigation:
Opposite-hemisphere method using neutrino truth
labels provides
\varepsilon_{b}/\varepsilon_{\text{uds}}\approx 4.3

at
\chi>5
. Closure test passes (
-0.5\sigma
). Systematic
dominated by
C_{b}
hemisphere correlation (0.012)

.62.3
Design Decisions

\subsubsubsection

[D1] Flavour identification
strategy

Developed a generator-level flavour tagger from displaced vertices in

tgenBefore
. When the tagger failed to meet the >95%
purity requirement, the original fallback was activated:
R_{c}
fixed
to SM, external efficiency ratios from published measurements. A
regression subsequently introduced per-hemisphere neutrino truth labels
with the opposite-hemisphere method, replacing external efficiency
ratios with internally measured per-flavour efficiencies.
R_{c}

remains fixed to SM.

\subsubsubsection

[D2] Fiducial volume

|\cos\theta_{T}|<0.9
for
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
, restricting the
measurement to the central detector region with good tracking and
vertexing.

\subsubsubsection

[D3] Selection approach
comparison

Three approaches compared: IP significance (A), cut-based displaced
tracks (B), BDT (C). Approach A selected as primary based on superior
double-tag excess at all working points. Approach C rejected due to
data/MC disagreement.

\subsubsubsection

[D4] Primary technique for

R_{b}

Double-tag counting extraction, the standard LEP method exploiting
hemisphere independence and self-calibration.

\subsubsubsection

[D5]
R_{c}

extraction

R_{c}
fixed to SM value as the baseline. Simultaneous extraction
dropped due to insufficient charm efficiency contrast from IP-only
tagging.

\subsubsubsection

[D6]
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}

technique

Multi-working-point jet charge fit, simultaneously determining

A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
, efficiency ratios, and charge separation from the
data.

\subsubsubsection

[D7] Number of tag working
points

Two or more working points implemented (6 in the final multi-WP fit).
Five qualitatively different tag types (as in the published ALEPH
analysis) not feasible without secondary vertex mass.

.63
Machine-Readable Results

All numerical results are stored in machine-readable JSON format in the

results/
directory, enabling external reuse and reproducibility
checks. The files are:

•

results/fitted_parameters.json
: primary fitted parameters
(
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
,
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}
,

\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{eff}}
,
R_{b}
) on full data

•

results/uncertainties.json
: statistical and systematic
uncertainty breakdown for all observables

•

results/covariance.json
:
9\times 9
covariance matrix
(statistical,
\kappa
systematic, and total) for the

\langle Q_{\text{FB}}\rangle
measurement, with eigenvalue
decomposition and PSD verification

•

results/systematic_shifts.json
: per-source systematic shifts
on
A_{\text{FB}}^{0,b}

•

results/afb_full_data.json
: complete extraction results at
all
\kappa
values on full data, with comparisons to 10% and
expected

•

results/afb_expected.json
:
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
extraction
for all
\kappa
values (MC pseudo-data)

•

results/rb_expected.json
:
R_{b}
extraction at multiple
working points (MC pseudo-data)

•

results/closure_and_systematics.json
: closure test,
systematics, GoF, stability

•

results/afb_10pct.json
:
A_{\text{FB}}^{b}
and
R_{b}

extraction on 10% data

•

results/regression_final.json
: complete regression results
including pre/post comparison, systematic breakdown, and validation

•

results/rb_regression.json
: detailed
R_{b}
extraction at
all working points (MC pseudo-data, closure, full data, 10% data,
stability scan)

The covariance matrix has been verified to be positive semi-definite
with a condition number of 327. The eigenvalue decomposition confirms no
zero or negative eigenvalues, ensuring the matrix can be inverted for

\chi^{2}
calculations in external comparisons.

.64.1
Displaced track
distributions

The distributions of displaced track counts at multiple thresholds
provide insight into the b-tagging discrimination power before
constructing the
\chi_{\text{signed}}
variable. These distributions
confirm that the IP-based approach correctly identifies the B-hadron
lifetime signature in the open data.

Figure 214:
Distribution of tracks with
|d_{0}|>100\ \mu
m per
hemisphere for data and MC. The tail of the distribution is enriched in
b-hadron hemispheres, where multiple displaced tracks originate from the
B decay vertex. The data/MC agreement validates the MC modeling of the
track displacement spectrum.

Figure 215:
Distribution of tracks with
|d_{0}|>200\ \mu
m per
hemisphere. At this larger displacement threshold, the b-enrichment in
the tail is more pronounced and the light-flavour contamination is
reduced.

Figure 216:
Distribution of tracks with
|d_{0}|>500\ \mu
m per
hemisphere. This is the threshold used for Approach B (the cut-based
cross-check tag). The data/MC agreement is adequate for the cross-check
purpose.

.64.2
BDT diagnostics

Although the BDT approach was rejected due to data/MC disagreement on
the input variables (see Section
.51.5
), the
diagnostic plots from the BDT training are documented here for
completeness. These demonstrate both the potential discrimination power
of a multivariate approach and the limitations imposed by the data/MC
disagreement.

Figure 217:
XGBoost BDT score distribution for data and MC
(area-normalized). The shift between the data and MC distributions
exceeds the acceptable threshold for direct data application. This
disagreement arises from the combination of imperfect IP resolution
modeling for nvdet = 0 tracks and the single-year MC not perfectly
matching the multi-year data.

Figure 218:
BDT feature importance ranking for the 13 hemisphere-level
input variables. The sum of positive significances and the displaced
track multiplicity dominate, confirming that the lifetime-based
variables carry the primary discrimination power. The less-important
features contribute mainly noise and worsen the data/MC
agreement.

.64.3
Per-flavour tag
distributions

The following figures show the
\chi_{\text{signed}}
and displaced
track distributions separated by the approximate MC flavour labels,
illustrating the per-flavour tagging efficiency at different working
points.

Figure 219:
Per-flavour distribution of the sum of positive significances
(the continuous version of the
\chi_{\text{signed}}
variable). The
b-labeled events (blue) show a clear tail toward large values, while uds
events (green) are concentrated at low values. The c events (orange) lie
between, reflecting the intermediate displacement of D-hadron
decays.

Figure 220:
Per-flavour distribution of the displaced track count
(
N(|d_{0}|>500\ \mu
m)) used in Approach B. The b-labeled events have
a wider distribution extending to higher multiplicities, while uds
events are strongly peaked at zero.

|d_{0}|
per hemisphere for data and MC. The

Correlation and excess
growth

These figures provide additional views of the double-tag method
performance.

Figure 226:
Hemisphere correlation factor
C_{b}
versus

\chi_{\text{signed}}
cut threshold. The correlation increases with
tighter cuts, consistent with QCD-induced correlations becoming more

at

to

at

is accounted for in the double-tag

) shows the excess as a function of the

approach (Approach A) dominates across the full efficiency
range.

Figure 228:
ROC curves comparing the three tagging approaches against the

achieves the best overall discrimination, though the absolute AUC values
are suppressed by the label impurity. The BDT achieves slightly better
separation at intermediate working points but cannot be applied to data
due to input variable data/MC disagreement.

References

Two-Point Energy-Energy Correlator Measurement with ALEPH Data

.66
Introduction

The energy-energy correlator (EEC) is an event-shape observable that
probes the angular structure of hadronic final states in
e^{+}e^{-}

annihilation. Originally proposed by Basham, Brown, Ellis, and Love
(
[
Basham:1978bw
]
,

[
Basham:1978zq
]
), the EEC measures the
energy-weighted angular correlation between all pairs of final-state
particles. Because the EEC is infrared and collinear safe, it is
calculable in perturbative QCD and has been used extensively at LEP to
measure the strong coupling constant
\alpha_{s}
. Recent interest in
the EEC has been renewed by connections to conformal field theory,
energy flux operators, and jet substructure in hadron collider physics.

The EEC is defined as the normalized energy-weighted cross section
differential in the opening angle
\chi
between particle pairs:

{\mathrm{EEC}(\chi)=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{events}}}\sum_{\mathrm{events}}\sum_{i<j}\frac{E_{i}E_{j}}{E_{\mathrm{vis}}^{2}}\delta(\chi-\chi_{ij})}

(38)

where
E_{i}=|\vec{p}_{i}|
is the energy of particle
i
in the
massless approximation,
\chi_{ij}
is the opening angle between
particles
i
and
j
,
E_{\mathrm{vis}}=\sum_{k}E_{k}
is the total
visible energy from selected charged particles, and the sum runs over
ordered pairs
i<j
(no self-pairs). In practice, the delta function
is replaced by binning in
\chi
.

The EEC sum rule, which serves as an important cross-check, states that

{\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{EEC}(\chi)\,d\chi=\frac{1}{2}(1-H_{2})}

(39)

where
H_{2}=\sum_{i}E_{i}^{2}/E_{\mathrm{vis}}^{2}
is the normalized second
energy moment. For typical hadronic Z decays,
H_{2}\approx 0.14
,
giving an expected integral of approximately 0.43.

The asymmetry of the EEC (AEEC) is defined as:

{\mathrm{AEEC}(\chi)=\mathrm{EEC}(\pi-\chi)-\mathrm{EEC}(\chi)}

(40)

for
0<\chi<\pi/2
. The AEEC has the important property that
leading-order hadronization corrections cancel in the difference, making
it more sensitive to
\alpha_{s}
and less dependent on non-perturbative
modelling (
[
ALEPH:1992alphas
]
;

[
Boyle1991
]
).

The perturbative QCD prediction for the EEC at next-to-leading order
takes the form (
[
Boyle1991
]
;

[
ERT:1981
]
):

{\mathrm{EEC}(\chi)=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}A(\cos\chi)+\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{2}\left[A(\cos\chi)\cdot 2\pi b_{0}\ln\frac{\mu^{2}}{Q^{2}}+B(\cos\chi)\right]}

(41)

where
A
and
B
are the first- and second-order coefficient
functions.

The mean charged particle multiplicity
\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle

is computed as the average number of selected charged particles per
event. It provides an important validation observable sharing the same
tracking efficiency corrections as the EEC. The PDG world average at the
Z pole is
\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle=20.76\pm 0.16

(
[
PDG:2024
]
), while the early
ALEPH measurement gave
20.36\pm 0.06\pm 0.79

(
[
ALEPH:1990mult
]
).

This analysis uses archived ALEPH data from the LEP1 running period
(1992–1995) at
\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV. The primary correction method
is bin-by-bin multiplicative correction factors, following the
established approach used in all published ALEPH EEC measurements
(
[
Boyle1991
]
;

[
ALEPH:1995cluster
]
). Iterative
Bayesian unfolding (IBU) is used as a cross-check. The analysis corrects
the detector-level EEC to a particle-level fiducial definition using
charged particles with
|\cos\theta|<0.95
satisfying the

highPurity
quality requirement.

This document presents the full data results (Phase 4c). The expected
results from MC pseudo-data (Phase 4a) and the 10% data validation
(Phase 4b) are retained for comparison as cross-checks. The full dataset
of 2,889,543 events constitutes the primary measurement.

.67
Data Samples

.67.1
Dataset overview

The analysis uses archived ALEPH data collected during the 1992–1995
LEP1 running period at a center-of-mass energy of
\sqrt{s}=91.2

GeV. The data are stored in ROOT files containing per-event trees with
track-level and event-level information. The files have undergone a
standard pre-selection during the archiving process (denoted by
“aftercut” in the filenames), which applies basic hadronic event
quality requirements.

.68
Event Selection

.68.1

passesAll

flag, which combines seven individual quality criteria designed for

(
[
Badea:2019QGP
]
2019). The track-level
selection requires
charge != 0
(charged particles only) and

highPurity == 1
(high-quality track reconstruction),
retaining approximately 61% of all reconstructed particles.

.68.4
Data/MC agreement on selection
variables

Data/MC comparisons were produced for all kinematic variables entering
the EEC observable, using 200,000 data events and 19,000 MC events after
the

selection. These distributions serve as the input
validation gate required before computing correction factors.

\subsubsubsection

Track-level
distributions

The track momentum distribution shows good data/MC agreement across the
full range from 0 to approximately 30 GeV, with the expected steeply
falling spectrum. The ratio panel indicates agreement within 5% in most
bins.

The polar angle distribution exhibits the characteristic dip near

\theta=\pi/2
from the barrel/endcap geometry of the ALEPH TPC.
Data/MC agreement is within 2%. The azimuthal angle distribution is approximately uniform, as expected
for an unpolarized
e^{+}e^{-}
collision. The transverse and longitudinal impact parameter distributions are
peaked sharply at zero, with tails from secondary decay tracks.

. All distributions show agreement within a few percent, validating the MC modelling of track kinematics.

The TPC hit multiplicity distribution shows a data/MC discrepancy of
5–10% near the peak. This is a known limitation of the discrete TPC
hit simulation, and its impact on the corrected EEC is evaluated as a
systematic.

The per-track weight distribution shows non-trivial structure, with
data/MC disagreement at the 5–10% level in the weight range
0.85–0.95.

The event-level distributions confirm good data/MC agreement for the

.69
Observable Definition

.69.1
Energy-energy correlator

The EEC is computed as the energy-weighted pair correlation function of
charged tracks in hadronic Z decay events. For a given angular bin

\chi_{k}
(bin center), the EEC is:

{\mathrm{EEC}(\chi_{k})=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{events}}}\sum_{\mathrm{events}}\sum_{i<j}\frac{w_{i}E_{i}\cdot w_{j}E_{j}}{E_{\mathrm{vis}}^{2}}}

(42)

where the sum includes all ordered pairs
i<j
of selected charged
tracks whose opening angle
\chi_{ij}
falls within the bin boundaries
of bin
k
. The per-track weights
w_{i}
from the
weight

branch are included because they are non-trivial (range 0.08 to 4.5,
mean approximately 1.02) and represent reconstruction-level corrections.
At generator level, all weights are 1.0. The visible energy is computed
as
E_{\mathrm{vis}}=\sum_{k}w_{k}E_{k}
using the same weighted tracks.

The histogram is normalized by the number of events and by the bin width
(in radians), yielding a differential distribution in units of
per-radian.

The EEC is computed in 36 uniform bins of 5 degrees each, covering the
angular range
\chi\in[0,180]
degrees. All 36 bins are reported,
following the convention of the Boyle thesis
(
[
Boyle1991
]
). The first bin
(
\chi\approx 0
) is sensitive to two-track resolution at detector
level and collinear singularities at theory level; the last bin
(
\chi\approx 180
) is populated primarily by back-to-back 2-jet topologies.

.69.2
Asymmetry of the EEC

The AEEC is computed from the corrected EEC as:

{\mathrm{AEEC}(\chi)=\mathrm{EEC}_{\mathrm{corrected}}(\pi-\chi)-\mathrm{EEC}_{\mathrm{corrected}}(\chi)}

(43)

for
\chi\in[0,90]
degrees, yielding 18 bins. The AEEC cancels
leading-order non-perturbative corrections and provides enhanced
sensitivity to
\alpha_{s}
.

.69.3
Mean charged multiplicity

The mean charged multiplicity is computed as:

{\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{events}}}\sum_{\mathrm{events}}n_{\mathrm{ch}}^{\mathrm{event}}}

(44)

where
n_{\mathrm{ch}}^{\mathrm{event}}
is the number of selected charged
tracks (charge
\neq 0
, highPurity) per event. The corrected mean
multiplicity is obtained by applying a single multiplicative correction
factor:

{\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle_{\mathrm{corrected}}=\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle_{\mathrm{data}}\times\frac{\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle_{\mathrm{gen,MC}}}{\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle_{\mathrm{reco,MC}}}}

(45)

.69.4
Particle-level fiducial
definition

The measurement is defined at particle level as all primary charged
particles satisfying
|\cos\theta|<0.95
and the
highPurity

quality criterion. “Primary charged particles” are those produced in
fragmentation/hadronization plus daughters of particles with lifetime

c\tau<10
mm. Particles with
c\tau>10
mm (e.g.,
K_{S}^{0}
,

\Lambda
) are treated as stable and their charged daughters are not
included at particle level. At detector level, however, these daughters
are reconstructed as tracks; the bin-by-bin correction factors absorb
this mismatch.

The visible energy
E_{\mathrm{vis}}
at particle level is computed from
selected charged particles only (no ISR photons or neutral particles
contribute). This is a charged-particle EEC; comparisons to calorimetric
or full-event EEC measurements must account for this difference.

.70
Correction Procedure

.70.1
Bin-by-bin correction
factors

The primary correction method uses bin-by-bin multiplicative correction
factors derived from the MC simulation, following the standard approach
employed in all published ALEPH EEC measurements
(
[
Boyle1991
]
;

[
ALEPH:1995cluster
]
,

[
ALEPH:1992alphas
]
). The correction factor for each
angular bin is:

{C(\chi_{k})=\frac{\mathrm{EEC}_{\mathrm{gen}}(\chi_{k})}{\mathrm{EEC}_{\mathrm{reco}}(\chi_{k})}}

(46)

where
\mathrm{EEC}_{\mathrm{gen}}
is computed from generator-level
particles in the
tgen
tree (752,301 events, all weights unity)
and
\mathrm{EEC}_{\mathrm{reco}}
from reconstructed MC in the

t
tree (712,705 events passing
passesAll
, with
per-track reconstruction weights).

The
tgen
and
t
trees are matched 1:1 by entry index:
every reconstructed event has a corresponding generator-level event. At
generator level, the
passesAll
flag is not populated (always
False), so all
tgen
entries are used for the numerator. The
gen-level event selection is implicit:
tgen
only contains
events whose reco counterpart was stored in the pre-selected ROOT files.

The corrected data EEC is then:

{\mathrm{EEC}_{\mathrm{corrected}}(\chi_{k})=C(\chi_{k})\times\mathrm{EEC}_{\mathrm{data}}(\chi_{k})}

(47)

Table
177
summarizes the correction
factors across the angular range.

Table 177:
