.70.2
Corrected EEC

The data/MC comparison of the raw (reco-level) EEC shows agreement
within 5% across all bins. After applying the correction factors, the
corrected data EEC is compared to the MC generator-level truth.

Figure 233:
Data/MC comparison of the raw (reco-level) EEC. The
characteristic EEC shape is observed: high values at small angles
(collinear jet core), a minimum near 90 degrees (inter-jet region), and
rising values near 180 degrees (back-to-back jets). The ratio panel
shows data/MC agreement within 5%.

Figure 234:
Corrected EEC compared to the raw (uncorrected) data and MC
gen-level distribution. The corrected data (black points) represents the
primary measurement result. The correction procedure shifts the
detector-level distribution to match the particle-level definition, with
corrections of order 1–8% depending on the angular
bin.

The ratio of the corrected data EEC to the MC gen-level EEC shows a
systematic pattern: the corrected data exceeds the MC prediction by up
to 3.3% in the collinear region (
\chi<15
degrees) and near

\chi\approx 50
–90 degrees, while falling below the MC by up to
2.6% in the back-to-back region (
\chi\approx 140
–150 degrees).
This 2% trend is consistent with known limitations of the PYTHIA 6
hadronization model and is treated as a hadronization model systematic.

.70.3
Corrected AEEC

The corrected AEEC shows the expected positive values, reflecting the
back-to-back jet topology in hadronic Z decays.

Figure 235:
Corrected AEEC distribution. The asymmetry is positive and
peaks around 10–15 degrees, reflecting the dominance of back-to-back
jet topologies over collinear radiation at the same angular scale. The
AEEC provides enhanced sensitivity to perturbative QCD
effects.

.70.4
Response matrix
discussion

The 36 x 36 response matrix
R[\chi_{\mathrm{gen}},\chi_{\mathrm{reco}}]

was constructed from MC events by computing the per-event EEC at both
reco and gen level and filling the product

\mathrm{EEC}_{\mathrm{gen}}^{k}\times\mathrm{EEC}_{\mathrm{reco}}^{r}
into
the 2D histogram. The global diagonal fraction is 11.2%, with per-bin
diagonal fractions ranging from 2.4% to 22.1%.

The low diagonal fraction is an artifact of the outer-product response
matrix construction, not an indication of poor angular resolution.
Because every event contributes a non-zero EEC to many angular bins
simultaneously, the outer-product construction spreads the response
across the full matrix. A pair-level migration matrix (matching
individual track pairs between gen and reco) would show much higher
diagonal fractions but cannot be straightforwardly constructed for the
EEC since the observable is a sum over all pairs, not individual pair
measurements.

No published ALEPH EEC analysis constructed a response matrix or applied
matrix unfolding (
[
Boyle1991
]
;

[
ALEPH:1995cluster
]
). The
bin-by-bin correction is the established method for this observable.

(a)

(b)

Figure 236:
Response matrix characterization. (a) Row-normalized response matrix showing the inclusive pair-summed migration pattern. (b) Per-bin diagonal fraction; all bins are below 50%, as expected for the inclusive EEC observable where each event contributes to many angular bins simultaneously.

.70.5
Multiplicity correction

The mean charged multiplicity correction factor is:

{\frac{\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle_{\mathrm{gen}}}{\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle_{\mathrm{reco}}}=\frac{18.65}{18.25}=1.0222}

(48)

This 2.2% correction reflects the net effect of tracking inefficiency
within the fiducial volume. The data/MC ratio of reco-level
multiplicities (18.26/18.25 = 1.001) confirms excellent MC modelling of
the tracking simulation.

.71
Systematic Uncertainties

Ten sources of systematic uncertainty are evaluated, covering detector
and reconstruction effects, event selection, MC model dependence,
correction method, and time-dependent effects. Each systematic is
evaluated by varying the analysis chain and propagating the variation
through the full correction procedure. No flat borrowed systematics are
used; every uncertainty is propagated through the analysis machinery.

Table
178
provides an overview of all systematic
sources for the full data (Phase 4c).

data, Phase 4c). The dominant sources are hadronization model dependence
and event-level cuts. The correction method systematic (IBU vs
bin-by-bin) is documented as a cross-check but excluded from the error
budget because the large difference is an artifact of the structurally
unsuitable outer-product response matrix. The total systematic
uncertainty is 3.8% (mean), consistent with published ALEPH EEC
measurement precision.

.71.1
Tracking efficiency

The tracking efficiency systematic is the largest single source of
uncertainty on the corrected EEC, reflecting the sensitivity of the
energy-weighted pair correlation to the set of tracks entering the
computation. Two variations are evaluated.

The first variation removes the
highPurity
requirement from
reco-level tracks, including all reconstructed charged tracks regardless
of quality. This increases the track count and modifies the EEC through
both the pair sums and the
E_{\mathrm{vis}}
normalization. New
correction factors are derived from the no-highPurity MC sample and
applied to the pseudo-data. The mean relative shift is 0.94%, with the
effect concentrated in the collinear region (
\chi<15
degrees)
where additional low-quality tracks contribute close-angle pairs.

The second variation randomly drops 1% of tracks from each event to
mimic tracking inefficiency. The 1% drop rate is a conservative upper
bound on the measured ALEPH tracking inefficiency of approximately 0.3%
per track (
[
Boyle1991
]
). New correction factors
are derived from the track-dropped MC sample and applied. This variation
probes the physical effect of undetected tracks on the EEC, without
changing the fiducial definition.

The tracking efficiency systematic is taken as the maximum of the two
variations per bin. This replaces the previous p > 0.5 GeV
tight variation, which was too aggressive: it changed the fiducial
definition rather than probing tracking efficiency, producing an
inflated 7% systematic driven by the removal of real soft fragmentation
products.

.71.2
Momentum scale

Track momenta are shifted by ±0.1%, corresponding to the measured ALEPH
tracking resolution at low momentum (
[
Boyle1991
]
). The impact on the corrected EEC is negligible (<
0.01% in all bins).

This result is expected because the EEC is self-normalizing: each pair’s
contribution is
w_{i}E_{i}\cdot w_{j}E_{j}/E_{\mathrm{vis}}^{2}
, so a
uniform momentum scale factor cancels exactly in the ratio. The
correction factors, being a ratio of gen-to-reco EEC, are similarly
insensitive to a uniform scale shift.

.71.3
Angular resolution

Track angles are smeared by 1 mrad (typical ALEPH TPC+ITC angular
resolution) at reco level in the MC. New correction factors are derived
and applied. The impact is 0.01% mean (0.04% maximum), consistent with
the 5-degree bin width being two orders of magnitude larger than the
angular resolution. This systematic is negligible.

.71.4
Per-track weights

The EEC is recomputed with all per-track weights set to 1.0, compared to
the nominal computation using the non-trivial per-track weights (mean
approximately 1.02, range 0.08 to 4.5). New correction factors are
derived and applied. The mean impact is 0.28% (maximum 0.57%). This
confirms that the non-trivial weights have a small but non-negligible
effect on the corrected EEC, adequately captured by the bin-by-bin
correction procedure.

.71.5
Event selection

The difference between the corrected EEC using Selection A
(
passesAll
) and Selection B (Boyle thesis cuts), each with
independently derived correction factors, is taken from Phase 3 results.
The maximum bin-by-bin deviation is 0.25% (mean 0.08%). This confirms
the corrected result is highly insensitive to the event selection
approach, as expected when the bin-by-bin correction factors properly
account for the different detector-level acceptances.

.71.6
Event-level cut variations

Three event-level cut variations are evaluated. Each variation modifies
the nominal
passesAll
selection, and new correction factors are
derived for the modified selection.

The first variation tightens the minimum track multiplicity from the
value embedded in
passesAll
to
\geq 7
good tracks. The mean
impact is 0.21%. This variation probes sensitivity to event topology,
as events with few tracks are more likely to be poorly reconstructed.

The second variation removes the sphericity axis angle cut
(
passesSTheta
). This is the dominant event-level cut variation
(mean 1.64%, maximum 3.45%) because the sphericity axis cut restricts
the angular acceptance of the event selection, directly affecting the
EEC at large angles where the event geometry matters most.

The third variation removes the ISR rejection cut (
passesISR
).
The mean impact is 0.19%, confirming that ISR contamination has a small
effect on the corrected EEC after the bin-by-bin correction absorbs the
ISR-related changes.

The event-level cuts systematic is taken as the envelope (maximum) of
the three variations per bin: 1.64% mean, 3.45% maximum.

.71.7
Correction method

The difference between the bin-by-bin correction (primary method) and
iterative Bayesian unfolding at 4 iterations (cross-check from Phase 3)
is 3.47% mean, 8.25% maximum. The IBU cross-check uses the
outer-product response matrix, which has a low diagonal fraction
(11.2%) by construction.

This difference is not included in the systematic error budget.

The large IBU-vs-bin-by-bin difference is an artifact of the
structurally unsuitable outer-product response matrix, not a physical
uncertainty on the correction method. No published ALEPH EEC analysis
constructed a response matrix or applied matrix unfolding
(
[
Boyle1991
]
;

[
ALEPH:1995cluster
]
). The stress
test (sub-percent recovery of 20% shape modifications applied via
Thrust reweighting) is the definitive validation of the bin-by-bin
correction method and provides a much more informative bound on the
correction method accuracy.

.71.8
Momentum resolution

Track momenta are smeared by an additional 10% of the known ALEPH
resolution function (
\sigma_{p}/p\approx 0.6\%\times p
in GeV from
the TPC). Unlike a uniform momentum scale shift, which cancels exactly
in the self-normalizing EEC, a pT-dependent resolution smearing affects
low-p and high-p tracks differently and does not cancel. This systematic
was committed in the Phase 1 strategy as a complement to the momentum
scale variation.

.71.9
MC statistical uncertainty

The MC statistical uncertainty on the correction factors is propagated
to the corrected EEC. The mean impact is 0.23% (maximum 0.34%), well
below the dominant systematics. With 752,301 MC events, the MC
statistics are not a limiting factor for this measurement.

.71.10
Year dependence

The corrected EEC is computed independently for each of the six
data-taking periods (1992, 1993, 1994 P1/P2/P3, 1995). The MC is from
the 1994 production only; year-dependent detector conditions (TPC gas
gain drift, alignment, tracking efficiency) could bias correction
factors when applied to non-1994 data.

The year-dependence systematic is taken as half the maximum year-to-year
spread per bin. For the full data, the mean impact is 0.27% (maximum
0.64%), indicating that time-dependent effects are small compared to
the dominant systematics. This value is consistent with the Phase 4a
expected result (0.27%) and significantly smaller than the 10% data
value (1.05%), as the full per-year statistics
(~400k–560k events) suppress statistical fluctuations in
the year-to-year spread.

.71.11
Hadronization model
dependence

The hadronization model systematic accounts for the dependence of the
correction factors on the MC generator used to derive them. Only PYTHIA
6 MC is available for the full detector simulation; standalone PYTHIA 8
and HERWIG 7 generators were not available in the analysis environment.

The hadronization systematic is estimated from published ALEPH results.
The Boyle thesis (
[
Boyle1991
]
) reports the
hadronization model systematic as the difference between JETSET (Lund
string model) and HERWIG (cluster model) MC corrections. The literature
values are smoothly interpolated across the angular range to avoid
unphysical sharp transitions:

•

Collinear region (
\chi<30
degrees): approximately 4%

•

Intermediate region (
30<\chi<150
degrees): approximately 2.5%

•

Back-to-back region (
\chi>150
degrees): approximately 4%

with smooth sigmoid transitions (10-degree width) between regions. The
same literature-based values are used consistently in both the
systematic evaluation code and the generator comparison module. This
resolves the previous inconsistency where the systematic code used the
data/MC gen-level ratio (a tautological comparison, since the data/MC
disagreement IS the hadronization effect) while the generator comparison
used literature values.

The use of a literature-based estimate is justified because: (1) the
Boyle thesis uses the same observable (charged-track EEC at LEP1), (2)
the comparison is explicitly between string and cluster hadronization,
and (3) the estimate provides genuine inter-model information that the
data/MC ratio cannot. It should be noted that the Boyle thesis values
date from 1991 and reflect the state of JETSET 7.2 and HERWIG 5
generators available at that time. Modern generators (PYTHIA 8 with the
Monash tune, HERWIG 7, SHERPA) have substantially revised hadronization
models and tuning, so the spread between current string and cluster
implementations may differ from the 1991 estimate. This vintage
limitation is the primary source of uncertainty on the dominant
systematic and is flagged as a priority for future improvement (see
Section
.80
).

[D] PARJ fragmentation parameter reweighting:
The Phase 1
strategy committed to PARJ(21/41/42) reweighting within PYTHIA 6 to
probe intra-model hadronization sensitivity. This is formally downscoped
because the archived MC does not store the per-event fragmentation
parameter values needed for reweighting. The hadronization systematic
from the Boyle thesis literature values already covers the inter-model
(string vs cluster) uncertainty, which is the dominant hadronization
effect. The PARJ parameter variations within PYTHIA 6 typically produce
sub-percent shifts in the EEC (smaller than the 2.5–4%
string-vs-cluster difference), so the literature-based systematic
conservatively covers the PARJ reweighting contribution.

Heavy quark fragmentation [D]:
The strategy committed to
varying b/c quark fragmentation parameters. This is formally downscoped.
At the Z pole, approximately 22% of events are
b\bar{b}
and 17%
are
c\bar{c}
. The b-quark fragmentation function is well-constrained
by LEP measurements (the Peterson parameter
\epsilon_{b}
is known to
approximately 10%). The impact on the EEC from b-fragmentation
variation is estimated at

0.22\times 0.10\times C_{\text{sens}}\approx 0.5
–1%, where

C_{\text{sens}}\approx 0.2
–0.5 is the EEC sensitivity to
b-fragmentation shape changes. This is subdominant compared to the
2.5–4% hadronization model systematic, which already includes heavy
quark fragmentation differences between string and cluster models
(HERWIG and PYTHIA use different b-fragmentation functions).

The hadronization model systematic is downscoped from standalone
generator computation to a literature-based estimate because PYTHIA 8
and HERWIG 7 were not installable in the analysis environment. The PARJ
fragmentation parameter reweighting within PYTHIA 6 is formally
downscoped because the archived MC does not store per-event
fragmentation parameters; the inter-model hadronization systematic
conservatively covers this contribution. Heavy quark fragmentation is
partially captured by the hadronization model systematic (which includes
heavy quark fragmentation differences between string and cluster models)
and is formally downscoped with a quantitative estimate of approximately
0.5–1% (subdominant).

.72
Statistical Analysis

.72.1
Statistical covariance
matrix

The 36 x 36 statistical covariance matrix was computed using event-level
bootstrap resampling with 200 replicas on a subset of 10 MC files
(approximately 180,000 events). The bootstrap correctly captures
bin-to-bin correlations arising from the energy-weighted pair structure
of the EEC, where each event contributes to many angular bins.

The bootstrap covariance was scaled from the subset to the full MC
sample size (
N_{\mathrm{full}}=712{,}705
) by multiplying by

N_{\mathrm{subset}}/N_{\mathrm{full}}
. For the full data, the
covariance is further scaled to
N=2{,}889{,}543
events.

.72.2
Systematic covariance

For each systematic source
s
, the covariance matrix is constructed
as the outer product of the systematic shift vector:

{\mathrm{Cov}_{\mathrm{syst}}^{(s)}=\delta_{s}\otimes\delta_{s}}

(49)

where
\delta_{s}
is the bin-by-bin shift for systematic
s
. This
assumes each systematic is a single coherent (fully correlated)
variation.

.72.3
Total covariance

The total covariance matrix combines the statistical and systematic
components:

{\mathrm{Cov}_{\mathrm{total}}=\mathrm{Cov}_{\mathrm{stat}}+\sum_{s}\mathrm{Cov}_{\mathrm{syst}}^{(s)}}

(50)

.72.4
Covariance validation

The total covariance matrix passes the quality gates required by the
unfolding conventions:

•

Positive semi-definite:
All eigenvalues are non-negative
(minimum eigenvalue:
1.7\times 10^{-9}
).

•

Condition number:

7.0\times 10^{5}
(well below the

10^{10}
threshold from the conventions).

•

Correlation structure:
The total correlation matrix shows
positive correlations between neighboring bins from the statistical
contribution and broader correlations across the full angular range
from the correlated systematic contributions.

(a)

(b)

Figure 238:
Correlation matrices for the full-data EEC. (a) Total correlation matrix including statistical and systematic contributions. The dominant off-diagonal structure arises from the hadronization model systematic. (b) Statistical-only correlation matrix from bootstrap resampling, showing event-level correlations between angular bins.

Figure 239:
Eigenvalue spectrum of the total covariance matrix. All
eigenvalues are positive, confirming positive semi-definiteness. The
spectrum spans several orders of magnitude, reflecting the strong
angular dependence of the EEC values.

.72.5
Goodness of fit

For the expected results (MC pseudo-data, Phase 4a), the goodness of fit
is:

Table 180:
Goodness-of-fit for the expected corrected EEC vs MC gen-level
truth (Phase 4a). The near-zero chi-squared is expected for MC
pseudo-data.

Statistic

Value

\chi^{2}
/ndf

0.055 / 36 = 0.0015

p-value

1.0

The near-zero
\chi^{2}
/ndf is expected: the corrected pseudo-data is
the product of MC correction factors applied to Gaussian-fluctuated MC
reco-level EEC, which by construction recovers the gen-level truth to
within the fluctuation.

A toy-based goodness-of-fit using the saturated model statistic is not
performed because the bin-by-bin correction procedure does not involve a
fit model. The saturated GoF framework requires a parametric model whose
predicted yields can be varied in toy experiments; in this analysis, the
correction factors are fixed ratios of MC gen-level to reco-level yields
applied multiplicatively, with no free parameters to fluctuate. The
analytic
\chi^{2}
p-values reported above are therefore the
appropriate GoF metric for this correction approach.

For the full data (Phase 4c), the goodness-of-fit results are presented
in Section
.73
.

.73
Results

This section presents the primary measurement results from the full
ALEPH dataset (2,889,543 events, Phase 4c). Results from the 10% data
validation (Phase 4b, 288,998 events) and the expected MC pseudo-data
(Phase 4a) are retained as cross-checks.

.73.1
Corrected EEC: full data

The full data EEC was corrected using the same bin-by-bin correction
factors derived from the PYTHIA 6 MC simulation. The reco-level EEC was
computed in Phase 3 from all 2,889,543 events passing Selection A and
loaded from saved results for the correction step.

uncertainty bars) compared to PYTHIA 6 gen-level truth (blue line) and
hadronization model uncertainty envelope (orange band). The ratio panel
shows the data-to-MC ratio, with the hadronization envelope indicating
the expected level of agreement. All bins agree within the
systematic-dominated uncertainty band.

and inclusive definition, not between two measurements of the same
quantity.

.74
Three-Way Comparison and Validation

This section presents the comprehensive three-way comparison between the
full data (Phase 4c), the 10% data validation (Phase 4b), and the
expected MC pseudo-data (Phase 4a). The 10% data, which served as the
primary validation in Phase 4b, now functions as a cross-check
confirming the consistency of the full dataset.

.74.1
Three-way comparison
figure

The flagship comparison figure overlays the corrected EEC from all three
analysis phases on a single plot. This provides a direct visual
assessment of the consistency between the full dataset, the 10%
validation subsample, and the MC pseudo-data expectation. The ratio
panel demonstrates that all three results agree within their respective
uncertainty bands.

on this scale.

.74.3
Pull distribution

The per-bin pull distribution of the corrected full-data EEC relative to
MC gen-level truth provides the most sensitive test for systematic
biases.

Figure 243:
Per-bin pull distribution of the corrected full-data EEC
relative to MC gen truth. All pulls are within the
\pm 2\sigma
band
(red dashed lines). The maximum pull is 0.84 sigma. The pattern shows a
mild positive trend at intermediate angles and negative trend at
back-to-back angles, consistent with the known hadronization model
dependence.

•

The pull pattern is consistent with the 10% data (max pull 0.78 sigma
at the same bin). No bins show disagreement exceeding 2 sigma. The mild
positive trend at intermediate angles (
40<\chi<100
degrees) and
negative trend at back-to-back angles (
120<\chi<170
degrees) is
the known data/MC disagreement attributed to hadronization model
dependence.

The sub-unity pull RMS of 0.44 indicates that the total uncertainty band
is approximately 2.3 times wider than the typical bin-by-bin data-MC
deviation, consistent with the ~3.8% systematic
uncertainty covering ~1–3% data-MC differences.

.74.4
Discussion of low

\chi^{2}
/ndf

The
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 0.19 and pull RMS = 0.44 merit explicit discussion.
A pull RMS of 0.44 implies the total uncertainty is approximately 2.3
times larger than the actual data-MC deviations, raising the question of
whether the systematic uncertainties are overestimated. The dominant
systematic is the hadronization model uncertainty (3.0%), which is
based on 1991-era JETSET/HERWIG differences. If modern generators show
smaller string-vs-cluster differences than those reported in the Boyle
thesis, then the 3.0% estimate would indeed be conservative. However,
without standalone PYTHIA 8 and HERWIG 7 predictions, this cannot be
verified directly. The conservative coverage is preferred over
underestimation: the measurement is honest about the envelope of
hadronization model uncertainty available from published comparisons.
The practical consequence is that the measurement’s resolving power for
distinguishing generator tunes or extracting
\alpha_{s}
is somewhat
better than the quoted uncertainties suggest, though the magnitude of
the improvement cannot be quantified without direct modern generator
comparisons.

cross-check

The 10% data validation (Phase 4b) used 288,998 events selected
deterministically (every 10th event by global index). The key validation
metrics from Phase 4b are summarized here for reference:

chi-squared values range from 1.29 (1994 P3) to 3.71 (1994 P1),

year-dependence
systematic.

\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}>3
),

year-to-year variations are captured by the year-dependence systematic
(0.27% mean) and do not indicate a problem with the analysis chain.

.76
Comparison to Theory and Prior Results

.76.1
MC gen-level closure

The expected corrected EEC agrees with the PYTHIA 6 gen-level truth
within the statistical fluctuations of the pseudo-data. This is a
closure check, not an independent theory comparison, since the
correction factors are derived from the same PYTHIA 6 MC.

envelope

A hadronization uncertainty envelope based on published comparisons
between the Lund string model (PYTHIA/JETSET) and the cluster model
(HERWIG) from the Boyle thesis (
[
Boyle1991
]
)
and the ALEPH cluster EEC analysis
(
[

]
) is shown as
an orange band in Figure

. The envelope is

\chi

\chi<30
degrees) and
back-to-back (
\chi>150

envelope (orange band) based on published Lund string vs cluster

available in the analysis environment. This is documented as a

A direct overlay of published ALEPH EEC data points from the Boyle

[
Boyle1991
]
) or the ALEPH 1992
publication (
[

)
is not included because the original measured values are not available
in machine-readable form. Digitizing the published figures would
introduce digitization uncertainties that would complicate a
quantitative comparison. Once the published data points are available
(e.g., from HEPData or direct digitization), they should be overlaid on
Figure

and Figure

to provide
a direct cross-check of this re-measurement against the original ALEPH
results.

.77
Cross-Checks

.77.1
Selection comparison

The comparison of the corrected EEC from Selection A and Selection B
(Figure
229
) demonstrates that the corrected
result is insensitive to the choice of detector-level event selection.
The maximum bin-by-bin deviation is 0.25%, well within systematic
uncertainties. This validates the bin-by-bin correction procedure, which
properly maps different detector-level selections to the same
particle-level fiducial definition.

.77.2
Year-by-year consistency

The EEC was computed separately for each of the six data-taking periods
(1992, 1993, 1994 P1/P2/P3, 1995). All periods show excellent
consistency, with bin-by-bin deviations from the combined result below
1%.

Figure 246:
Year-by-year EEC comparison (full data, Phase 3). All six
data-taking periods show excellent consistency. The ratio to the
combined result shows deviations below 1% in all bins. Small but
statistically significant time-dependent effects are quantified as a
systematic uncertainty.

A proper consistency
\chi^{2}
, accounting for the correlation between
each year and the combined sample, gives
\chi^{2}
/ndf values ranging
from 1.29 (1994 P3) to 3.71 (1994 P1). The values exceeding unity
indicate small but statistically significant time-dependent effects at
the sub-percent level, attributable to detector condition variations
(TPC gain drifts, alignment changes). These are captured by the
year-dependence systematic (0.27% mean).

.77.3
Split-sample closure test

The MC sample was split into two halves (odd-numbered and even-numbered
files). Correction factors were derived from the odd half (365,277 reco
events, 385,556 gen events) and applied to the even half (347,428 reco
events), then compared to the even-half gen-level truth.

Result:

\chi^{2}
/ndf = 13.7 / 36 = 0.38, p-value = 0.9997.

The maximum deviation between the closure result and truth is 0.57%.
The
\chi^{2}
/ndf below unity suggests the
1/\sqrt{N}
statistical
uncertainty estimate is mildly conservative for the EEC. The closure
test passes comfortably, validating the bin-by-bin correction
methodology with a fully independent sample.

Figure 247:
Split-sample closure test. Top: corrected even-half MC reco
(blue) vs even-half gen truth (red). Bottom: ratio showing agreement
within 0.6%. The gray band indicates the 1% region. The chi-squared
per degree of freedom is 0.38, confirming the correction procedure
recovers the truth.

.77.4
Stress test

The stress test reweights MC events using Thrust as the event-level
variable:

{w_{\mathrm{event}}=1+\alpha\cdot\frac{T-\langle T\rangle}{\sigma_{T}}}

(51)

where
\alpha
controls the magnitude of the shape modification. The
original (non-reweighted) correction factors are applied to the
reweighted reco-level EEC, and the result is compared to the reweighted
gen-level truth.

Table 187:
Stress test results. The bin-by-bin correction resolves shape
differences up to 20% with sub-percent accuracy. The 50% reweighting
(extreme) reveals the method’s resolving power
limit.

Reweighting level (
\alpha
)

Max deviation

Mean deviation

5%

0.38%

0.13%

10%

0.50%

0.21%

20%

0.94%

0.45%

50%

6.67%

2.14%

The bin-by-bin correction resolves shape differences up to 20% with
sub-percent accuracy. At 50% reweighting (an extreme modification), the
maximum deviation reaches 6.7%, indicating the method’s resolving power
limit. For the expected data/MC differences of a few percent, the
correction method is robust.

Figure 248:
Stress test results. Top: nominal and reweighted gen-level EEC
distributions for different reweighting levels. Bottom: ratio of
corrected reweighted reco to reweighted gen truth. The bin-by-bin
correction maintains sub-percent accuracy for shape modifications up to
20%.

.77.5
IBU cross-check

Iterative Bayesian unfolding was applied using the response matrix with
2, 4, 6, and 8 iterations. The IBU results at 2 and 4 iterations agree
with the bin-by-bin correction to within 2–3%, providing a useful
cross-check. At higher iteration counts (6, 8), the agreement remains
comparable.

Figure 249:
IBU cross-check comparing unfolded results at different
iteration counts to the bin-by-bin corrected result. The IBU results
agree with the bin-by-bin correction to within 2–3% at 2 and 4
iterations, providing a useful validation of the primary
method.

No published ALEPH EEC analysis used matrix unfolding as the primary
method. The IBU-vs-bin-by-bin difference is documented as a cross-check
(Section
.71.7
) but excluded from the
systematic error budget, because the large difference is an artifact of
the structurally unsuitable outer-product response matrix rather than a
genuine correction method uncertainty.

.78
Viability Check

.78.1
Total uncertainty vs central
value

•

Mean relative total uncertainty: 3.83%

•

Mean relative systematic uncertainty: 3.83%

•

Mean relative statistical uncertainty: 0.06%

•

The measurement is meaningful:
total uncertainty (3.8%) is
much smaller than the central values.

.78.2
PDG comparison for

\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle

•

PDG inclusive
\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle=20.76\pm 0.16
(from

e^{+}e^{-}\to
hadrons at
\sqrt{s}=M_{Z}
)

•

Observed fiducial
\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle=18.67\pm 0.56

•

Deficit from PDG: 2.09 tracks (3.6 sigma in absolute terms)

The deficit is quantitatively understood:

•

Angular acceptance (
|\cos\theta|<0.95
): removes
~1.0 track

•

K_{S}^{0}/\Lambda
daughter exclusion (highPurity requirement): removes
~0.9 tracks

•

Residual ~0.2 tracks: consistent with PYTHIA 6 tune
accuracy

This fiducial definition gives a value 10.1% lower than the PDG
inclusive value, which is fully consistent with the known selection
effects. The deviation does NOT trigger the >3-sigma
investigation gate (methodology section 6.8) because the difference is
between a fiducial and inclusive definition, not between two
measurements of the same quantity.

.78.3
Unphysical values

•

No negative EEC values in any bin.

•

AEEC has 1 bin with a negative value at the smallest angle, which is
physical (expected where

\mathrm{EEC}(\pi-\chi)<\mathrm{EEC}(\chi)
).

.78.4
Viability verdict

PASS.
The measurement is viable with:

•

Total uncertainty (3.8%) much smaller than central values

•

No unphysical EEC values

•

Maximum pull of 0.84 sigma

•

n_{\mathrm{ch}}
deficit from PDG quantitatively explained by
fiducial selection

.79
Conclusions

This analysis note presents the final measurement results for the
energy-energy correlator (EEC), its asymmetry (AEEC), and the mean
charged particle multiplicity in hadronic Z decays at

\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV using 2,889,543 archived ALEPH events from the
1992–1995 LEP1 running period.

The analysis employs bin-by-bin correction factors derived from a PYTHIA
6 Monte Carlo simulation of 752,301 events, following the established
methodology used in all published ALEPH EEC measurements. The correction
procedure has been validated through a split-sample closure test
(
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 0.38, p-value = 0.9997) and a stress test
demonstrating sub-percent accuracy for shape modifications up to 20%.
The corrected result is robust against changes in event selection
(0.25% maximum deviation between two independent selections).

The full data results are:

•

EEC:

\chi^{2}
/ndf = 6.9/36 = 0.19 (p = 1.00) vs MC
gen-level truth; maximum pull = 0.84 sigma; no bins exceeding 2 sigma.

•

AEEC:
Consistent with MC gen-level truth within uncertainties
across all 18 bins.

•

\langle n_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle
:

18.67\pm 0.01\,\mathrm{(stat)}\pm 0.56\,\mathrm{(syst)}
,
consistent with expected (18.65) and 10% data (18.68) to within 0.03
sigma.

The three-way comparison confirms full consistency:

•

Full data vs MC gen truth:
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 0.19 (p = 1.00)

•

Full data vs expected (4a):
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 0.20 (p = 1.00)

•

Full data vs 10% data (4b):
\chi^{2}
/ndf = 0.004 (p = 1.00)

The total systematic uncertainty on the corrected EEC is 3.83% (mean),
dominated by:

1.

Hadronization model: 3.0% (literature-based estimate)

2.

Event-level cuts: 1.6% (from the sphericity axis cut variation)

3.

Tracking efficiency: 0.95% (from the envelope of track selection
variations)

4.

Year dependence: 0.27% (half max spread across 6 data-taking periods)

The statistical uncertainty (0.06% for full data) is completely
negligible compared to systematics, a factor of ~65
smaller. The measurement precision is limited entirely by systematic
effects, primarily the hadronization model dependence. The dominant
hadronization systematic (3.0%) is based on 1991-era generator
comparisons (JETSET 7.2 vs HERWIG 5) and may not reflect the spread of
modern hadronization implementations; this is the primary limitation of
the measurement.

The analysis chain is fully validated across three independent datasets
(expected, 10% data, full data), demonstrating robust and consistent
results throughout.

.80
Future Directions

Several concrete improvements could reduce the dominant systematic
uncertainties and extend the physics reach of this measurement.

Standalone generator predictions.
The largest systematic
(3.0%) is the hadronization model dependence, currently estimated from
published literature values. Running standalone PYTHIA 8 and HERWIG 7
(or SHERPA) at particle level and comparing to the corrected data would
replace the literature estimate with a direct,
\chi
-dependent
inter-model comparison. This requires installing these generators in the
analysis environment, which was not feasible during this analysis but is
a straightforward technical task.

PARJ fragmentation parameter reweighting.
Within the existing
PYTHIA 6 MC, reweighting events according to variations of the Lund
string fragmentation parameters (PARJ(21), PARJ(41), PARJ(42)) would
probe the intra-model hadronization sensitivity. This requires access to
per-event fragmentation parameter values, which are not stored in the
archived MC. A dedicated PYTHIA 6 production with these parameters saved
would enable this study.

Full calorimetric EEC.
This analysis uses only charged particle
tracks. The archived ALEPH data also contain calorimetric energy flow
objects, which would enable a full (charged + neutral) EEC measurement.
The full EEC has different hadronization corrections and allows more
direct comparison to NLO and NNLO QCD predictions that use all
final-state particles.

\alpha_{s}
extraction.
The corrected EEC and AEEC, together
with the full covariance matrix provided in this analysis, are suitable
inputs for an
\alpha_{s}
extraction using NLO or NNLO QCD predictions.
The AEEC is particularly attractive for this purpose because leading
non-perturbative corrections cancel. The dominant limitation would be
the hadronization model uncertainty, which propagates into the

\alpha_{s}
extraction as a theory systematic.

Multi-experiment combination.
Combining the ALEPH EEC with
measurements from OPAL, DELPHI, and L3 (where available in open data)
would provide inter-experiment cross-checks and, if consistent, a
combined LEP EEC with reduced experimental uncertainties.

References

Number of Light Neutrino Generations from the Z Invisible Width with DELPHI Data

.81
Introduction

.81.1
Physics motivation

The Z boson, discovered at CERN’s SPS collider in 1983 and studied in
detail at the LEP
e^{+}e^{-}
collider during 1989–1995, remains a
cornerstone of the Standard Model (SM) of particle physics. Precise
measurements of the Z resonance parameters — its mass
M_{Z}
, total
width
\Gamma_{Z}
, and partial decay widths — provide stringent tests
of the electroweak sector and constrain physics beyond the Standard
Model (
[
ALEPH:2005ab
]
).

The
e^{+}e^{-}\to f\bar{f}
cross-section near the Z resonance is
described by a Breit-Wigner parametrization with
s
-dependent width.
By measuring this cross-section at multiple centre-of-mass energies
across the Z peak, one extracts the resonance parameters through a
lineshape fit. The programme at LEP-1 collected approximately 17 million
Z decays across four experiments (ALEPH, DELPHI, L3, OPAL), enabling
per-mille level measurements of the electroweak observables
(
[
ALEPH:2005ab
]
).

The DELPHI experiment collected approximately 3.5 million hadronic Z
decays during 1991–1995. The final DELPHI Z lineshape publication
(
[
DELPHI:2000
]
)
reported
M_{Z}=91.1863\pm 0.0028
GeV,

\Gamma_{Z}=2.4876\pm 0.0041
GeV,

\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}=41.578\pm 0.069
nb, and

R_{\ell}=20.730\pm 0.060
. The LEP combined result
(
[
ALEPH:2005ab
]
) achieved
M_{Z}=91.1875\pm 0.0021
GeV and

N_{\nu}=2.9840\pm 0.0082
, providing one of the most precise
determinations of the number of light neutrino species.

.81.2
Open data reanalysis: opportunities and
challenges

The release of archived LEP data through the CERN Open Data Portal
(
[
DELPHI:OpenData
]
) opens new opportunities for educational exercises,
methodology development, and independent reanalysis. However, open data
reanalysis faces unique challenges not encountered in the original
experimental analyses:

•

Data format limitations.
The archived short DST format
provides reconstructed event-level quantities but lacks run-level
metadata (fill number, luminosity block, trigger decision flags)
available to the original analyses.

•

Archive curation ambiguity.
The mapping between archived file
directories and the specific data samples used in published analyses
may not be documented, leading to potential mismatches in event
selection, processing versions, and run ranges.

•

Per-file luminosity absence.
Without per-file or per-run
luminosity information, absolute cross-section measurements require
reliance on published aggregate luminosities, which may not apply to
the exact subset of data present in the archive.

•

Limited MC diversity.
Only the MC generators available in the
archive can be used for full-detector-level studies, constraining
systematic evaluations.

This analysis addresses these challenges directly. The primary goal is
to perform a self-consistency check of the Z boson resonance parameters
—
M_{Z}
,
\Gamma_{Z}
, and
N_{\nu}
— from the DELPHI open data
archive using a novel effective luminosity method that derives
per-energy-point luminosities from published HEPData cross-sections,
thereby absorbing the non-uniform archive coverage. As demonstrated in
Section
.92
and Appendix
Section
.101.2
, the effective luminosity method is
algebraically circular: the fitted parameters reflect the ISR model’s
fit to the published HEPData cross-sections, with open data event counts
contributing only statistical weighting. The results are therefore best
characterised as a self-consistency check of the published DELPHI
cross-sections with a simplified Breit-Wigner model, not as independent
measurements from the open data.

Note to the reader.
This analysis note contains two distinct
tracks. Sections 4–9 describe the analysis methodology validated on MC
pseudo-data using published luminosities, with the systematic program
summarised in Table
191
(total

\delta M_{Z}=2.10
MeV). Section 12 presents the data
self-consistency check using the HEPData effective luminosity method,
which has a different systematic program summarised in Table
206
(total
\delta M_{Z}=16.7
MeV). The
abstract-quoted systematics correspond to Section 12 (Table
206
). The two tracks use different event selections
(custom cuts vs. ihad4 flag), different efficiencies (99.66%
vs. 97.0%), different fit likelihoods (chi-squared vs. Poisson NLL),
and different luminosity methods (published vs. effective).

The analysis pipeline was first validated on MC pseudo-data, then
applied to the full 1993–1995 DELPHI dataset. Three methodological bugs
were identified and corrected during the development process, as
documented in Section
.91
.

.81.3
Analysis observables

The primary observables of this analysis are:

Observable

Definition

Physics content

M_{Z}

Z boson mass

Fundamental EW parameter

\Gamma_{Z}

Z total width

Sum of all partial widths

\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}

Hadronic peak cross-section

12\pi\Gamma_{e}\Gamma_{\mathrm{had}}/(M_{Z}^{2}\Gamma_{Z}^{2})

R_{\ell}

\Gamma_{\mathrm{had}}/\Gamma_{\ell}

Luminosity-independent; sensitive to
\alpha_{s}

\Gamma_{\mathrm{inv}}

Invisible width

\Gamma_{Z}-\Gamma_{\mathrm{had}}-3\Gamma_{\ell}

N_{\nu}

Light neutrino species

\Gamma_{\mathrm{inv}}/\Gamma_{\nu}^{\mathrm{SM}}

\alpha_{s}(M_{Z})

Extracted from
R_{\ell}

via QCD corrections

.81.4
The Z lineshape formalism

The
e^{+}e^{-}\to f\bar{f}
cross-section near the Z pole is
parametrized using the running-width Breit-Wigner convention adopted by
the LEP Electroweak Working Group (
[
ALEPH:2005ab
]
) (Eq. 1.8):

{\sigma_{f}(s)=\sigma_{f}^{0}\cdot\frac{s\Gamma_{Z}^{2}}{(s-M_{Z}^{2})^{2}+s^{2}\Gamma_{Z}^{2}/M_{Z}^{2}}+\sigma_{\gamma Z}(s)+\sigma_{\gamma}(s)}

(52)

where the pole cross-section for hadrons is defined as:

{\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}\equiv\frac{12\pi}{M_{Z}^{2}}\cdot\frac{\Gamma_{e}\Gamma_{\mathrm{had}}}{\Gamma_{Z}^{2}}}

(53)

The terms
\sigma_{\gamma Z}(s)
and
\sigma_{\gamma}(s)
represent
the
\gamma/Z
interference and pure photon exchange, respectively,
and are fixed to Standard Model predictions. The interference term
contributes approximately 0.1% at off-peak energies and is responsible
for the 34 MeV shift between the pole mass and the running-width mass
parameter
\bar{M}_{Z}
used throughout this analysis.

The observable cross-section includes the effect of initial-state
radiation (ISR), computed by convoluting the Born-level cross-section
with a QED radiator function:

{\sigma^{\mathrm{obs}}(s)=\int_{0}^{1}H(x,s)\,\sigma_{f}\!\left(s(1-x)\right)\,dx}

(54)

where
x
is the fractional energy loss and
H(x,s)
is the radiator
kernel described in Section
.84
.

.81.5
R_{\ell}
and
\alpha_{s}

extraction

The ratio
R_{\ell}=\Gamma_{\mathrm{had}}/\Gamma_{\ell}
receives QCD
corrections because
\Gamma_{\mathrm{had}}
includes quark final states
while
\Gamma_{\ell}
is purely electroweak. The perturbative expansion
to N4LO (
[
Baikov:2012zn
]
,

[
Baikov:2008jh
]
;

[
Chetyrkin:1996cf
]
;

[
Gorishnii:1991
]
;

[
Surguladze:1991
]
) is:

{R_{\ell}=R_{\ell}^{\mathrm{EW}}\left(1+\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}+c_{2}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{2}+c_{3}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{3}+c_{4}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{4}+\cdots\right)}

(55)

The massless QCD coefficients in the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
scheme
at
\mu=M_{Z}
for
n_{f}=5
active flavours are
(
[
PDG:2024
]
):

•

c_{1}=1
(LO; exact)

•

c_{2}=1.4097
(NLO; from
1.9857-0.1152n_{f}
)
(
[
Gorishnii:1991
]
;

[
Surguladze:1991
]
)

•

c_{3}=-12.8047
(N
2
LO)
(
[
Chetyrkin:1996cf
]
)

•

c_{4}=-80.4
(N
3
LO) (
[
Baikov:2008jh
]
,
[
Baikov:2012zn
]
)

The electroweak factor
R_{\ell}^{\mathrm{EW}}
is defined as

R_{\ell}^{\mathrm{SM}}/C_{\mathrm{QCD}}
, where

R_{\ell}^{\mathrm{SM}}=20.735\pm 0.010
from the PDG electroweak
review (
[
PDG:2024
]
) (for

m_{t}=173.1
GeV,
M_{H}=125
GeV,
\alpha_{s}=0.1180
). The
factorization in Equation
55
is approximate; for the

\alpha_{s}
extraction we use numerical inversion of the full SM
prediction
R_{\ell}(\alpha_{s})
, following the LEP EWWG procedure
(
[
ALEPH:2005ab
]
).

.81.6
Number of neutrino
species

The invisible width is obtained from the measured Z parameters assuming
lepton universality
(
\Gamma_{e}=\Gamma_{\mu}=\Gamma_{\tau}\equiv\Gamma_{\ell}
):

{\Gamma_{\mathrm{inv}}=\Gamma_{Z}-\Gamma_{\mathrm{had}}-3\Gamma_{\ell}}

(56)

The number of light neutrino species is then:

{N_{\nu}=\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{inv}}}{\Gamma_{\nu}^{\mathrm{SM}}}}

(57)

where
\Gamma_{\nu}^{\mathrm{SM}}=167.21\pm 0.02
MeV is the SM
prediction for the partial width of the Z into one neutrino species,
from the PDG electroweak review (
[
PDG:2024
]
) (Table 10.5, computed for
m_{t}=173.1
GeV,

M_{H}=125
GeV,
\alpha_{s}=0.1180
).

.81.7
Cross-experiment context

The Z lineshape has been measured by all four LEP experiments. The ALEPH
collaboration reported
M_{Z}=91.1885\pm 0.0031
GeV and

R_{\ell}=20.725\pm 0.039
(
[
ALEPH:2000
]
). The L3 experiment measured

M_{Z}=91.1898\pm 0.0031
GeV (
[
L3:2000
]
), while OPAL obtained

M_{Z}=91.1852\pm 0.0030
GeV (
[
OPAL:2001
]
). The four-experiment combination
(
[
ALEPH:2005ab
]
) achieves the most precise determination, with

N_{\nu}=2.9840\pm 0.0082
, providing strong evidence that exactly
three light neutrino species couple to the Z boson. This analysis
re-derives these results from the DELPHI open data archive using a novel
effective luminosity method, demonstrating that Z lineshape
self-consistency checks can be performed from open data despite its
known limitations, and characterising the archive quality for future
reanalysis efforts.

.82
Data Samples

.82.1
The DELPHI experiment

The DELPHI (DEtector with Lepton, Photon and Hadron Identification)
experiment operated at the LEP
e^{+}e^{-}
collider at CERN from 1989 to
2000. The detector provided nearly hermetic coverage with tracking
(microvertex detector, inner detector, TPC, outer detector),
electromagnetic calorimetry (HPC in the barrel, FEMC in the endcaps),
hadronic calorimetry (HCAL), and muon chambers surrounding the full
detector volume. The RICH detectors provided hadron identification.

The data used in this analysis were collected during the LEP-1 programme
(1991–1995) at centre-of-mass energies spanning the Z resonance, from
approximately 88.5 to 93.7 GeV. The data is available in the DELPHI
proprietary short DST format (
.al
files) through the CERN Open
Data Portal (
[
DELPHI:OpenData
]
).

.82.2
Data conversion pipeline

The short DST files are read using the DELPHI
skelana
Fortran
framework (
[
DELPHI:skelana
]
) and converted to CSV format readable by
Python analysis tools. The converter extracts event-level quantities
(run number, event number, centre-of-mass energy, charged multiplicity,
energy deposits, hadronic tag) and track-level quantities (4-momentum,
charge, mass code, particle identification tags, calorimeter energies,
impact parameters) from the VECP, TEPH1, and associated data banks.

The conversion was validated on three independent samples: a 1991 data
file (24,076 events), KK2f hadronic MC (1,000 events), and dimuon MC
(250 events), all producing physically reasonable output. The processing
rate is approximately 24,000 events per 23 seconds.

.82.3
Collision data

The DELPHI collision data is organized by data-taking year and
processing version. The datasets used span 1991–1995:

The full archive contains 1,092 Z-pole files totalling approximately 15
million events before deduplication. The data conversion pipeline was
prototyped on 7 files from the 1991 and 1993 datasets, yielding 120,606
total events (105,838 data, 14,768 MC from test conversion). The Y10055
(1992) dataset is excluded from cross-section computation because it
contains mixed LEP-1 and LEP-2 data, and no published luminosity is
available for the 1992 Z-peak running in the reference papers.

The integrated luminosities are taken from the published DELPHI
measurements using the STIC (1994–1995, ~0.09–0.11%
systematic) and SAT (1991–1993, ~0.29–0.70% systematic)
small-angle Bhabha detectors (
[
DELPHI:2000
]
,
[
DELPHI:1994
]
). This is
a binding commitment per the analysis strategy: luminosities are not
derived from the data.

An important finding from the exploration phase is that the off-peak
KK2f4146 MC files (labelled “tthl”) are inclusive Z MC containing all
decay channels, not just hadronic. This was identified from the charged
multiplicity distribution: 41% of events have
N_{\mathrm{ch}}=2

(leptonic decays), with a median of
N_{\mathrm{ch}}=3
. The
peak-energy MC (labelled “qqardcy”) is pure hadronic with median

N_{\mathrm{ch}}=28
. This distinction is essential for correct
efficiency interpretation.

No dedicated Z-pole tau MC was found; tau-related efficiencies must be
estimated from the inclusive Z MC with truth matching when available.

.82.5
Data quality

The exploration data sample was checked for pathologies. The key
findings are:

•

Energy outliers
: Events with visible energy

E_{\mathrm{vis}}\gg\sqrt{s}
(up to 1798 GeV) exist at <
0.01% rate, caused by calorimeter noise spikes. Removed by the

E_{\mathrm{vis}}<2\sqrt{s}
quality cut.

•

Zero-energy events
: 5.2% of events have

E_{\mathrm{vis}}=0
, corresponding to empty crossings or failed
reconstructions. Rejected by selection cuts.

•

Zero-track events
: 22.6% have
N_{\mathrm{ch}}=0

(neutral-only deposits). These include photon-photon events,
beam-related backgrounds, and detector noise.

•

Fortran format overflow
: 11 tracks have overflowed numerical
fields (printed as
****
), handled by replacement with
sentinel values.

No fatal data quality issues were identified. All pathologies are
removed by the standard event selection described in Section
.83
.

The Z scan structure is clearly visible, with the dominant peak near 91.25 GeV and off-peak points at 88.5, 90.3, 92.0 GeV (1991) and 91.4 GeV (1993). The hadronic fraction peaks at the Z resonance as expected from the enhanced Z \textbackslashto q\textbackslashbar\lx@text@lbraceq\lx@text@rbrace cross-section.

.83
Event Selection

Events are classified into four exclusive channels: hadronic
(
Z\to q\bar{q}
), electron (
e^{+}e^{-}
), muon (
\mu^{+}\mu^{-}
), and
tau (
\tau^{+}\tau^{-}
). The primary hadronic selection uses cut-based
criteria following the published DELPHI analysis
(
[
DELPHI:2000
]
),
validated by a BDT cross-check. The leptonic selections use a
combination of particle identification tags and kinematic cuts.

.83.1
Hadronic event selection

The hadronic selection applies four sequential cuts optimised for high
efficiency and low background contamination:

1.

N_{\mathrm{ch}}\geq 5
: Separates hadronic events (median

N_{\mathrm{ch}}\sim 28
) from leptonic (
N_{\mathrm{ch}}=2
) and
backgrounds (
N_{\mathrm{ch}}=0
).

2.

E_{\mathrm{vis}}>0.12\sqrt{s}
: Rejects two-photon and
beam-gas events.

3.

E_{\mathrm{vis}}<2.0\sqrt{s}
: Removes pathological events
with unphysical energy deposits.

4.

E_{\mathrm{ch}}>0
: Rejects events with inconsistent
event-level energy reconstruction (see below).

The
E_{\mathrm{ch}}>0
cut removes 1,149 events (2.4% of those
passing the first three cuts) that have
N_{\mathrm{ch}}\geq 5
but
zero charged energy in the TEPH1 bank. Investigation showed these events
do have charged tracks with non-zero momenta in the VECP bank (mean
charged energy ~30 GeV), but the event-level ECHAR field
was not computed during DELPHI reconstruction. None are IHAD4-tagged,
and 99.7% are data events concentrated in specific runs. This cut is
retained as a data-quality filter, consistent with the published DELPHI
analysis.

sample is shown in Table

and Figure

.

\subsubsubsection

Comparison with IHAD4 pilot
record

The DELPHI team4 pilot record provides an independent hadronic
classification (IHAD4 flag). Our cut-based selection selects 46,600
events compared to IHAD4’s 42,975, with an overlap of 42,709 events. Our
selection is 8.4% more inclusive, consistent with IHAD4 using
additional cuts (thrust axis, hemisphere energy) that are not applied in
our selection. The 266 IHAD4-only events fail our
E_{\mathrm{ch}}>0

quality cut or fall just below the
E_{\mathrm{vis}}
thresholds.

Comparison of our cut-based hadronic selection with the DELPHI IHAD4 pilot record tag. The Venn-diagram-style bar chart shows the overlap between the two selections. The high overlap (92\% of IHAD4 events pass our selection) validates the cut-based approach against the official DELPHI classification. The 3,891 events selected by our cuts but not IHAD4 are predominantly events near the multiplicity boundary that pass our inclusive criteria.

Figure 259:
Comparison of our cut-based hadronic selection with the DELPHI
IHAD4 pilot record tag. The Venn-diagram-style bar chart shows the
overlap between the two selections. The high overlap (92% of IHAD4
events pass our selection) validates the cut-based approach against the
official DELPHI classification. The 3,891 events selected by our cuts
but not IHAD4 are predominantly events near the multiplicity boundary
that pass our inclusive criteria.

.83.2
Leptonic event selections

\subsubsubsection

Electron selection

Electron identification uses the EM calorimeter energy (FEMC) rather
than the KELID tag, which was found to not discriminate electrons from
muons (91.4% of muon tracks in dimuon MC have KELID = 2, the same as
electron tracks). The selection requires:

This yields 94 electron events from the exploration data sample
(0.09%). The low count reflects the limited EMF information coverage in
the short DST; the published DELPHI analysis used the full
reconstruction chain with dedicated electron algorithms.

\subsubsubsection

Particle identification
diagnostics

The DELPHI short DST provides two particle identification tags relevant
to leptonic channel selection: KELID (electron likelihood) and KMUID
(muon chamber match quality). These diagnostics guided the choice of
identification strategy for each leptonic channel.

Electron identification tag (KELID) distribution for tracks in low-multiplicity events. The tag does not discriminate electrons from muons: both peak at KELID = 2. This finding motivates the use of EM calorimeter-based electron identification (EMF \textgreater{} 5 GeV, EMF/p \textgreater{} 0.3) instead of the KELID tag.

> 0.3) instead of the KELID
tag.

Per-energy-point yields

The hadronic event yields per energy point from the exploration data (7

, demonstrating the
characteristic Z resonance shape with the cross-section peaking near

Hadronic event yields per energy point from the exploration sample (7 files). The resonance structure is clearly visible, with the peak at 91.25 GeV containing \textasciitilde18,000 events. The off-peak points at 88.5 GeV (30 events) and 92.0 GeV (1,620 events) provide the lever arm for constraining M\_Z and \textbackslashGamma\_Z. Error bars show Poisson statistical uncertainties.

and

Hadronic fraction (selected events / total events) as a function of centre-of-mass energy. The fraction peaks at \textasciitilde64\% at the Z pole (91.25 GeV) and drops to \textasciitilde7\% at 88.5 GeV and \textasciitilde20\% at 92 GeV. The energy dependence is consistent with the Z resonance enhancing hadronic events relative to backgrounds, which have a flatter energy dependence.

.83.4
BDT cross-check

A gradient-boosted decision tree (BDT) was trained on KK2f MC as a
cross-check of the cut-based hadronic selection. The BDT uses 7
kinematic features (
N_{\mathrm{ch}}
,
E_{\mathrm{vis}}/\sqrt{s}
,

E_{\mathrm{ch}}/\sqrt{s}
,
E_{\mathrm{EM}}/\sqrt{s}
,

E_{\mathrm{HAD}}/\sqrt{s}
,
E_{\mathrm{EM}}/E_{\mathrm{vis}}
,

N_{\mathrm{ch}}/N_{\mathrm{VECP}}
) with 100 estimators, max depth 3, and
learning rate 0.1.

The key results are:

•

AUC = 1.0000
: Perfect separation on the MC test set.

•

Feature importance
:
N_{\mathrm{ch}}
dominates at 97.4%,
with
E_{\mathrm{vis}}/\sqrt{s}
contributing 2.6% and all other
features < 0.1%.

•

Overlap with cuts
: At threshold 0.5, the BDT selects 32,750
data events with 99.2% overlap with the cut-based selection.

The BDT effectively learns the
N_{\mathrm{ch}}\geq 5
cut boundary. It
does not improve upon the cut-based selection because the hadronic
classification problem is inherently simple: charged multiplicity
provides near-perfect discrimination. The cut-based selection is adopted
as primary.

ROC curve for the BDT hadronic classifier. The AUC of 1.0000 indicates perfect separation between hadronic signal and non-hadronic background on the MC test set. This confirms that the classification problem is trivially solved and a BDT offers no advantage over simple multiplicity cuts.

Figure 265:
ROC curve for the BDT hadronic classifier. The AUC of 1.0000
indicates perfect separation between hadronic signal and non-hadronic
background on the MC test set. This confirms that the classification
problem is trivially solved and a BDT offers no advantage over simple
multiplicity cuts.

BDT score distribution for signal (hadronic, blue) and background (non-hadronic, orange) events. The complete separation at all thresholds confirms the AUC = 1.0 finding. Both distributions peak at the extremes (0 and 1) with no overlap.

Figure 266:
BDT score distribution for signal (hadronic, blue) and
background (non-hadronic, orange) events. The complete separation at all
thresholds confirms the AUC = 1.0 finding. Both distributions peak at
the extremes (0 and 1) with no overlap.

Feature importance ranking for the BDT classifier. Charged multiplicity (N\_\textbackslashmathrm\lx@text@lbracech\lx@text@rbrace) accounts for 97.4\% of the total importance, confirming it is the single dominant discriminator. The visible energy fraction contributes 2.6\%, and all other features are negligible. This validates the cut-based approach using N\_\textbackslashmathrm\lx@text@lbracech\lx@text@rbrace \textbackslashgeq 5 as the primary selection criterion.

Figure 267:
Feature importance ranking for the BDT classifier. Charged
multiplicity (
N_{\mathrm{ch}}
) accounts for 97.4% of the total
importance, confirming it is the single dominant discriminator. The
visible energy fraction contributes 2.6%, and all other features are
negligible. This validates the cut-based approach using

N_{\mathrm{ch}}\geq 5
as the primary selection
criterion.

.84
Corrections and Cross-Section
Measurement

.84.1
Cross-section formula

At each energy point
i
, the hadronic cross-section is measured as:

{\sigma_{\mathrm{had}}(\sqrt{s_{i}})=\frac{N_{\mathrm{had}}^{i}-N_{\mathrm{bkg}}^{i}}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{had}}^{i}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{i}}}

(58)

where
N_{\mathrm{had}}^{i}
is the number of selected hadronic events,

N_{\mathrm{bkg}}^{i}
is the estimated background (0.3% contamination
from tau, two-photon, and beam-gas events, following
(
[
DELPHI:2000
]
)),

\epsilon_{\mathrm{had}}^{i}
is the selection efficiency (99.66% at all
energies), and
\mathcal{L}_{i}
is the published integrated luminosity.

Similarly, the leptonic cross-section for channel
f
is:

{\sigma_{f}(\sqrt{s_{i}})=\frac{N_{f}^{i}}{\epsilon_{f}^{i}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{i}}}

(59)

The ratio
R_{\ell}
is obtained as a luminosity-independent quantity
from the lineshape fit, using the relation

\sigma_{\ell}^{0}=\sigma_{\mathrm{had}}^{0}/R_{\ell}

(
[
ALEPH:2005ab
]
).

.84.2
ISR convolution

The ISR-convoluted cross-section (Equation
54
) uses the

\mathcal{O}(\alpha)
Bonneau-Martin / Kuraev-Fadin radiator
(
[
BonnMartin:1971
]
;

[
Kuraev:1985
]
) with

\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})
additive corrections
(
[
Nicrosini:1987
]
):

{H(x,s)=\beta x^{\beta-1}\left(1+\frac{3}{4}\beta\right)+\delta_{\mathrm{hard}}(x,s)}

(60)

where
\beta=\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}\left(\ln\frac{s}{m_{e}^{2}}-1\right)

resums the leading soft-photon logarithms to all orders, and

\delta_{\mathrm{hard}}
contains the hard-photon corrections. The
numerical integration uses 50-point Gauss-Legendre quadrature with a
change of variables
t=x^{\beta}
to flatten the
x^{\beta-1}

singularity near
x=0
.

The ISR implementation was validated by comparing the peak
cross-section:

\sigma_{\mathrm{had}}^{\mathrm{obs}}(91.2\text{ GeV})=31.6
nb, with a
ratio to the Born cross-section of 0.76, consistent with the expected
~25% ISR reduction at the Z pole. An initial
implementation using direct Gauss-Legendre quadrature on the
x
-space
(without the change of variables) failed catastrophically (ratio 0.04)
due to inadequate sampling of the integrable singularity, confirming the
necessity of the
t=x^{\beta}
transformation.

.84.3
Breit-Wigner
parametrization

The running-width (s-dependent) Breit-Wigner follows the LEP EWWG
convention (
[
ALEPH:2005ab
]
) (Eq. 1.8). The
\gamma/Z
interference and pure
photon exchange terms are fixed to SM values using

\sin^{2}\theta_{W}^{\mathrm{eff}}=0.23153
from the PDG electroweak
review (
[
PDG:2024
]
).

.84.4
Efficiency corrections

The hadronic selection efficiency
\epsilon_{\mathrm{had}}=99.66\%
is
applied uniformly across all energy points, as described in Section
.83.1
. The energy-independence of this efficiency is a
consequence of the
\sqrt{s}
-relative cut definitions and is
validated by the published DELPHI results.

For the combined fit including the muon channel, the muon selection
efficiency
\epsilon_{\mu}=68.0\pm 3.0\%
is used, derived from 250
dimuon MC events. The large statistical uncertainty on this efficiency
is a limitation of the available MC sample size and dominates the muon
channel sensitivity analysis.

.84.5
Background subtraction

Background contamination in the hadronic channel is estimated at 0.3%
following the published DELPHI analysis
(
[
DELPHI:2000
]
). The
contamination arises from
\tau^{+}\tau^{-}
events with
\geq 5

charged tracks (0.07% from MC), two-photon hadronic events, and
beam-gas interactions. The background is subtracted by scaling the
expected event count:

N_{\mathrm{bkg}}^{i}=f_{\mathrm{bkg}}\cdot N_{\mathrm{had}}^{i}
, with

f_{\mathrm{bkg}}=0.003
.

.85
Systematic Uncertainties

This section documents the systematic uncertainty evaluation for the Z
lineshape fit parameters. The dominant sources (beam energy, luminosity,
background, selection efficiency) are evaluated by varying the input
parameter, re-running the full chi-squared fit, and taking the symmetric
half-difference of the up/down shifts. Three subdominant sources (EW
corrections, ISR modelling, energy spread) are flat-borrowed with
documented justification; see the individual subsections for the three
required conditions (subdominant, propagation infeasible, cited
measurement).

.85.1
Summary

The systematic uncertainties on the three hadronic fit parameters are
summarised in Table
191
.

different systematics (Table

). The total is
computed as the quadrature sum of individual sources. Three systematics
are flat-borrowed (documented in

.85.2
Beam energy calibration

The LEP beam energy is calibrated using resonant depolarization and
spectrometer measurements by the LEP Energy Working Group
(
[
ALEPH:2005ab
]
). The centre-of-mass energy for each fill is an
external input to the analysis with an uncertainty of approximately ±2
MeV per point.

The beam energy uncertainty has two components
(
[
ALEPH:2005ab
]
):

1.

Correlated component
: shifts all energies coherently,
affecting primarily
M_{Z}
. Evaluated by shifting all points by ±2
MeV:
\delta M_{Z}=2.00
MeV, with negligible impact on
\Gamma_{Z}
(0.002 MeV)
and
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}
(< 0.001 nb).

2.

Point-to-point uncorrelated component
: different shifts at
different energy points, affecting primarily
\Gamma_{Z}
. Evaluated
by generating 50 toy datasets with random uncorrelated Gaussian shifts
(±2 MeV per point), propagating each through the full fit, and taking
the RMS of the fitted parameter shifts. This yields

\delta\Gamma_{Z}=1.02
MeV, consistent with the published
DELPHI/ALEPH value of ~1.2 MeV.

The correlated component dominates the
M_{Z}
systematic (2.0 MeV,
compared to the published DELPHI value of 1.7 MeV). The point-to-point
component is the dominant systematic for
\Gamma_{Z}
(1.02 MeV,
consistent with published analyses). The sum in quadrature gives a total
beam energy systematic of
\delta M_{Z}=2.10
MeV and

\delta\Gamma_{Z}=1.02
MeV.

Per-energy-point impact of the correlated beam energy systematic on the hadronic cross-section. A coherent \textpm2 MeV shift of all energy points produces the largest effect on the steeply-falling wings of the Z resonance, with opposite signs below and above the peak. At the peak, the cross-section is insensitive to energy shifts due to the flat maximum.

Figure 269:
Per-energy-point impact of the correlated beam energy
systematic on the hadronic cross-section. A coherent ±2 MeV shift of all
energy points produces the largest effect on the steeply-falling wings
of the Z resonance, with opposite signs below and above the peak. At the
peak, the cross-section is insensitive to energy shifts due to the flat
maximum.

Per-energy-point impact of the point-to-point beam energy systematic. Random uncorrelated energy shifts produce non-coherent cross-section variations at each energy point. The RMS of 50 toy realizations gives the uncertainty. This component dominates the \textbackslashGamma\_Z systematic because uncorrelated energy shifts effectively broaden or narrow the observed lineshape.

Figure 270:
Per-energy-point impact of the point-to-point beam energy
systematic. Random uncorrelated energy shifts produce non-coherent
cross-section variations at each energy point. The RMS of 50 toy
realizations gives the uncertainty. This component dominates the

\Gamma_{Z}
systematic because uncorrelated energy shifts effectively
broaden or narrow the observed lineshape.

.85.3
Luminosity

The luminosity systematic reflects the published DELPHI luminosity
measurement uncertainties: 0.70% for 1991 (SAT detector), 0.29% for
1993 (SAT), and 0.11% for 1994–1995 (STIC)
(
[
DELPHI:2000
]
,

[
DELPHI:1994
]
). In addition, a 0.054% theory
systematic from the BHLUMI Bhabha generator calculation
(
[
Jadach:BHLUMI
]
) is correlated across all years.

The systematic is evaluated by shifting all luminosities coherently
within their year-dependent uncertainties. The impact is

\delta M_{Z}=0.01
MeV (negligible),
\delta\Gamma_{Z}=0.36
MeV,
and
\delta\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}=0.056
nb. The luminosity
uncertainty is the dominant systematic for
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}

because the peak cross-section scales inversely with luminosity. The
different luminosity systematics for different years (SAT vs STIC)
partially decorrelate across the scan.

Per-energy-point impact of the luminosity systematic on the hadronic cross-section. The luminosity uncertainty shifts all points within a year coherently, with the magnitude determined by the year-specific luminosity precision (0.70\% for 1991, 0.29\% for 1993, 0.11\% for 1994--1995). The 1991 points show the largest shifts due to the larger SAT luminometer systematic.

Figure 271:
Per-energy-point impact of the luminosity systematic on the
hadronic cross-section. The luminosity uncertainty shifts all points
within a year coherently, with the magnitude determined by the
year-specific luminosity precision (0.70% for 1991, 0.29% for 1993,
0.11% for 1994–1995). The 1991 points show the largest shifts due to
the larger SAT luminometer systematic.

.85.4
Background contamination

The background contamination uncertainty is derived from the published
DELPHI background composition (
[
DELPHI:2000
]
) (Section 6). The total background of
~0.3% comprises:

•

Tau contamination: 0.07% (from MC), with ±30% relative uncertainty
from MC statistics (absolute uncertainty: ±0.02%)

•

Two-photon events: ~0.10%, with ±50% relative
uncertainty (absolute: ±0.05%)

•

Beam-gas and cosmic ray events: ~0.05%, with ±100%
relative uncertainty (absolute: ±0.05%)

The combined background uncertainty is

\sqrt{0.02^{2}+0.05^{2}+0.05^{2}}\approx 0.074\%
absolute,
corresponding to ±25% relative on the 0.3% total. This motivated
estimate replaces the arbitrary ±50% variation that would have been
unmotivated.

The systematic is evaluated by varying the background fraction by ±25%
and re-running the fit. The impact is
\delta M_{Z}=0.01
MeV
(negligible),
\delta\Gamma_{Z}=0.06
MeV, and

\delta\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}=0.032
nb. The background systematic is
a significant but not dominant source for
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}
,
behind luminosity (0.056 nb) and EW corrections (0.042 nb).

Per-energy-point impact of the background contamination systematic. The background affects all energy points approximately equally (proportional to the event count at each point), shifting the extracted cross-section downward when the background fraction is increased.

Figure 272:
Per-energy-point impact of the background contamination
systematic. The background affects all energy points approximately
equally (proportional to the event count at each point), shifting the
extracted cross-section downward when the background fraction is
increased.

.85.5
Electroweak corrections

The electroweak corrections affect the relationship between the measured
cross-sections and the extracted Z parameters through the
\gamma/Z

interference term and higher-order electroweak loops. The parametric
uncertainty arises from the input values of
m_{t}
,
M_{H}
, and

\alpha(M_{Z}^{2})
.

A flat 0.1% uncertainty is assigned to
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}
,
following the published DELPHI analysis
(
[
DELPHI:2000
]
) (Table
11) and the PDG electroweak review (
[
PDG:2024
]
). This is a flat-borrowed systematic: the ISR
convolution does not parameterize EW corrections explicitly (they are
absorbed into the Breit-Wigner parameters), making direct propagation
through the fit infeasible. The three conditions for flat-borrowing are
satisfied: (1) confirmed subdominant (0.042 nb vs total 0.078 nb); (2)
propagation is infeasible (EW corrections are not an explicit parameter
in the cross-section model); (3) the value is from a cited measurement
(DELPHI Table 11). This produces
\delta\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}=0.042

nb, with negligible impact on
M_{Z}
and
\Gamma_{Z}
.

.85.6
LEP beam energy spread

The LEP beam energy spread of approximately 50 MeV broadens the
effective centre-of-mass energy distribution at each fill, which
broadens the observed lineshape and biases the extracted
\Gamma_{Z}
if
not accounted for. Following (
[
ALEPH:2005ab
]
) (Section 5), the energy
spread is implemented as a Gaussian convolution of the theoretical
cross-section.

This is a flat-borrowed systematic: a full Gaussian convolution of the
theoretical cross-section with re-fitting would require per-fill beam
energy spread profiles, which are not available in the open data
archive. The three conditions are satisfied: (1) confirmed subdominant
(0.20 MeV vs total 1.10 MeV on
\Gamma_{Z}
); (2) propagation requires
per-fill data; (3) the value is from the LEP EWWG report (Section 5).
The systematic uncertainty of
\delta\Gamma_{Z}=0.20
MeV is
consistent with the published DELPHI and LEP combined analyses.

.85.7
Selection efficiency

The hadronic selection efficiency uncertainty arises from the MC
statistical precision:
\epsilon_{\mathrm{had}}=99.66\pm 0.024\%

from 57,480 KK2f MC events. The efficiency is varied by ±0.024% and the
fit is re-run.

The impact is
\delta M_{Z}\approx 0
(the mass is insensitive to
efficiency),
\delta\Gamma_{Z}=0.02
MeV, and

\delta\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}=0.010
nb. The selection efficiency is
a subdominant systematic because the hadronic selection is highly
efficient (99.7%) and the MC statistical precision is excellent (57,480
events yield 0.024% uncertainty).

The muon efficiency uncertainty (±3.0% from 250 dimuon MC events) has a
large impact on the combined fit:
\delta\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}=1.87

nb (29.5 times the statistical uncertainty). This is expected because a
3% shift in muon efficiency propagates through
R_{\ell}
to the
hadronic peak cross-section. In the actual measurement, the muon
efficiency would be calibrated from data with much better precision.

.85.8
ISR modelling

The ISR modelling systematic accounts for the uncertainty in the
radiator function calculation. Following the published DELPHI analysis
(
[
DELPHI:2000
]
) (Table
11), a 0.02% uncertainty is assigned to
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}
,
representing the effect of missing higher-order radiative corrections.

This is a flat-borrowed systematic: varying the ISR radiator function
order would require re-implementing the radiator with different
approximation levels, which is beyond the scope. The three conditions
are satisfied: (1) confirmed subdominant (0.008 nb vs total 0.078 nb);
(2) propagation requires alternative ISR implementations; (3) the value
is from a cited measurement (DELPHI Table 11). This produces

\delta\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}=0.008
nb, with zero impact on
M_{Z}

and
\Gamma_{Z}
because the ISR correction is approximately
energy-independent across the narrow Z scan range.

.85.9
QCD truncation (for
\alpha_{s}

only)

The QCD perturbative truncation uncertainty on the
\alpha_{s}

extraction is estimated from the difference between the N
3
LO and
N
4
LO results:
\delta\alpha_{s}\approx 0.001

(
[
Baikov:2012zn
]
,

[
Baikov:2008jh
]
). This represents the convergence of
the perturbative series and is standard for
\alpha_{s}
extractions
from
R_{\ell}
.

.85.10
Error budget narrative

The systematic uncertainty budget reveals distinct limiting factors for
each parameter:

•

M_{Z}
: Dominated by the correlated beam energy calibration
(2.0 MeV), with a subdominant contribution from the point-to-point
component (0.64 MeV). The total beam energy systematic of 2.10 MeV
accounts for > 99% of the systematic budget. This is an
external input from the LEP Energy Working Group and cannot be reduced
within this analysis.

•

\Gamma_{Z}
: The point-to-point beam energy uncertainty
dominates (1.02 MeV out of 1.10 MeV total), followed by the luminosity
(0.36 MeV) and energy spread (0.20 MeV). The
\Gamma_{Z}
systematic
(1.10 MeV) is consistent with the published DELPHI value
(~1.2 MeV beam energy systematic on
\Gamma_{Z}
). The
measurement remains statistically limited
(
\sigma_{\mathrm{stat}}=4.0
MeV vs
\sigma_{\mathrm{syst}}=1.10

MeV).

•

\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}
: Luminosity (0.056 nb), electroweak
corrections (0.042 nb), and background (0.032 nb) are the dominant
sources. The total systematic (0.078 nb) is comparable to the
statistical uncertainty (0.063 nb), making this measurement balanced
between statistical and systematic limitations.

The measurement is statistically limited for
M_{Z}

(
\sigma_{\mathrm{stat}}/\sigma_{\mathrm{syst}}=1.1
) and roughly balanced for
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}

(
\sigma_{\mathrm{stat}}/\sigma_{\mathrm{syst}}=0.81
). Concrete
improvements would require: (1) better luminosity measurements (reduces

\Gamma_{Z}
and
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}
systematics), (2) more MC
statistics for background estimation (reduces the background
systematic), and (3) data-driven efficiency calibration for the muon
channel (reduces the
R_{\ell}
systematic).

Resolving power for
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{had}}
: With a total
uncertainty of 0.100 nb (0.24%), the measurement can distinguish
predictions differing by ~0.20 nb at 2-sigma significance.
This corresponds to a ~0.5% sensitivity to the hadronic
peak cross-section.

compares the implemented

analyses (
[

]
;
[

]
),
and the LEP combination (
[

]
).
