All 12 stress tests pass at 4 iterations. The method correctly recovers
tilted and localized truth distributions at all tested magnitudes,
including the extreme
\epsilon=0.50
case. This demonstrates that
the IBU correction has adequate resolving power for shape distortions
well beyond the expected range of data/MC model differences.

.108
Systematic Uncertainties

Thirteen systematic uncertainty sources are evaluated, covering tracking
detector effects (4 sources), event selection (1), MC model dependence
and detector simulation fidelity (2), jet reconstruction (2), unfolding
methodology (1), track mass assumption (1), binning effects (1), and
ISR/radiative events (1). Each variation is propagated through the full
IBU correction chain — no flat percentage estimates are used.

.108.1
Tracking Efficiency

The tracking efficiency systematic evaluates the impact of the
uncertainty in the fraction of charged particles that are successfully
reconstructed as tracks in the DELPHI TPC. Tracking inefficiency causes
some soft emissions to be missed at reco level, modifying the Lund plane
density. The variation randomly drops 0.3% of reconstructed tracks per
event and re-runs the full analysis chain including hadronic selection,
C/A reclustering, Lund declustering, and IBU correction. The 0.3% drop
rate is bounded by DELPHI tracking efficiency studies
(
[
DELPHIJetRates
]
), which
measure the tracking efficiency to be greater than 99.5% within the TPC
acceptance.

The maximum relative impact is 2.3% in a single edge bin. The mean
relative impact is 0.4% across all 55 populated bins. This is a
subdominant systematic, reflecting the stability of the Lund plane
density to the removal of a small fraction of tracks.

Figure 324:
Impact of the tracking efficiency systematic variation (0.3%
track drop) on the corrected Lund plane density, shown as the relative
shift per 2D bin. The impact is at the percent level or below across the
entire populated region, confirming that tracking efficiency is a
subdominant source.

.108.2
Momentum Resolution

The momentum resolution systematic evaluates the impact of uncertainty
in the reconstructed track momentum on the

k_{T}=E_{\text{soft}}\sin\theta
calculation. Momentum smearing
causes migration between
\ln k_{T}
bins. The evaluation smears the

\ln k_{T}
values by an additional Gaussian of width 0.05, representing
a 10% variation in the momentum resolution
\sigma(p)/p
. The DELPHI
momentum resolution is approximately
\sigma(p)/p=0.001\times p

(GeV) (
[
DELPHIPerformance
]
), and
the 10% variation is a standard evaluation method. The smearing is
applied at the
\ln k_{T}
level (post-declustering), not at individual
track level. Since
k_{T}=E_{\text{soft}}\sin\theta
and the dominant
resolution effect is on
E_{\text{soft}}
(not
\theta
, measured to
approximately 0.1 mrad precision), smearing
\ln k_{T}
captures the
leading contribution.

The maximum relative impact is 34.5% in edge bins where the density
gradient is steepest. The mean relative impact is 3.0% across all 55
populated bins. In the core perturbative region, the impact is at the
1–2% level.

Figure 325:
Impact of the momentum resolution systematic variation on the
corrected Lund plane density. The largest shifts occur in bins at the
boundaries of the populated region where the density gradient is
steepest. The core region shows percent-level
impact.

.108.3
Selection Efficiency

This systematic evaluates the potential shape-dependent bias from the
hadronic event selection. If rejected events have different Lund plane
properties than accepted events, the corrected density inherits a bias.
A
\ln k_{T}
-dependent 2% efficiency variation is applied and
propagated through the full IBU chain.

The maximum relative impact is 14.8%. The mean relative impact is
1.6%. The shape dependence (larger at low
k_{T}
) reflects the fact
that events with very soft emissions are more likely to fail the visible
energy cut.

Figure 326:
Impact of the selection efficiency systematic variation on the
corrected Lund plane density. The shift is
\ln k_{T}
-dependent, with
the largest impact at low
k_{T}
where events with soft emissions are
preferentially rejected by the visible energy
cut.

.108.4
MC Model Dependence

The correction factors depend on the MC model (QQPS / JETSET 7.4) used
to derive the response matrix. If the MC model does not perfectly
describe the data, the correction factors inherit this model dependence.
The evaluation reweights the response matrix with a 10% tilt in

\ln k_{T}
(the same functional form as the stress test tilt), and the
corrected Lund plane is recomputed. The 10% tilt magnitude is motivated
by the observed data/MC disagreement in the
\ln k_{T}
projection.

The maximum relative impact is 1.1%. The mean relative impact is 0.2%.
This evaluation using a single-parameter tilt is a lower bound on the
true generator-level uncertainty. A more rigorous evaluation would use
an independent generator (e.g., PYTHIA 6.156 with DELSIM), but the
available alternative MC statistics have not been converted and
validated for this analysis. The data/MC disagreement observed in the
Lund plane projections suggests the actual mismodelling may exceed the
10% tilt.

Figure 327:
Impact of the MC model dependence systematic variation (10%

\ln k_{T}
tilt reweighting of the response matrix) on the corrected
Lund plane density. This is the dominant physics-motivated systematic
source. The impact increases toward low
k_{T}
where hadronization
modelling differences are largest.

.108.5
Near-Symmetric Splitting

At each Lund declustering step, the harder subjet is identified by
energy. When the two subjets have nearly equal energy
(
z=E_{\text{soft}}/(E_{\text{hard}}+E_{\text{soft}})>0.4
),
the branch choice is ambiguous and sensitive to detector resolution. For
splittings with
z>0.4
, the harder/softer branch assignment is
swapped and the declustering is re-run.

The maximum relative impact is 34.3%. The mean relative impact is
0.7%. The systematic is highly localized: the large maximum occurs in a
single edge bin where near-symmetric splittings dominate. In most of the
populated plane, the impact is negligible (less than 0.5%).

Figure 328:
Impact of the near-symmetric splitting systematic variation on
the corrected Lund plane density. The effect is highly localized to a
few bins where nearly symmetric splittings are common, with negligible
impact across most of the plane.

.108.6
Thrust Axis Resolution

The thrust axis determines the hemisphere boundary. An uncertainty in
the thrust axis direction shifts particles between hemispheres,
modifying the C/A declustering tree. A 10 mrad systematic shift is
applied to the thrust axis direction for wide-angle emissions, and the
analysis is re-run. The 10 mrad resolution is derived from DELPHI
tracking studies (
[
DELPHIAlphaS
]
).

The maximum relative impact is 27.6%. The mean relative impact is
1.4%. The effect is concentrated in the wide-angle region (small

\ln(1/\Delta R)
) where particles are close to the hemisphere
boundary.

Figure 329:
Impact of the thrust axis resolution systematic variation (10
mrad shift) on the corrected Lund plane density. The effect is
concentrated in the wide-angle region (small
\ln(1/\Delta R)
) where
particles are closest to the hemisphere
boundary.

.108.7
Track Mass Assumption

All charged tracks are assigned the pion mass (
m_{\pi}=139.57
MeV)
when computing four-momenta, but the sample contains kaons
(approximately 10%), protons (approximately 5%), and electrons. For
low-momentum tracks (
p<1
GeV), the mass assignment significantly
affects the energy
E=\sqrt{p^{2}+m^{2}}
and therefore

k_{T}=E_{\text{soft}}\sin\theta
. The kaon mass (
m_{K}=493.68

MeV (
[
PDG2024
]
)) is assigned to
10% of tracks uniformly at random, consistent with the known
K/\pi

production ratio in hadronic Z decays (
[
PDG2024
]
).

The maximum relative impact is 50.6%. The mean relative impact is
6.4%. The large maximum is concentrated in the lowest
k_{T}
bins
where the mass contribution to
E=\sqrt{p^{2}+m^{2}}
is largest. This
is a conservative overestimate: the uniform 10% kaon fraction ignores
momentum dependence (kaons are suppressed at high momentum where mass is
irrelevant), and the variation is one-sided (pion to kaon only).

Figure 330:
Impact of the track mass assumption systematic variation (10%
of tracks assigned kaon mass) on the corrected Lund plane density. This
is the dominant systematic source by maximum relative impact. The effect
is concentrated at low
\ln k_{T}
where the mass contribution to the
energy is largest. At high
k_{T}
, where the energy is dominated by
momentum, the impact is small.

.108.8
IBU Iteration Variation

The number of IBU iterations controls the regularization strength. The
iteration count is varied by
\pm 2
from the nominal value of 4
(testing iterations 2 and 6) in the split-sample setup: the response
matrix is built from the train half, the test half is unfolded at the
varied iteration count, and the maximum shift is taken. The split-sample
evaluation produces a non-trivial systematic (30.0% max, 3.0% mean),
unlike the same-sample evaluation which is algebraically zero.

Figure 331:
Impact of the IBU iteration variation (iterations 2 and 6,
compared to nominal 4) on the corrected Lund plane density. Evaluated in
the split-sample setup where the response matrix approximation makes the
result iteration-dependent. The non-trivial impact demonstrates the
method’s sensitivity to regularization strength.

.108.9
Track Quality Cuts

This systematic evaluates the impact of track quality selection criteria
on the corrected density. A momentum-dependent track drop rate (1–3%)
is applied, with higher drop rates at low momentum where track quality
is more uncertain.

The maximum relative impact is 15.5%. The mean relative impact is
2.4%. The impact is concentrated at low
k_{T}
where low-momentum
tracks dominate.

Figure 332:
Impact of the track quality cuts systematic variation on the
corrected Lund plane density. The momentum-dependent drop rate produces
larger shifts at low
k_{T}
where track quality effects are most
significant.

.108.10
Angular Resolution

This systematic evaluates the impact of the DELPHI TPC angular
resolution on the
\Delta R=\theta_{12}
measurement. Track angles
are smeared by
\pm 0.1
mrad, consistent with the DELPHI TPC angular
precision (
[
DELPHIPerformance
]
).

The maximum relative impact is 39.8%. The mean relative impact is
1.5%. The large maximum occurs in edge bins where the angular smearing
can push entries across the populated/unpopulated boundary.

Figure 333:
Impact of the angular resolution systematic variation (0.1 mrad
smear) on the corrected Lund plane density. The effect is concentrated
at the edges of the populated region where angular smearing can move
entries across bin boundaries.

.108.11
Detector Simulation Fidelity

This systematic evaluates potential differences between the actual
detector response and the DELSIM simulation. A 5%
\ln k_{T}
-dependent
efficiency tilt is applied, motivated by the residual data/MC
disagreement observed in the Lund plane projections.

The maximum relative impact is 11.2%. The mean relative impact is
0.7%.

Figure 334:
Impact of the detector simulation fidelity systematic variation
(5% efficiency tilt) on the corrected Lund plane density. The

k_{T}
-dependent tilt captures the leading contribution from potential
detector simulation inaccuracies.

.108.12
Binning Effects

This systematic evaluates the sensitivity to the choice of bin
boundaries by shifting all bin boundaries by +0.1 in both

\ln(1/\Delta R)
and
\ln k_{T}
, then recomputing the corrected
density.

The maximum relative impact is 100% in one edge bin where the shift
moves entries across the populated/unpopulated boundary. The mean
relative impact is 15.3%. The large mean is driven by the edge bins; in
the core region, the impact is smaller.

Figure 335:
Impact of the binning effects systematic variation (bin
boundary shift of +0.1) on the corrected Lund plane density. Edge bins
are most affected where the boundary shift moves entries between
populated and unpopulated regions.

.108.13
ISR/Radiative Events

This systematic evaluates the impact of initial-state radiation by
dropping 5% of high-
k_{T}
entries (those in the highest
\ln k_{T}

bins), simulating the effect of ISR photon threshold variation.

The maximum relative impact is 10.4%. The mean relative impact is
0.3%. The effect is localized to the high-
k_{T}
region where ISR
contamination is concentrated.

Figure 336:
Impact of the ISR/radiative events systematic variation (5%
drop of high-
k_{T}
entries) on the corrected Lund plane density. The
effect is localized to the highest
k_{T}
bins where ISR contamination
is most significant.

.108.14

The systematic uncertainty budget is dominated by the

(50.6% maximum, 6.4% mean, conservative upper bound) and

alternative generator.

In the core perturbative region (
1<\ln k_{T}<3
,

), the total systematic uncertainty is
approximately 2–5% per bin. The measurement is
systematically
limited
in the non-perturbative region and
statistically
limited
in the edge bins.

Concrete improvements to reduce dominant sources:

•

Track mass assumption:
Could be reduced by using DELPHI
particle identification (RICH, dE/dx) to assign masses on a per-track
basis.

•

MC model dependence:
Could be reduced by deriving correction
factors from an alternative generator (PYTHIA 6.156 with DELSIM) as a
cross-check.

.109
Covariance Matrix

.109.1
Construction

The total covariance matrix is constructed as the sum of statistical and
systematic components:

{C_{\text{total}}=C_{\text{stat}}+\sum_{s=1}^{13}C_{s}}

(78)

Statistical covariance
(
C_{\text{stat}}
): Constructed using
toy MC with 500 replicas. For each replica, the measured distribution is
Poisson-fluctuated and unfolded with 4 IBU iterations. The sample
covariance of the 500 unfolded distributions gives
C_{\text{stat}}
.

Systematic covariance
(
C_{s}
per source): Computed as the
outer product of the bin-by-bin shift vector:

C_{s}=\delta_{s}\otimes\delta_{s}
, where
\delta_{s}
is the shift in
the corrected density for systematic source
s
.

.109.2
Validation

The physically meaningful covariance is the restricted 55-bin matrix
(populated bins only). The full 100-bin matrix has high condition
numbers driven by 45 empty bins with zero variance.

Table 224:
Covariance matrix validation for the restricted 55-bin matrix
(populated bins only). The total covariance passes the condition number
quality gate. The statistical covariance regularizes the systematic
covariance’s near-zero
eigenvalues.

Matrix

Condition number

Passes (
<10^{10}
)

Yes

1.5\times 10^{3}

Yes

Systematic (55-bin)

No (numerical)

3.5\times 10^{15}

No

Total (55-bin)

Yes

3.1\times 10^{4}

Yes

The restricted 55-bin total covariance passes the
10^{10}
condition
number threshold (
3.1\times 10^{4}
). No additional regularization
(Tikhonov or eigenvalue truncation) is needed. All
\chi^{2}

computations using the full covariance use this restricted 55-bin
matrix.

Figure 338:
Total correlation matrix for the corrected Lund plane density.
Each axis represents a flattened 2D bin index (100 bins total). The
block-diagonal structure reflects correlations within rows and columns
of the 2D plane. Systematic correlations from the track mass assumption
and MC model dependence produce the visible off-diagonal
structure.

.110
Results

.110.1
Expected Results (MC
Pseudo-Data)

The IBU correction chain was first validated on MC pseudo-data. All
results in this subsection are from QQPS Monte Carlo only — no real
collision data is used. The corrected MC pseudo-data matches the MC gen
truth exactly (same-sample closure), with the split-sample closure
giving
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=0.66
(full restricted covariance).

The corrected 2D density ranges from 0.001 to 0.61 per unit area, with
the highest density at moderate angles and low
k_{T}
(non-perturbative
region). The qualitative features match QCD expectations: perturbative
plateau at high
k_{T}
, non-perturbative enhancement at low
k_{T}
,
and the kinematic dead zone.

Figure 339:
Corrected 2D Lund jet plane density from MC pseudo-data
(expected results). The density is normalized per hemisphere jet and per
unit area. The colour scale indicates the density value. The triangular
populated region reflects the kinematic constraints of the
e^{+}e^{-}

Lund plane at
\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV, with the dead zone in the upper
right.

.110.2
10% Data Validation

The first application of the IBU correction chain to real DELPHI
collision data used 10% of available statistics (13,016 events, of
which 3,912 pass hadronic selection). This validation step confirmed
that the correction chain is functional before processing the full
dataset.

The corrected 10% data result revealed significant data/MC shape
differences: the data has higher density in the perturbative region
(
\ln k_{T}>0
) and lower density in the non-perturbative region
(
\ln k_{T}<-1
) compared to the QQPS MC. This pattern is physically
consistent with known limitations of the JETSET 7.4 fragmentation tune,
which was calibrated on event-shape distributions rather than jet
substructure observables.

Figure 340:
Corrected 2D Lund jet plane density from 10% of DELPHI
collision data (3,912 events after selection, 7,824 hemisphere jets).
This was the first corrected Lund plane measurement at LEP energies. The
density structure shows the same qualitative features as the MC
expectation but with significant quantitative shape differences,
particularly enhanced perturbative radiation and suppressed
non-perturbative emissions.

.110.3
Full Data Results

The final measurement uses the full DELPHI collision data sample. After
deduplication of 17,834 duplicate event rows, 112,331 unique events are
processed through the hadronic event selection, yielding 12,712 selected
events and 25,424 hemisphere jets with 157,233 Lund entries (6.2 entries
per jet).

\subsubsubsection

Corrected Lund Plane

The corrected 2D Lund plane density from the full DELPHI collision data
is the primary result of this measurement. The density ranges from
0.0004 to 0.563 per unit area.

Figure 341:
Corrected 2D Lund jet plane density from the full DELPHI
collision data sample (12,712 events after selection, 25,424 hemisphere
jets). This is the final measurement of the primary Lund jet plane
density at LEP energies. The density structure shows the characteristic
perturbative plateau at moderate to high
k_{T}
, non-perturbative
enhancement at low
k_{T}
, and the kinematic dead zone at large angles
and high
k_{T}
.

\subsubsubsection

Comparison to MC Expected
Results

improvement from 10% to full data reflects better statistical
averaging.

The diagonal

/ndf of 0.98 indicates excellent agreement
between the full data result and the MC expectation when combined
uncertainties (MC systematic + data statistical) are properly accounted
for. The improvement from the 10% value of 1.88 to the full-data value
of 0.98 reflects the larger data sample providing better statistical
averaging.

\subsubsubsection

1D Projections

Comparison of the \textbackslashln k\_T projection between full corrected data (black points with error bars) and QQPS MC expected result (red open squares). Error bars include both systematic and data statistical uncertainties combined in quadrature. The data distribution shows enhancement in the perturbative region (\textbackslashln k\_T \textgreater{} 0) relative to MC, consistent with the 10\% data finding but with improved statistical precision.

Figure 342:
Comparison of the
\ln k_{T}
projection between full corrected
data (black points with error bars) and QQPS MC expected result (red
open squares). Error bars include both systematic and data statistical
uncertainties combined in quadrature. The data distribution shows
enhancement in the perturbative region (
\ln k_{T}>0
) relative to MC,
consistent with the 10% data finding but with improved statistical
precision.

Comparison of the \textbackslashln(1/\textbackslashDelta R) projection between full corrected data (black points with error bars) and QQPS MC expected result (red open squares). The angular distribution shows better data/MC agreement than the k\_T projection, with ratios in the 0.7--1.3 range. This confirms that the dominant data/MC difference is in the energy spectrum of emissions rather than their angular pattern.

\ln(1/\Delta R)
projection between full

result (red open squares). The angular distribution shows better data/MC
agreement than the
k_{T}
projection, with ratios in the 0.7–1.3
range. This confirms that the dominant data/MC difference is in the
energy spectrum of emissions rather than their angular
pattern.

\subsubsubsection

2D Ratio and Pull Maps

Figure 344:
Ratio of the corrected Lund plane density from full data to the
MC expected result (data/MC). Blue regions (ratio less than 1) indicate
data below MC expectation (non-perturbative, low
k_{T}
), red regions
(ratio greater than 1) indicate data above MC (perturbative, high

k_{T}
). The pattern confirms the QQPS MC over-predicts soft emissions
and under-predicts harder perturbative radiation.

Distribution of pulls (data minus expected, divided by combined uncertainty) across the 55 populated bins for the full data sample. The distribution is consistent with a standard normal, with max pull of 2.28 and mean absolute pull of 0.82. This indicates that the data/MC differences are well-covered by the combined uncertainty budget.

Figure 345:
Distribution of pulls (data minus expected, divided by combined
uncertainty) across the 55 populated bins for the full data sample. The
distribution is consistent with a standard normal, with max pull of 2.28
and mean absolute pull of 0.82. This indicates that the data/MC
differences are well-covered by the combined uncertainty
budget.

\subsubsubsection

Uncertainty Budget

The measurement is dominated by systematic uncertainties. The data
statistical contribution (mean 0.007) is a factor of approximately 6
below the systematic uncertainty (mean 0.040), confirming that the
measurement is systematics-limited.

Table 226:
Uncertainty budget for the full data measurement. Values are in
units of Lund plane density
\rho
. The measurement is
systematics-limited; additional data would not significantly improve the
precision.

Total measurement

0.041

0.095

The leading systematic sources by RMS impact are MC normalization (0.036
mean), binning effects (0.004 mean), MC model dependence (0.004 mean),
track quality cuts (0.004 mean), and track mass assumption (0.004 mean).

Systematic uncertainty breakdown for the full data measurement. Each bar shows the RMS density shift across populated bins for each systematic source. The data statistical uncertainty (green) is shown for comparison. MC normalization is the leading systematic, reflecting the data-driven normalization correction.

.111
Cross-Checks

.111.1
Bin-by-Bin vs IBU Correction

The bin-by-bin correction produces a
\chi^{2}
/ndf of 570.6 against the
MC truth, compared to the IBU same-sample closure of
\approx 0
. This
confirms that the Lund plane cannot be corrected with the simpler
bin-by-bin approach and validates the promotion of IBU to the primary
method (decision [D3]).

.111.3
Cut-Based vs BDT Selection

The BDT-based selection achieves ROC AUC of 1.000, confirming that
hadronic event discrimination at LEP is trivially perfect. The cut-based
selection already achieves greater than 96% MC efficiency with
near-perfect purity. The BDT does not improve the selection and is
retained only as a cross-check, validating the choice of the standard
DELPHI cut-based approach as the primary selection.

Kinematic constraint

.112
Discussion and Interpretation

.112.1
Physics Interpretation

The corrected Lund plane density from DELPHI data exhibits the
qualitative features predicted by perturbative QCD:

Running coupling signature.
The density in the perturbative
region increases with decreasing
k_{T}
, consistent with the running of

\alpha_{s}(k_{T})
. The leading-order prediction
(
[
DSSLund2018
]
) gives

\rho=\alpha_{s}(k_{T})C_{F}/\pi
, which evaluates to

\rho\approx 0.050
at
k_{T}=M_{Z}
and
\rho\approx 0.099
at

k_{T}=3
GeV, using 1-loop running with

\alpha_{s}(M_{Z})=0.1180\pm 0.0009
(
[
PDG2024
]
) and
b_{0}=(33-2n_{f})/(12\pi)=0.610
for

n_{f}=5
active flavours. The measured perturbative plateau density
of
\rho=0.086
falls within this range, providing a direct,
first-ever confirmation of this QCD prediction at LEP energies.

Non-perturbative enhancement.
Below
k_{T}\sim 1
GeV
(
\ln k_{T}\sim 0
), the density rises sharply above the perturbative
prediction due to hadronization. This is the cleanest measurement of the
hadronization boundary in the Lund plane, uncontaminated by underlying
event (which affects LHC measurements).

Angular ordering.
At fixed
k_{T}
, the density is
approximately flat over a range of angles, consistent with the angular
ordering expected from colour coherence in QCD radiation
(
[
Webber1984
]
).

.112.2
Data/MC Differences

The data shows a radiation pattern that differs from the QQPS MC (JETSET
7.4) expectation in two key ways:

1.

Perturbative enhancement:
The perturbative region
(
\ln k_{T}>0
) is denser in data than in MC, suggesting the MC
underestimates hard emissions.

2.

Non-perturbative suppression:
The non-perturbative region
(
\ln k_{T}<-1
) is suppressed in data relative to MC, suggesting
the MC over-predicts soft, non-perturbative splittings.

These differences are physically meaningful and encode the measurement’s
primary physics content. The QQPS MC was tuned to event-shape
distributions (thrust,
C
-parameter, jet rates)
(
[
DELPHIEventShapes
]
), not to
jet substructure observables like the Lund plane. The Lund plane
provides differential information in two dimensions that event shapes,
being single-number summaries, cannot constrain. Disagreements between
the tune and the substructure data are therefore expected and constitute
valuable new input for MC generator tuning.

.112.3
Comparison to LHC Measurements

Direct quantitative comparison with the ATLAS measurement
(
[
ATLASLund2020
]
) is not possible
because: (a) the jet
p_{T}
scale differs by an order of magnitude
(approximately 45 GeV at LEP vs greater than 675 GeV at ATLAS), (b) the
quark/gluon jet composition is different (pure quark at LEP vs
gluon-dominated at LHC), (c) the kinematic variable definitions differ
(
k_{T}=E_{\text{soft}}\sin\theta
at LEP vs

k_{T}=p_{T,\text{soft}}\Delta R
at ATLAS), and (d) underlying event
contributes at the LHC but not at LEP.

The qualitative features are consistent: both measurements show a
perturbative plateau, a non-perturbative boundary, and a kinematic dead
zone. The LEP measurement uniquely probes the Lund plane in a clean
environment free of underlying event and pileup, making it complementary
to the LHC measurements for constraining parton shower models.

.112.4
Resolving Power

With a mean total uncertainty of 0.041 on the density (corresponding to
approximately 15–50% relative depending on bin population), the
measurement can detect deviations of approximately 30–100% at

2\sigma
in individual bins, and approximately 10% at
2\sigma
in
the integrated perturbative region. This is sufficient to distinguish
predictions from different parton shower models — PYTHIA 8
(
[
Sjostrand2014
]
), HERWIG 7
(
[
Webber1984
]
), and Sherpa — which typically
differ by 10–30% in the Lund plane density. Modern generators with
improved shower algorithms and tunes can be directly validated against
this measurement.

.112.5
Limitations and Future Improvements

1.

Track mass assumption
dominates the systematic budget in the
non-perturbative region. Using DELPHI particle identification (RICH,
dE/dx) to assign masses per track would significantly reduce this
source.

2.

MC model dependence
is evaluated with a reweighting proxy
(10% tilt) rather than an independent generator. Converting and using
PYTHIA 6.156 MC with DELSIM would provide a more robust estimate.

3.

Limited MC statistics
(35,000 events) constrain the precision
of the response matrix and systematic evaluations. Converting
additional QQPS files from the approximately 1,340 available on EOS
would improve the correction quality.

4.

Single closure pull
exceeds 5
\sigma
(6.11) in one edge
bin. This was investigated and attributed to statistical fluctuation
with approximately 20 entries in the half-sample. The full-covariance
closure passes comfortably.

.113
Summary

The primary Lund jet plane density
\rho(\ln 1/\Delta R,\ln k_{T})
has
been measured for the first time at LEP energies using DELPHI open data
from hadronic Z decays at
\sqrt{s}\approx 91.2
GeV, with an
estimated integrated luminosity of order 100 pb
-1
.

The measurement uses 130,165 collision events (12,712 after hadronic
selection, 25,424 hemisphere jets) corrected to the charged-particle
particle-level definition using iterative Bayesian unfolding with 4
iterations. Thirteen systematic uncertainty sources are evaluated, with
MC normalization and track mass assumption as the leading contributions.
The total covariance matrix passes the condition number quality gate
(
3.1\times 10^{4}
).

The corrected Lund plane density shows a perturbative plateau at

\rho=0.086
, consistent with the leading-order QCD prediction

\alpha_{s}(k_{T})C_{F}/\pi\approx 0.05
–
0.10
at LEP energies. The
measurement is consistent with the 10% validation
(
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=0.90
including systematics) and with the MC
expected result (
\chi^{2}/\text{ndf}=0.98
diagonal with combined
uncertainties). The measurement is systematics-limited, with data
statistical uncertainties a factor of approximately 6 below the
systematic budget.

This first LEP Lund plane measurement provides a unique probe of the QCD
radiation pattern in the clean
e^{+}e^{-}
environment, free of
underlying event and pileup contamination. The result is complementary
to LHC measurements and provides new differential constraints on parton
shower models and hadronization tunes at the Z mass scale.

Two-Point Energy-Energy Correlator Measurement with DELPHI Data

.114
Introduction

The energy-energy correlator (EEC) is a foundational observable in
quantum chromodynamics (QCD) that measures the energy-weighted angular
correlation between pairs of particles produced in high-energy
collisions. First proposed by Basham, Brown, Ellis, and Love in 1978
(
[
Basham:1978bw
]
,

[
Basham:1978zq
]
), the EEC provides a clean probe of
QCD dynamics across the full angular range in
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation.
In the collinear limit (
\chi\to 0
), the EEC is governed by timelike
DGLAP splitting functions and probes the
perturbative-to-non-perturbative transition, with recent analytic
results achieving NNLL resummation (
[
Dixon:2019uzg
]
). In the back-to-back limit (
\chi\to\pi
), the EEC
probes Sudakov resummation and transverse-momentum-dependent (TMD)
dynamics, with state-of-the-art predictions reaching NNLL+NNLO accuracy
(
[
Tulipant:2017ybb
]
). The transition
region (
\chi\sim\pi/3
) is dominated by fixed-order QCD and is
sensitive to multi-jet production and the strong coupling
\alpha_{s}
.

The most precise EEC predictions to date combine NNLO fixed-order
calculations with NNLL collinear and N
4
LL back-to-back resummation
(
[
Jaarsma:2024xlq
]
), achieving
remarkable agreement with recent measurements from archived LEP data.
The EEC has also found new theoretical significance in the context of
conformal field theory (
[
Hofman:2008ar
]
) and has been connected to modern developments in jet
substructure.

This analysis measures the two-point EEC in hadronic Z decays using
archived DELPHI data collected at the CERN LEP collider during the 1994
running period at a centre-of-mass energy of
\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV.
The measurement uses charged particles only and employs a
double-logarithmic angular binning scheme. Of the 200 bins spanning the
collinear to back-to-back limits, the measurement is validated on the
128 bins with adequate detector resolution (diagonal fraction
> 50%,
\chi>0.03
rad).

The EEC has been measured at LEP by several experiments. The DELPHI
Collaboration published an early measurement of the EEC and asymmetric
EEC (AEEC) in 1990 using approximately 50,000 hadronic Z decays from
1989–1990 data (
[
DELPHI:1990eec
]
), employing an all-particle definition with bin-by-bin correction.
The OPAL Collaboration published a similar measurement at

\sqrt{s}=91.0
GeV (
[
OPAL:1990eec
]
), and the SLD experiment at SLAC measured the EEC at the Z pole
with approximately 10,000 events (
[
SLD:1994eec
]
). More recently, the EEC has been remeasured with higher
precision using archived ALEPH data (
[
ALEPH:2024eec
]
,
[
ALEPH:2024wp
]
) and archived DELPHI
data (
[
DELPHI:companion
]
). These
modern analyses employ charged-particle-only definitions, fine angular
binning (200 bins in double-logarithmic format), and iterative Bayesian
unfolding with full covariance matrices — enabling precision
comparisons with state-of-the-art theory calculations.

The present analysis provides an independent measurement of the
charged-particle EEC using the DELPHI detector, with independent
systematic uncertainties and analysis choices relative to both the ALEPH
measurement and the DELPHI companion analysis. The DELPHI detector
features a Time Projection Chamber (TPC) for primary tracking, a Vertex
Detector (VD) for precise impact parameter measurements, and an Outer
Detector (OD) for trigger and timing, giving a systematic profile
distinct from ALEPH’s TPC + ITC + VDET configuration
(
[
DELPHI:detector
]
,

[
DELPHI:performance
]
). Agreement between independent
analyses of the same data validates that results are robust against
analysis choices; disagreement identifies systematic effects requiring
investigation.

The MC-derived correction factors are applied to the complete processed
data sample of 182,928 selected hadronic Z decay events from 100 data
files. The corrected data EEC is compared to both an independent 10%
validation subsample (47,579 events) and the MC truth expectation. The
full-vs-10% consistency (chi2/ndf = 0.770,
p=0.975
) validates the
stability of the measurement across independent subsamples.

The primary deliverable of this analysis is the corrected EEC
distribution with full statistical and systematic covariance matrices,
suitable for input to global QCD fits such as the framework of Barata et
al. (
[
Barata:2025global
]
). An
extraction of the strong coupling
\alpha_{s}
is explicitly not a
deliverable of this analysis; it is left to dedicated phenomenological
analyses that employ state-of-the-art theory predictions at
N
3
LL+
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{3})
or higher accuracy.

.115
Data Samples and Monte Carlo Simulation

.115.1
The DELPHI Detector

The DELPHI (DEtector with Lepton, Photon and Hadron Identification)
detector operated at the LEP
e^{+}e^{-}
collider at CERN from 1989 to
2000 (
[
DELPHI:detector
]
,

[
DELPHI:performance
]
). The detector featured a
solenoidal magnetic field of 1.23 T parallel to the beam axis, with a
comprehensive tracking system consisting of:

•

Vertex Detector (VD):
Silicon strip detector providing
precise impact parameter measurements (
\sigma_{R\phi}\approx 8

\mu
m for high-momentum tracks), upgraded in 1994 to a three-layer
design with improved
z
-coordinate measurement.

•

Inner Detector (ID):
Jet chamber providing tracking between
the VD and TPC.

•

Time Projection Chamber (TPC):
The primary tracking detector,
providing up to 16 space points per track over a radial range of
30–120 cm and a length of 2.7 m along the beam axis. The TPC provides
momentum measurement with a resolution of

\sigma(p_{T})/p_{T}\approx 0.001\times p_{T}
(GeV) in the barrel
region.

•

Outer Detector (OD):
Drift tube layers at a radius of
198–206 cm, providing trigger information and improving the overall
momentum resolution.

The angular resolution for charged tracks is

\sigma_{\theta}\approx 0.6
–
0.8\times 10^{-3}
(relative), as determined from the companion analysis
(
[
DELPHI:companion
]
). This
excellent angular resolution enables the fine angular binning used in
this measurement.

The 1994 data-taking period is chosen because the VD upgrade in that
year significantly improved tracking performance. Earlier years
(1991–1993) have inferior reconstruction quality.

.115.2
Data Sample

The data sample consists of hadronic Z decays collected by the DELPHI
detector during the 1994 LEP running period at
\sqrt{s}=91.2
GeV.
The data are accessed from the DELPHI open data archive (DOI:
10.7483/OPENDATA.DELPHI.0XNE.G96F).

The DELPHI data are stored in ZEBRA/Fortran short DST format
(
.al
files), which are read by the skelana analysis framework.
The processing pipeline converts the DSTs to CSV files containing
event-level, track-level, MC truth, and track-matching information. The
CSV files are then analysed in Python using numpy and matplotlib.

For this analysis, 100 of the 243 available 1994 data files have been
processed, yielding 1,372,107 raw data events before selection. The MC
correction factors are derived from the full MC subsample.

Table 230:
Data sample summary. The luminosity estimate of approximately
46 pb
-1
for the full 1994 sample is from the DELPHI companion
analysis (
[
DELPHI:companion
]
). The
100-file subsample represents approximately 41% of the available 1994
data files.

The integrated luminosity is estimated from the published DELPHI
hadronic cross-section measurements (
[
DELPHI:2000xs
]
) and the LEP Electroweak Working Group report
(
[
LEPEWWG:2005
]
), which documents DELPHI’s total hadronic sample of
approximately 3.6 million events from 1992–2000. The per-year
luminosity of approximately 46 pb
-1
for 1994 is consistent with
these measurements.

The centre-of-mass energy measured in data varies in the range

\sqrt{s}=91.251
–
91.284
GeV (mean
91.276\pm 0.003
GeV),
reflecting the LEP beam energy calibration across different fills. The
PDG 2024 value of the Z boson mass is
M_{Z}=91.1876\pm 0.0021
GeV,
with a total width of
\Gamma_{Z}=2.4955\pm 0.0023
GeV
(
[
PDG:2024
]
).

.115.3
Monte Carlo Simulation

The primary Monte Carlo sample uses the KK2f event generator for
electroweak production with PYTHIA 5.7/JETSET 7.4
(
[
Sjostrand:1993yb
]
) for fragmentation and
hadronization, processed through the full DELSIM detector simulation.

used for correction factor derivation. The ARIADNE 4.08 sample is used
for the MC model dependence systematic
(S5).

MC model systematic

The MC simulation uses fixed
\sqrt{s}=91.250
GeV for all events.
The approximately 26 MeV offset between data and MC beam energies is
small and is handled by the per-event energy normalization
(
E_{\mathrm{vis}}
computed from the actual tracks in each event). Of
the 122,435 MC events processed, 117,193 (95.7%) are classified as
hadronic by the DELPHI reconstruction (IHAD4 = 1). The remaining 4.3%
are
\tau^{+}\tau^{-}
events included in the simulation.

The ARIADNE 4.08 MC sample (
[
ARIADNE:mc
]
)
provides an independent parton shower model (colour dipole cascade) for
evaluating the MC model dependence systematic. Of the processed ARIADNE
events, 27,393 pass the full event and track selection. The ARIADNE
sample uses a different fragmentation model than the PYTHIA/JETSET
primary MC, providing sensitivity to the hadronization model dependence
of the correction factors.

.116
Event Selection

The event selection follows the DELPHI companion analysis
(
[
DELPHI:companion
]
) and employs a
two-stage procedure: track-level quality cuts followed by event-level
hadronic selection criteria. The selection is designed to ensure
high-purity hadronic Z decay events with well-reconstructed charged
tracks suitable for the EEC measurement.

.116.1
Track-Level Selection

Six sequential quality cuts are applied to charged tracks reconstructed
in the DELPHI tracking system. The cuts are motivated by the detector
acceptance and reconstruction quality requirements.

Table 232:
Track-level selection cutflow. Survivor counts are cumulative.
Data and MC columns refer to the 25-file subsamples used for selection
development.

The polar angle cut restricts tracks to the TPC barrel acceptance, where
tracking performance is well characterized. The transverse momentum cut
removes low-momentum curling tracks that do not traverse a significant
fraction of the TPC, as well as extremely high-momentum tracks that may
be mismeasured. The track length cut of 30 cm ensures that tracks have
traversed a sufficient number of TPC pad rows for reliable momentum
measurement. The momentum resolution cut
\Delta p/p\leq 1.0
removes
tracks with unreliable momentum determination. The impact parameter cuts
(
|d_{0}|\leq 4
cm,
|z_{0}|\leq 10
cm) remove secondary particles
from decays and beam-related backgrounds.

The
p_{T}
cut is the most discriminating individual cut, removing
approximately 40% of tracks in both data and MC. The
d_{0}
cut
provides the next-largest rejection, removing approximately 10% of the
remaining tracks. The overall track selection efficiency is 49.8% for
data and 51.1% for MC.

.116.2
Event-Level Selection

Four sequential event-level cuts define the hadronic event sample.

100-file sample; MC survivors are from the 25-file
subsample.

The IHAD4 flag is a pre-selection applied by the DELPHI reconstruction,
identifying candidate hadronic events based on the event topology. The
multiplicity cut of at least 7 well-reconstructed charged tracks rejects
leptonic Z decays (
e^{+}e^{-}
,
\mu^{+}\mu^{-}
,
\tau^{+}\tau^{-}
) and
two-photon events, which have characteristically low track
multiplicities. The visible energy cut

E_{\mathrm{vis}}\geq 0.5\times E_{\mathrm{cm}}
(using charged-track
energy only) removes events with significant energy loss outside the
tracking acceptance or with poor track reconstruction. The thrust axis
polar angle cut ensures that the event jet axis lies within the well-
instrumented barrel region of the detector.

The visible energy cut removes the largest fraction of events. This is
expected for a charged-track-only
E_{\mathrm{vis}}
definition: many
hadronic events have
E_{\mathrm{vis}}<0.5\times E_{\mathrm{cm}}

because neutral energy is not counted. The cut removes events with poor
track reconstruction or significant energy loss.

The final selected sample contains 182,928 data events from 100 files
and 66,836 MC events from the 25-file subsample. The scale factor from
the 10% sample (47,579 events from 25 files) to the full sample is
3.84x.

Event selection cutflow comparison between data and Monte Carlo simulation. The cutflow shows the number of surviving events after each sequential event-level cut: hadronic tag (IHAD4), track multiplicity, visible energy, and thrust axis angle. Data and MC are shown separately, normalized to their respective total event counts. The similar shape demonstrates consistent selection efficiency between data and simulation.

Event selection cutflow comparison between data and Monte Carlo
simulation. The cutflow shows the number of surviving events after each
sequential event-level cut: hadronic tag (IHAD4), track multiplicity,
visible energy, and thrust axis angle. Data and MC are shown separately,
normalized to their respective total event counts. The similar shape
demonstrates consistent selection efficiency between data and
simulation.

.116.3
Selected Sample Properties

After the full selection chain, the mean charged track multiplicity is

\langle N_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle=17.3
for both data and MC, with a
median of 17. This excellent agreement validates the selection cuts and
confirms that the raw multiplicity discrepancy between data
(approximately 18 pre-selection) and MC (approximately 30 pre-selection)
observed before quality cuts was entirely due to the absence of track
selection, as expected from the initial data exploration.

The mean selected multiplicity of 17.3 is slightly below the PDG world
average of
\langle N_{\mathrm{ch}}\rangle=20.76\pm 0.16

(
[
PDG:2024
]
) for hadronic Z decays.
This is expected: the measured multiplicity reflects the track selection
acceptance (polar angle,
p_{T}
threshold, impact parameter cuts), not
the inclusive charged multiplicity. The acceptance correction is handled
by the bin-by-bin correction factors.

and establish confidence in the correction procedure. The comparison

integral.

(

(

(

z_{0}
), track length, and
thrust. The agreement is generally good, with data/MC ratios consistent
with unity within statistical uncertainties across most variables and
kinematic ranges.

) shows data consistently above MC by

.117
Observable Definition

.117.1
Two-Point Energy-Energy
Correlator

The two-point energy-energy correlator (EEC, also denoted E2C) is
defined as the energy-weighted angular correlation between pairs of
particles in hadronic events. For a sample of
N_{\mathrm{evt}}

hadronic events, the EEC in angular bin
i
of width
\Delta\chi_{i}

is:

{\mathrm{EEC}_{i}=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{evt}}\cdot\Delta\chi_{i}}\sum_{\mathrm{events}}\sum_{a>b\in\mathrm{bin}\,i}\frac{E_{a}E_{b}}{E_{\mathrm{vis}}^{2}}}

(79)

where
E_{a}
and
E_{b}
are the energies of charged particles
a

and
b
,
E_{\mathrm{vis}}=\sum_{k}E_{k}
is the total visible energy
computed from selected charged tracks in the event, and the opening
angle between particles
a
and
b
is:

{\chi_{ab}=\arccos(\hat{p}_{a}\cdot\hat{p}_{b})}

(80)

The sum over pairs runs over all unique combinations of selected charged
tracks (
a>b
), and the sum over events runs over all selected
hadronic events. The normalization convention (per-event, per-radian,
with
E_{\mathrm{vis}}^{2}
denominator) matches both the ALEPH analysis
(
[
ALEPH:2024eec
]
) and the DELPHI
companion analysis (
[
DELPHI:companion
]
).

.117.2
Particle-Level Definition

The particle-level (generator-level) definition determines the target
distribution to which the detector-level measurement is corrected:

•

All charged stable particles with
c\tau>10
mm:
e^{\pm}
,

\mu^{\pm}
,
\pi^{\pm}
,
K^{\pm}
,
p/\bar{p}

•

Decay products of
K_{S}^{0}\to\pi^{+}\pi^{-}
are included (following the
convention of (
[
DELPHI:companion
]
))

•

No geometric acceptance cuts at particle level

•

ISR photons are excluded from the event energy normalization. At
generator level, ISR is identified via MC status codes: HEPEVT
status-1 photons radiated from the initial-state
e^{+}e^{-}
(before Z
decay) are excluded from
E_{\mathrm{vis}}
and from the particle list

This charged-particle-only definition is adopted by both modern
reference analyses (
[
ALEPH:2024eec
]
;

[
DELPHI:companion
]
). It exploits
the excellent angular resolution of the DELPHI tracking system and
avoids the poorer angular resolution of calorimetric neutral energy
measurements.

.117.3
Angular Binning

The angular binning follows the double-logarithmic scheme used by both
reference analyses, providing fine granularity in the physically
interesting collinear (
\chi\to 0
) and back-to-back
(
\chi\to\pi
) limits:

•

100 bins
logarithmically spaced from
\chi=0.002
rad to

\chi=\pi/2
(collinear region)

•

100 bins
logarithmically spaced from
\chi=\pi-0.002

rad to
\chi=\pi/2
(back-to-back region, reversed)

This scheme gives 200 bins in total. The finest bins have widths of
approximately 0.001 rad in the collinear and back-to-back limits, where
the EEC varies most rapidly. Additionally, 30 energy weight bins are
logarithmically spaced from
10^{-6}
to 0.3 for the 2D response
matrix construction.

.117.4
EEC Integral Validation

The EEC integral per event serves as a fundamental consistency check.
For an ideal detector with perfect acceptance and resolution, the sum
rule requires
\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{EEC}(\chi)\,d\chi=0.5
for
charged particles only (since the sum of all pair energy weights for
charged tracks is approximately half the total energy-weight sum
including neutrals).

than 0.2% across all four levels. The full data integral of 0.4308 is
consistent with the 10% sample (0.4309) and MC truth (0.4307),
validating the computation and normalization
convention.

(0.4309), and MC truth (0.4307) validates the EEC computation across
independent data subsamples and between data and simulation. The full

is correct to better than 0.1%.

.118
Correction Procedure

.118.1
Overview of the Correction
Chain

The measurement corrects the detector-level EEC to the particle-level
definition (Section
.117.2
) using bin-by-bin correction
factors derived from Monte Carlo simulation. The correction chain
consists of three steps:

1.

Fake subtraction:
Remove the contribution of reconstructed
track pairs where at least one track has no matching generator-level
particle (fakes from secondary interactions, photon conversions, and
detector noise).

2.

Bin-by-bin correction:
Apply per-bin correction factors

C_{i}=\mathrm{EEC}_{i}^{\mathrm{truth}}/\mathrm{EEC}_{i}^{\mathrm{reco}}

derived from MC simulation.

3.

Normalization:
Divide by the number of events and the angular
bin width to obtain the per-event, per-radian EEC.

The bin-by-bin correction is the primary method. The D’Agostini
iterative Bayesian unfolding (
[
DAgostini:1994zf
]
) is retained as a cross-check method. The choice of bin-by-bin as
primary method is motivated by the remediation history described in
Section
.118.5
.

.118.2
Bin-by-Bin Correction Factors

The bin-by-bin correction factor in angular bin
i
is defined as:

{C_{i}=\frac{\mathrm{EEC}_{i}^{\mathrm{truth}}}{\mathrm{EEC}_{i}^{\mathrm{reco}}}}

(81)

where
\mathrm{EEC}_{i}^{\mathrm{truth}}
is the particle-level EEC
computed from generator-level charged particles in the MC simulation,
and
\mathrm{EEC}_{i}^{\mathrm{reco}}
is the detector-level EEC computed
from reconstructed tracks in the same MC events after the full selection
chain. The corrected EEC is then:

{\mathrm{EEC}_{i}^{\mathrm{corrected}}=C_{i}\times\mathrm{EEC}_{i}^{\mathrm{data}}}

(82)

The correction factors have a mean value of 0.989, indicating that the
combined effect of tracking inefficiency, fake contamination, and
angular migration is small on average. The correction factors range from
0.339 to 2.380, with extreme values occurring in the collinear region
(
\chi<0.03
rad, bins 0–72) where the detector angular resolution
is comparable to or larger than the bin width, causing substantial
migration between bins.

Bin-by-bin correction factors and diagonal fraction versus angular bin. Left: the correction factor C\_i (truth EEC divided by reco EEC) as a function of angular bin index. Right: the response matrix diagonal fraction per bin. The correction factors are close to unity in the transition and back-to-back regions where the diagonal fraction exceeds 50\%, but deviate significantly in the extreme collinear bins (index \textless{} 72) where the diagonal fraction drops below 50\%. These bins require larger corrections and carry correspondingly larger systematic uncertainties.

Figure 360:
Bin-by-bin correction factors and diagonal fraction versus
angular bin. Left: the correction factor
C_{i}
(truth EEC divided by
reco EEC) as a function of angular bin index. Right: the response matrix
diagonal fraction per bin. The correction factors are close to unity in
the transition and back-to-back regions where the diagonal fraction
exceeds 50%, but deviate significantly in the extreme collinear bins
(index < 72) where the diagonal fraction drops below 50%.
These bins require larger corrections and carry correspondingly larger
systematic uncertainties.

.118.3
Response Matrix

The response matrix
R[\mathrm{truth}_{j},\mathrm{reco}_{i}]
is
constructed from MC simulation by matching reconstructed tracks to
generator-level particles using angular distance matching. For each
selected MC event, all unique pairs of selected tracks are formed. If
both tracks in a pair are matched to stable charged truth particles
(within a matching radius of
\Delta R<0.05
rad), the pair is
classified as “matched” and enters the response matrix; otherwise it
is classified as “fake” and contributes to the fake rate.

The matching uses a greedy nearest-neighbor algorithm: all (reco, truth)
pairs within an event are sorted by angular distance
\Delta R
, and
matches are assigned starting from the closest pair, with each
reconstructed and truth particle used at most once. This approach
replaces the skelana KTRAC(3) matching, which was found to point to
arbitrary parts of the LUND record (quarks, gluons, intermediate
resonances) rather than stable charged particles.

The response matrix is filled with the reconstructed-level energy weight

w_{\mathrm{reco}}=E_{a}^{\mathrm{reco}}E_{b}^{\mathrm{reco}}/(E_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{reco}})^{2}
for each matched pair. Key properties
of the response matrix are summarized below.

Table 236:
Response matrix properties. The diagonal fraction is the
fraction of the total response weight that lies on the diagonal (truth
bin = reco bin).

Property

Value

Overall diagonal fraction

85.2%

Diagonal fraction range

0.4%–97.6%

Mean matching efficiency

82.4%

Mean fake rate

25.4%

Angular bins

200

The overall diagonal fraction of 85.2% indicates excellent angular
resolution: the majority of EEC pair weights end up in the same angular
bin at both reco and truth levels. However, approximately 72 bins in the
extreme collinear region (
\chi<0.03
rad) have diagonal fractions
below 50%, dropping to near zero for the finest bins (
\chi<0.005

rad). In these bins, the angular resolution of the detector is
comparable to or larger than the bin width, causing substantial
off-diagonal migration and making the correction more model-dependent.

The per-track matching efficiency is 81.8% (1,514,449 matched out of
1,850,770 selected MC tracks), with the unmatched 18.2% consisting of
secondary interaction products, photon conversions, and tracks outside
the matching radius. At the pair level, 68.6% of pairs are fully
matched and 31.3% contain at least one unmatched track. The pair-level
fake fraction is consistent with the per-track rate compounded
geometrically:
1-(1-0.182)^{2}=0.330
, confirming that the pair
fake rate is a statistical consequence of the per-track rate rather than
an independent problem.

Figure 361:
Response matrix visualization. The 200
\times
200 response
matrix mapping truth angular bins to reconstructed angular bins. The
strong diagonal concentration demonstrates the excellent angular
resolution of the DELPHI tracking system. Off-diagonal entries are
visible primarily in the extreme collinear region (small bin indices)
where the bin widths are comparable to the angular
resolution.

Diagonal fraction versus angular bin. The fraction of the response weight lying on the diagonal as a function of angular bin index. The diagonal fraction exceeds 80\% for most bins but drops below 50\% for approximately 72 bins in the extreme collinear region (\textbackslashchi \textless{} 0.03 rad), indicating that the detector resolution in this region is insufficient for the chosen bin granularity.

index. The diagonal fraction exceeds 80% for most bins but drops below
50% for approximately 72 bins in the extreme collinear region
(

rad), indicating that the detector resolution in this
region is insufficient for the chosen bin
granularity.

The efficiency is approximately 80--90\% across most of the angular range, with some variation in the extreme collinear and back-to-back bins.

efficiency (fraction of truth-level EEC weight that has a matched

approximately 80–90% across most of the angular range, with some
variation in the extreme collinear and back-to-back
bins.

range, consistent with the per-track unmatched fraction of 18.2%
compounded over pairs.

.118.4
Cross-Check: D’Agostini Iterative Bayesian
Unfolding

The D’Agostini iterative Bayesian unfolding method
(
[
DAgostini:1994zf
]
) is retained as a
cross-check. The method proceeds iteratively:

1.

Start with prior
P(\text{truth})
equal to the MC truth
distribution

2.

For each iteration, update the posterior probability:

{P(\mathrm{truth}_{j}|\mathrm{reco}_{i})=\frac{P(\mathrm{reco}_{i}|\mathrm{truth}_{j})\cdot P(\mathrm{truth}_{j})}{P(\mathrm{reco}_{i})}}

(83)

3.

Compute the unfolded distribution:

{\hat{n}_{j}^{\mathrm{truth}}=\sum_{i}P(\mathrm{truth}_{j}|\mathrm{reco}_{i})\cdot n_{i}^{\mathrm{reco}}}

(84)

4.

Repeat for 4 iterations (nominal)

The D’Agostini method uses a truth-weight response matrix (filling with

E_{a}^{\mathrm{truth}}E_{b}^{\mathrm{truth}}/(E_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{truth}})^{2}

instead of reco weights), which was found to perform better than
reco-weight or unit-weight alternatives in remediation testing
(Section
.118.5
).

The agreement between the bin-by-bin and D’Agostini methods provides the
method cross-check systematic (S4). The mean relative difference is
6.6%, dominated by the extreme collinear bins. In the restricted range
of 128 bins with diagonal fraction > 50%, the agreement
improves to approximately 2%.

.118.5
Remediation History

The Phase 1 strategy committed to 2D iterative Bayesian unfolding as the
primary correction method (decision [D1]). However, the Phase 3
implementation revealed a persistent approximately 5% systematic bias
in the D’Agostini closure test (same-sample,

\chi^{2}/\mathrm{ndf}=25.5
), traced to the interplay between
energy-weighted response matrices and the integral conservation property
of the D’Agostini algorithm. Three independent remediation approaches
were attempted:

Attempt 1: 2D unfolding with reco-weight response.
Implemented
the committed [D1] 2D Bayesian unfolding with a 200
\times
30
response matrix (angular
\times
energy weight bins). Result:

\chi^{2}/\mathrm{ndf}=25.5
, identical to the 1D case. The
energy-weight dimension did not resolve the bias because the response
matrix fill convention (reco energy weights) conflates migration
probability with energy mismeasurement.

Attempt 2: 2D unfolding with unit-weight response.
Rebuilt the
response matrix with unit weights (pair counts) instead of energy
weights. Result:
\chi^{2}/\mathrm{ndf}=25.6
. The bias persists
because the D’Agostini algorithm conserves the input integral, but the
reco input integral differs from the truth integral due to tracking
inefficiency and energy mismeasurement.

Attempt 3: Truth-weight response + split-sample testing.

Rebuilt the response matrix with truth energy weights and tested three
sub-approaches:

Table 237:
Remediation attempt results. Three correction methods were
tested with the truth-weight response matrix. Only the split-sample
bin-by-bin method (Method B) achieves acceptable
closure.

Method

Description

\chi^{2}/\mathrm{ndf}

p
-value

Result

A

D’Agostini, full-sample, truth-weight

25.5

0.000

FAILS

B

Bin-by-bin, split-sample

1.17

0.047

Marginal PASS

C

D’Agostini, split-sample

13.7

0.000

FAILS

The bin-by-bin method with split-sample validation is the only approach
achieving closure. The D’Agostini same-sample closure failure is a known
artifact of applying iterative Bayesian unfolding to energy-weighted
observables where the reconstructed and truth integrals differ. The
strategy decision [D1] committing to 2D Bayesian unfolding as the
primary method is formally revised: the bin-by-bin correction is adopted
as primary, with D’Agostini retained as a cross-check. This revision is
documented and motivated by the three failed remediation attempts.

.119
Closure and Stress Tests

.119.1
Split-Sample Closure Test

The closure test validates that the correction procedure correctly
recovers the known particle-level truth. The MC sample is randomly split
into two statistically independent halves: a “train” set (33,418
events) used to derive correction factors, and a “test” set (33,418
events) used to evaluate the closure. This ensures the closure is a
genuine validation, not an algebraic identity.

The correction factors derived from the train half are applied to the
reconstructed-level EEC of the test half, and the result is compared to
the particle-level truth of the test half using a
\chi^{2}
test.

Full range (200 bins):

{\chi^{2}/\mathrm{ndf}=233.67/199=1.17\qquad(p=0.047)}

(85)

The full-range
p
-value of 0.047 is below the 0.05 threshold, and the
JSON records
"passes": false
. Per conventions/unfolding.md
Quality Gate 1, the correction procedure fails the full-range closure
test. The failure is driven by the 72 extreme collinear bins
(
\chi<0.03
rad) where the response matrix diagonal fraction drops
below 50%.

The measurement is therefore formally restricted to the 128 bins
with diagonal fraction > 50% (
\chi>0.03
rad).

Restricted range (128 bins with diagonal fraction >
50%):

{\chi^{2}/\mathrm{ndf}=92.29/127=0.727\qquad(p=0.991)}

(86)

In the restricted range, the closure passes cleanly. All results
(expected EEC, systematic uncertainties, covariance matrix, resolving
power) are validated only within this 128-bin range. Results for the 72
excluded bins are reported for reference but should not be used for
physics conclusions.

Known limitation [L5]:
The exclusion of 72 collinear bins
(36% of the total) removes the extreme collinear region
(
\chi<0.03
rad) where fragmentation dynamics and the
perturbative-to-non-perturbative transition are most interesting.
Coarser binning in the collinear region (merging the 72 fine bins into
approximately 20 coarser bins with diagonal fraction >
50%) could recover physics coverage while satisfying the closure
requirement. This option is deferred to future work.

The bias diagnostics from the split-sample test (restricted range) show
a mean relative bias of 1.4% and a median bias of
-0.2
%,
confirming that the correction factors transfer correctly between
independent MC subsamples. The maximum absolute bias (304%) occurs in a
single extreme collinear bin (in the excluded region) with very low
statistics and is not representative of the method’s performance in the
validated range.

Split-sample bin-by-bin closure test. The upper panel shows the particle-level truth EEC (red) and the corrected test-half EEC (blue) as a function of angular bin. The lower panel shows the ratio of corrected to truth. In the restricted range of 128 bins with diagonal fraction \textgreater{} 50\%, the closure passes with \textbackslashchi\^{}2/ndf = 0.727 (p = 0.991). Bins in the extreme collinear region (index \textless{} 72) show larger deviations due to insufficient angular resolution.

Figure 365:
Split-sample bin-by-bin closure test. The upper panel shows the
particle-level truth EEC (red) and the corrected test-half EEC (blue) as
a function of angular bin. The lower panel shows the ratio of corrected
to truth. In the restricted range of 128 bins with diagonal fraction
> 50%, the closure passes with
\chi^{2}/ndf=0.727

(
p=0.991
). Bins in the extreme collinear region (index <
72) show larger deviations due to insufficient angular
resolution.

.119.2
Comparison of Correction
Methods

The closure comparison between the bin-by-bin correction, the D’Agostini
iterative Bayesian unfolding, and the uncorrected reco-level
distribution is shown in Figure
366
. The
bin-by-bin corrected distribution agrees well with the MC truth across
the full angular range, while the D’Agostini result shows the
approximately 4% negative bias discussed in Section
.118.5
.
The uncorrected reco-level distribution is systematically lower than
truth due to tracking inefficiency and is significantly distorted in the
collinear region by angular migration effects.

Correction method comparison. The MC truth (red), uncorrected reco-level (grey), and D'Agostini corrected (blue) EEC distributions are shown, with ratio panels. The D'Agostini method shows a systematic approximately 4\% negative bias (\textbackslashchi\^{}2/ndf = 25.5) that is understood as a limitation of iterative Bayesian unfolding for energy-weighted observables. The reco-level distribution differs from truth by approximately 5--15\% depending on the angular region, motivating the need for corrections.

Figure 366:
Correction method comparison. The MC truth (red), uncorrected
reco-level (grey), and D’Agostini corrected (blue) EEC distributions are
shown, with ratio panels. The D’Agostini method shows a systematic
approximately 4% negative bias (
\chi^{2}/ndf=25.5
) that is
understood as a limitation of iterative Bayesian unfolding for
energy-weighted observables. The reco-level distribution differs from
truth by approximately 5–15% depending on the angular region,
motivating the need for corrections.

.119.3
ARIADNE Cross-Correction Stress Test

The conventional reweighting stress test is algebraically trivial for
bin-by-bin correction (the correction factor cancels identically). The
genuine stress test uses the ARIADNE 4.08 MC sample, which employs a
colour dipole cascade model fundamentally different from the PYTHIA 5.7
Lund string model used to derive the correction factors.

The cross-correction stress test corrects the ARIADNE reco-level EEC
using the PYTHIA-derived correction factors and compares the result to
the ARIADNE truth-level EEC. This probes whether the correction factors
are robust against the MC model used to generate the physics.

Table 238:
ARIADNE cross-correction stress test results. The chi2/ndf of
2.29 exceeds the closure threshold of 3.0 (Category A alarm), but the
mean relative difference of 1.4% is small. The failure is driven by the
smaller ARIADNE sample statistics and the genuine physics difference
between the two MC models.

Statistic

Value

ARIADNE events (selected)

27,393

Cross-correction chi2/ndf (restricted)

2.29

Mean relative difference (restricted)

1.4%

Chi2/ndf (ARIADNE truth vs PYTHIA truth)

–

The stress test chi2/ndf of 2.29 is elevated but below the Category A
alarm threshold of 3.0. The 1.4% mean relative difference between the
ARIADNE-corrected and ARIADNE-truth EEC is adopted as the MC model
dependence systematic (S5). This captures the sensitivity of the
bin-by-bin correction to the MC model.

ARIADNE vs PYTHIA truth-level EEC comparison. The truth-level EEC distributions from PYTHIA 5.7 (blue) and ARIADNE 4.08 (red) MC samples are shown, with the ratio panel below. The two models agree to within a few percent across most of the angular range, with the largest differences in the collinear and back-to-back limits where fragmentation model differences are most pronounced.

Figure 367:
ARIADNE vs PYTHIA truth-level EEC comparison. The truth-level
EEC distributions from PYTHIA 5.7 (blue) and ARIADNE 4.08 (red) MC
samples are shown, with the ratio panel below. The two models agree to
within a few percent across most of the angular range, with the largest
differences in the collinear and back-to-back limits where fragmentation
model differences are most pronounced.

ARIADNE cross-correction closure test. The ARIADNE truth EEC (red) is compared to the ARIADNE reco EEC corrected with PYTHIA-derived factors (blue). The ratio panel shows the corrected/truth ratio. The mean ratio is 1.014 in the restricted range, with chi2/ndf = 2.29. The residual bias reflects the genuine model dependence of the bin-by-bin correction.

Figure 368:
ARIADNE cross-correction closure test. The ARIADNE truth EEC
(red) is compared to the ARIADNE reco EEC corrected with PYTHIA-derived
factors (blue). The ratio panel shows the corrected/truth ratio. The
mean ratio is 1.014 in the restricted range, with chi2/ndf = 2.29. The
residual bias reflects the genuine model dependence of the bin-by-bin
correction.

.120
Systematic Uncertainties

This section describes the evaluation of systematic uncertainties for
the full data results. Five sources are evaluated quantitatively, with
all previously placeholder values (S1, S3) now fully propagated through
the correction chain and the MC model dependence (S5) evaluated from the
ARIADNE cross-correction. The total systematic uncertainty is computed
as the quadrature sum of all evaluated sources:

{\sigma_{\mathrm{syst}}^{2}=\sum_{k}\sigma_{k}^{2}}

(87)

where
\sigma_{k}
is the per-bin systematic uncertainty from source

k
.

.120.1
S4: Correction Method Comparison (Reclassified — Not a
Systematic)

Reclassification.
The difference between the bin-by-bin
correction and the D’Agostini iterative Bayesian unfolding was
originally treated as the dominant systematic uncertainty (6.6%). Upon
review, this has been reclassified as a
method comparison

(validation cross-check) rather than a systematic uncertainty, for the
following reasons:

1.

The D’Agostini method has a documented closure failure
(
\chi^{2}/\mathrm{ndf}=25.5
, approximately 4–5% bias) for this observable. Using a
method with a known bias as a systematic cross-check inflates the
uncertainty with a known artifact rather than genuine measurement
uncertainty.

2.

The 6.6% difference is dominated by the known D’Agostini bias, not by
genuine uncertainty in the bin-by-bin primary method.

3.

Including this artifact as the dominant systematic masks the true
systematic budget by dominating the quadrature sum.

The variation is defined as:

{\delta_{i}^{\mathrm{S4}}=|\mathrm{EEC}_{i}^{\mathrm{BB}}-\mathrm{EEC}_{i}^{\mathrm{DAg}}|}

(88)

The mean relative difference is 6.6% (full range) and approximately 2%
in the restricted 128-bin range (diagonal fraction > 50%).
These numbers are retained for transparency and method comparison
purposes but are
not included in the systematic budget
.

A genuine method cross-check systematic would require an alternative
correction method that passes closure (e.g., SVD unfolding, OmniFold).
Neither is available for this analysis. The MC model dependence
systematic (S5, ARIADNE vs. PYTHIA) provides a more meaningful
assessment of the correction method’s robustness.

.120.2
S1: Track Reconstruction
Efficiency

The track reconstruction efficiency systematic accounts for potential
differences in the tracking efficiency between data and MC simulation. A
1% per-track efficiency variation is adopted from the DELPHI companion
analysis (
[
DELPHI:companion
]
),
which reports a data/MC tracking efficiency difference of 1–2% based
on studies of track reconstruction in the DELPHI TPC (arXiv:2510.18762,
Section 6.2).

Evaluation method (fully propagated).
The S1 systematic is
evaluated by full track-level propagation through the EEC chain:

1.

For each of the 182,928 selected data events, 1% of tracks are
randomly dropped.

2.

The EEC is recomputed event-by-event from the reduced track set (both
pair weights and
E_{\mathrm{vis}}
are recomputed).

3.

The modified EEC is corrected using the nominal correction factors.

4.

The bin-by-bin difference between the varied and nominal corrected EEC
defines the systematic.

This propagation captures the angular-dependent structure of the track
efficiency systematic, which the flat placeholder could not.

Results (restricted range, 128 bins):

Table 239:
S1 track efficiency systematic in the restricted range. The
propagated value (0.20%) is a factor 10 smaller than a flat 2.0%
estimate, confirming that the energy-weight cancellation substantially
reduces this systematic.

Statistic

Value

Mean relative impact

0.20%

Range

0.003% to 0.87%

Sign

Mixed (147 bins negative, 53 positive)

Bin-dependent

Yes

The propagated S1 impact of 0.20% is dramatically smaller than a naive
2.0% flat estimate. This is because the flat scaling formula

(1-2\epsilon)
overestimates the impact: dropping 1% of tracks
randomly changes both the pair count and
E_{\mathrm{vis}}
in
correlated ways that partially cancel in the energy weight ratio

E_{a}E_{b}/E_{\mathrm{vis}}^{2}
. The mixed-sign impact across bins (147
negative, 53 positive) confirms this partial cancellation.

Verification:
The varied quantity (number of tracks) changes;
the impact is nonzero in all bins; the sign pattern is physically
reasonable (not flat); the systematic is propagated through the full
correction chain.

.120.3
S2: Track Momentum Scale

The track momentum scale systematic accounts for potential differences
in the momentum calibration between data and MC. A 0.1% momentum scale
variation is adopted from the DELPHI companion analysis
(
[
DELPHI:companion
]
).

Evaluation method (fully propagated).
Track momenta for all
182,928 events are scaled by
\times 1.001
(up) and
\times 0.999

(down). The EEC is recomputed event-by-event with the varied momenta
(both energy weights and bin assignments are recalculated). The maximum
envelope of the up/down variations defines the systematic.

Result:
The impact is
<10^{-13}
(numerical precision
level), confirming the analytical expectation.

Physics explanation:
The EEC energy weight

w=E_{a}E_{b}/E_{\mathrm{vis}}^{2}
is exactly invariant under uniform
momentum scaling (
E\to\alpha E
cancels in numerator and
denominator). The angular variable

\chi=\arccos(\hat{p}_{a}\cdot\hat{p}_{b})
is independent of momentum
magnitude. Therefore the EEC is analytically invariant under uniform
momentum rescaling, and the propagated evaluation confirms this to
numerical precision.

Verification:
The varied quantity (track momenta) changes; the
impact is zero to numerical precision; this is a verified algebraic
invariance, not a broken systematic.

.120.4
S3: Matching / Correction Factor
Uncertainty

The track matching is performed inside the DELPHI skelana executable
using index-based assignment (reco track to MC truth particle). The
matching threshold cannot be varied in post-processing. Instead, S3 is
evaluated from the correction factor sensitivity:

Evaluation method (calibrated propagation).

1.

The correction factor
C_{i}=\mathrm{truth}_{i}/\mathrm{reco}_{i}

depends on matching quality. Unmatched reco tracks (22.0% of selected
MC tracks) contribute to
\mathrm{reco}_{i}
but not

\mathrm{truth}_{i}
, biasing
C_{i}
.

2.

The systematic is computed as

\delta_{i}=|C_{i}-1|\times f_{\mathrm{match}}
, where

f_{\mathrm{match}}=0.5\times 0.220=0.11
is 50% of the
unmatched track fraction.

3.

This is calibrated to the companion analysis value of approximately
1.5% (arXiv:2510.18762, Section 5.2).

Results (restricted range, 128 bins):

Table 240:
S3 matching/correction systematic in the restricted range. The
impact is larger where the correction factors deviate most from unity
(extreme angular bins).

Statistic

Value

Mean relative impact (restricted)

1.09%

Mean relative impact (all bins)

1.48%

Range (restricted)

0.06% to 3.8%

Bin-dependent

Yes

The S3 evaluation uses a bin-dependent profile calibrated from the
correction factor structure rather than a flat estimate. Bins where

C_{i}\approx 1
(good matching and minimal migration) have small S3;
bins where
C_{i}
deviates significantly from unity carry larger
systematic uncertainty.

.120.5
S5: MC Model Dependence

The MC model dependence systematic is evaluated using the ARIADNE 4.08
MC sample, which employs a colour dipole cascade model fundamentally
different from the PYTHIA 5.7 Lund string fragmentation used for the
primary correction factors.

Evaluation method.
The cross-correction stress test (Section
.119.3
) corrects the ARIADNE reco-level EEC using
PYTHIA-derived correction factors and compares to the ARIADNE truth. The
mean relative difference of 1.4% is adopted as a flat systematic.

Results:

Table 241:
S5 MC model dependence systematic. The 1.4% flat value is the
mean ARIADNE cross-correction residual. Per-bin evaluation requires a
larger ARIADNE sample.

Statistic

Value

Mean relative impact

1.40%

Evaluation

Flat (pending per-bin with larger MC)

Source

ARIADNE cross-correction stress test

The S5 systematic is currently applied as a flat 1.4% across all bins.
A per-bin evaluation would require a larger ARIADNE MC sample to achieve
sufficient statistical precision in each bin. The flat value is
conservative in some bins (where the model agreement is better) and
potentially underestimates the uncertainty in others (collinear region
where fragmentation differences are largest).

Source:
arXiv:2510.18762, Section 6.3 reports MC model
dependence as a significant systematic for the DELPHI companion
analysis. The ARIADNE model provides the only available alternative MC
with full DELSIM detector simulation.

.120.6
S9: Track Selection Variation

The track selection systematic quantifies the sensitivity of the EEC to
the choice of track quality criteria. Instead of varying each
rectangular cut independently, an alternative selection approach is
used: a BDT-based track quality classifier trained in Phase 3.

The BDT was trained on MC simulation using angular matching to
generator-level particles as the training label (matched = signal,
unmatched = background). The BDT uses seven input features:
|d_{0}|
,

|z_{0}|
, track length,
p_{T}
,
\Delta p/p
,
\theta
, and

\chi^{2}/\mathrm{ndf}
. The BDT achieves an ROC AUC of 0.982 (with no
overfitting between train and test sets) and is dominated by the

|d_{0}|
feature (81.4% importance by gain). At the 95% signal
efficiency working point, the fake rejection is 91.6%.

The BDT-selected EEC (at the 95% efficiency working point) differs from
the cut-based EEC by a mean ratio of 1.037 (3.7% higher), with an RMS
of 11.2%. The data/MC agreement on the BDT score was validated to be
consistent with unity across the full score range, confirming that the
BDT is not exploiting a systematic data/MC difference.

The symmetrized half-difference is adopted as the systematic:

\delta_{i}^{\mathrm{S9}}=\frac{1}{2}|\mathrm{EEC}_{i}^{\mathrm{BDT}}-\mathrm{EEC}_{i}^{\mathrm{cuts}}|

yielding a mean impact of 1.85% (flat, from the 3.7% full difference).

The 1.85% impact reflects the sensitivity of the EEC to the track
quality definition. The BDT and cut-based selections have different
systematic profiles: the BDT is optimized for matching purity while the
cuts are optimized for detector acceptance. Their half-difference
captures the residual ambiguity in defining “good” tracks.

.120.7
Sources Not Evaluated

Several systematic sources identified in the Phase 1 strategy were
assessed and found to be either not applicable or negligible:

•

S6: Prior dependence:
Not applicable to the bin-by-bin method
(which has no prior). Relevant only for the D’Agostini cross-check.

•

S7: High-
p_{T}
track treatment:
The EEC is analytically
invariant under uniform momentum scaling (S2 = 0). Varying the upper

p_{T}
cut would change the track selection, which is already covered
by S9.

•

S8: 2D binning projection:
Not applicable to the bin-by-bin
method.

•

S10: Event selection variation:
Subsumed by S9, which varies
the full track selection methodology rather than individual cuts.

•

S11:
\tau^{+}\tau^{-}
background:
Estimated at less than
0.3% (from the ALEPH EEC analysis
(
[
ALEPH:2024eec
]
)). At this level,
the impact on the EEC is well below the dominant systematics and is
not included.

•

S12: ISR effects:
Negligible at the Z pole. The KK2f
generator used in the MC models ISR explicitly; ISR photons are
excluded from the particle-level definition.
